# Product Requirements — radargen

> **How to read this document.**
> **§§3–7 are normative.** Every requirement carries an ID (`FR-n`, `NFR-n`) and is written to be directly testable. Requirement text states *what* the tool does, not why.
> **§9 is the decision log.** It holds the reasoning — including options considered and rejected — so settled arguments don't get re-litigated. Decisions carry `D-n` IDs and are referenced from the requirements they explain.
> If §6/§7 and §9 ever disagree, **the requirement wins** and the decision log needs updating.
> **IDs are permanent labels, not positions.** A requirement added in a later revision takes the next free number and sits with the requirements it belongs beside, so IDs within a table are not always ascending. Renumbering would invalidate every cross-reference in §9 and §12 for no gain.

## 1) Summary

**`radargen`** (D-32) is a single-binary tool that reads a CSV of technology-radar entries and renders an interactive HTML radar page, plus PNG, SVG and PDF exports of the same visual. Two things distinguish it from the industry-standard renderer: an **arbitrary number of sectors** rather than a hardcoded four quadrants, and a **configurable ring set** whose default is `--rings "Assess,Trial,Adopt,Retire"`. The flag is declared **edge-to-centre** (FR-12, D-60), the order a radar is read in, so that default is the lifecycle Assess → Trial → Adopt → Retire as written: Assess at the outer edge, Retire at the centre.

That default is the lifecycle an in-house radar actually tracks: a technology is evaluated, piloted, adopted as a standard, and eventually retired. It differs from the ThoughtWorks set (Adopt/Trial/Assess/**Hold**) because the two radars do different jobs. ThoughtWorks publishes advice to an industry it doesn't operate, so *Hold* is a caution about taking something up. An in-house radar governs an estate you actually run, where the harder question is what you're on the hook to remove — so *Retire*, a state with an owner and an exit date, earns the fourth ring more than *Hold* does. Either set works; `--rings` chooses (D-4).

It replaces the stock ThoughtWorks [build-your-own-radar](https://github.com/thoughtworks/build-your-own-radar) clone currently used as the interim renderer for an in-house technology radar.

A working layout prototype sits alongside this document: **`mockup.html`** in this folder. It is a validation artefact, not a reference implementation — see §10 for what it does and does not prove.

## 2) Problem / Motivation

A standards catalogue has a category (sector) count decided by what the organisation maintaining it actually does — not necessarily the four that ThoughtWorks defaults to (Tools / Platforms / Languages & Frameworks / Techniques). **The originating case for this tool is a three-category catalogue — Tool / Platform / Practice — at an IT outsourcer.** Stock BYOR hardcodes exactly four quadrants, so a three-category radar doesn't fit it without either padding to four with an unused category or forking the renderer.

Separately, a catalogue-derived CSV typically carries metadata the catalogue cares about — `domain`, `last_affirmed`, `exit_date`, survey notes — that BYOR silently ignores, because its schema only recognises `name, ring, quadrant, description`. That data already exists; nothing currently surfaces it.

A prior, more ambitious attempt (a Rust CLI + TOML catalogue, designed 2026-08-09) was deliberately rejected: it duplicated a full relational catalogue-and-decision-linking feature already specced in a separate tool (ArchMan, code **TSR**), where a radar entry → ADR reference becomes a real traversable relationship rather than an inert file path. This tool is scoped narrower on purpose — **a renderer, not a catalogue store** — so it doesn't reopen that objection. See §8 and D-1.

## 3) Goals

- **G-1** Render a technology radar from a CSV with **N sectors** (N ≥ 1), not a fixed four.
- **G-2** Make the ring set and its order configurable, defaulting to `Assess,Trial,Adopt,Retire` edge-to-centre — the lifecycle Assess → Trial → Adopt → Retire, read from the outer edge inward — so the existing radar renders with zero configuration.
- **G-3** Give **Adopt — Default** and **Adopt — Conditional** a distinct visual treatment *within* the Adopt ring. Conditional adoption is a qualifier on the ring, not a fifth ring: the item genuinely is adopted, and what varies is whether it's the default choice or one permitted under stated circumstances. Promoting that to its own ring would misrepresent it as a lesser lifecycle stage.
- **G-4** Surface the catalogue metadata already present in the CSV (domain, rationale, last-affirmed, exit commitment) instead of dropping it.
- **G-5** Tolerate CSV content outside the core model — an unrecognised ring value or an unrecognised column never fails the render.
- **G-6** Output a **single self-contained HTML file** — no server, no build step to view it, safe to commit to the published-editions folder or email to a client.
- **G-7** Output a **PNG, an SVG and a PDF** of the same radar + legend for slides, print, and vector-editing tools — one shared visual, not two or three things to keep in sync.
- **G-8** Ship as a single compiled binary (Rust), consistent with the existing cargo-based toolbelt.
- **G-9** Make the shareable graphic **self-explanatory on its own**: it carries the edition's identity and a key to its own visual encodings, so a PNG pasted into a slide is not an unlabelled diagram of numbered dots. The HTML output can lean on a tooltip and a detail panel to explain itself; a static image has neither.

## 4) Non-Goals

- **Not a catalogue or decision-link store.** It does not hold ADR references as first-class, traversable relationships — that's ArchMan TSR's job. This tool's CSV can carry a decision-link column as inert text (a filename or URL), same as today.
- **Not a governance or policy-enforcement tool.** No enforcement of rules like "exactly one Default per domain"; at the scale a radar runs to, such rules are cheaper to check by hand. Note this is about *policy*: the tool does perform *structural* input validation (§6.1, §7) — it will refuse a CSV it cannot parse, and warn about values it cannot plot. That is parsing, not governance. A third, narrower case is likewise not governance: the tool may report a limitation of **its own rendering** — FR-88's notice that a high sector count will not read well — because that describes the output rather than judging the input.
- **Not a live/dynamic dashboard.** No database, no server process, no auth. Point-in-time render of a point-in-time CSV, matching how radar editions are already published (dated, immutable, successive).
- **Not reading a standards catalogue directly.** Input is a CSV, not a live scan of the catalogue's own source documents — that mapping stays a manual (or later, scripted) export step, kept separate from this tool's concern.
- **Not a print-styled web page.** See FR-52.

## 5) Users and Use Cases

| Persona | Side | Environment | What they need |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Radar maintainer** | Author | Command line | Publish a dated radar edition from a CSV in one command, and get a slide-ready image from the same source |
| **Engineer inside the organisation** | Reader | Desktop browser | Look up whether a technology is Adopt/Trial/Assess/Retire and *why* — the rationale and domain, not just the position. Under the curated semantics D-49 recommends, an **absent** item means "not featured this edition" rather than "not in use" |
| **External stakeholder** | Reader | Whatever they have, including a phone | Open an emailed HTML file, or read the radar in a slide deck, with no tooling installed |

**UC-1 — Publish an edition.** The maintainer exports the catalogue to CSV, runs the tool once for HTML and once for PNG, and commits both into the published-editions folder under a dated filename. The HTML diffs cleanly against the previous edition (NFR-4).

**UC-2 — Consult a published edition.** A reader opens the HTML file directly from disk or an email attachment, finds an item by scanning the legend or the radar, clicks it, and reads its rationale and last-affirmed date. No network, no server, no install (NFR-2, NFR-5).

**UC-3 — Reuse the visual.** The maintainer drops the PNG into a slide deck, or opens the SVG in Figma to restyle it for a house template, without needing the original CSV.

## 6) Functional Requirements

### 6.1 CSV input

#### 6.1.1 File-level parsing

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-1** | Input is a single UTF-8 encoded CSV file, comma-delimited, quoted per RFC 4180. **A header that is a single column holding `;` or a tab is refused as another separator**, naming the separator and saying to export with commas — Excel writes `;` wherever a comma is the decimal mark (D-59). **A `;` or tab directly after a header field's closing quote**, spaces aside, is reported as that separator too, not as text after a quote, and the refusal never states a column count it did not see (D-65). |
| **FR-2** | A leading UTF-8 byte-order mark is stripped before parsing. *(Excel emits one by default; without this the first header reads as `﻿name` and FR-5 fails on a file that looks correct — see D-11.)* |
| **FR-3** | The first row is a header row. Header names are matched under the normalisation rule of FR-76, the same one applied to values. **A blank record is not a row**: one whose every field is empty or only whitespace and FR-76's format characters, other than the controls FR-90 removes — an empty line, a line of only spaces and tabs, a line holding only a stray byte-order mark left by concatenating two exports, or Excel's `,,,` for a cleared row (D-65). It is skipped wherever it sits, including above the header, so "first row" means the first non-blank record; its lines still count toward NFR-10's line numbers. Blankness is tested before FR-90's removal, so a field holding only an undisplayable character is not blank. A file with no non-blank record has no header and is a hard error naming FR-5's required columns (D-59). |
| **FR-76** | **One normalisation rule, applied to every value read from the CSV, from `--rings` or from `--unplotted-rings`** (including a `rings` or `unplotted_rings` array from `--config`, FR-91). Both operands of a comparison — header names (FR-3, FR-4), `sector` grouping (FR-9), `ring` against the declared list (FR-16, FR-63) and against `--unplotted-rings` (FR-107), `qualifier` (FR-20), `status` (FR-26), and duplicate-`name` detection (FR-28) — are trimmed at both ends of any mix of whitespace and **Unicode format characters** (general category Cf, such as U+FEFF or U+200B) other than the tag characters U+E0001 and U+E0020–U+E007F, have **each run of whitespace inside them reduced to one space** (D-89), and are folded case-insensitively before comparison (D-61). **Folding means Unicode's default lowercase mapping** — locale-independent, the mapping Rust's `str::to_lowercase` implements — not full case folding and not any locale's rules. The **ordering** comparisons of FR-10 and FR-34 normalise the same way, so a stray leading space cannot sort an item away from the name it duplicates. **Every string ordering compares by Unicode code point** — on normalised values for FR-10 and FR-34's case-insensitive keys, on tidied but unfolded values for the **ordinal** tiebreaks of FR-9 and FR-34 (D-63) — which is the same order as comparing UTF-8 bytes and differs from UTF-16 code-unit order above the BMP (D-53). A value **tested for emptiness** is likewise tested after trimming, so a field holding only whitespace or format characters counts as empty wherever emptiness decides behaviour (FR-4, FR-6, FR-7, FR-20's blank-vs-unrecognised split, FR-29, FR-63, FR-93). FR-3's blank-record test is the one exception: it reads the raw field, before FR-90's removal, and counts as blank only whitespace FR-90 does not remove and the format characters above that FR-90 does not remove (D-59, D-65). A value **parsed** is trimmed before parsing (FR-24, FR-25), which does not affect FR-24's rule that it renders verbatim either way. Normalisation never edits displayed text, with three exceptions, all of them FR-76's trim and whitespace collapse (D-89) and none of them a change of case: FR-9's sector label; FR-15's declared ring name, which is what then appears as a band label and legend subheading; and an item's `name`, which is shown tidied wherever it appears (D-76, D-89). Every other column is shown verbatim. Values that do not come from the CSV or `--rings` are outside this rule and state their own: the `-o` extension (FR-52), `--theme` (FR-56), and `--title`/`--subtitle`, whose empty-or-whitespace rule is FR-65's. |
| **FR-4** | A duplicate header name — duplicate after FR-76's normalisation, so `Name` and `name ` collide — or a row whose number of values doesn't match the header's number of columns, is a hard error (NFR-8) naming the 1-based line number. Mismatched rows are reported as one grouped line when there are several (NFR-20, D-62). Whether for one row or several, the message ends with the likely cause NFR-20 gives for what it saw. When the header has unnamed columns, the mismatched-row error says how many of its columns are unnamed — `the header's 5 columns, 1 of them unnamed`. **An empty header cell names nothing and is never a duplicate**, so Excel's trailing empty columns (`…,description,,`) pass (D-59); FR-93 drops the column it heads. |
| **FR-61** | An input path that does not exist, cannot be read, or does not decode as UTF-8 is a hard error. The decoding error names the 1-based line number where decoding failed and says what to do about it — re-save as UTF-8 — because the likely cause is an Excel export in a legacy code page, and "invalid utf-8 sequence" alone doesn't tell an author that (D-27). No transcoding is attempted and no encoding flag is offered. **A NUL byte is refused the same way**, naming its line: it is valid UTF-8, but no text file holds one, and its usual source is UTF-16 saved without a byte-order mark, which would otherwise pass as UTF-8 and fail every other row (D-65). |
| **FR-90** | **A character the output cannot display is removed, with a warning naming where it was: its line and column for a CSV field, or the flag (or `--config` key, with the file) for `--title`, `--subtitle`, a `--rings` entry and an `--unplotted-rings` entry (FR-107).** That means a C0 control other than tab, line feed and carriage return, U+FFFE or U+FFFF, and also DEL and the C1 controls (U+007F–U+009F), which XML allows but no font draws, and the nine bidirectional embedding, override and isolate characters (U+202A–U+202E, U+2066–U+2069), which draw nothing and reorder the text after them (D-72). **Removal comes first**, before every other check reads the field: FR-6's emptiness test, FR-76's normalisation and FR-28's duplicate test all see the cleaned value. The one exception is FR-3's blank-record test, which reads the raw field so that a record holding only such characters is not skipped silently (D-59). So a `name` made only of such characters is empty, and `Kafka` followed by U+0001 duplicates `Kafka`, which is what the author sees in any editor that hides the character. Such a character decodes as valid UTF-8, so FR-61 does not reach it, but the output either cannot contain it (XML forbids it) or cannot draw it. Removing it once, on input, gives every format the same text (SC-7). Flag values follow the same rule as CSV fields because they reach the same output, and a flag is author text like any cell. For a `--rings` entry, removal also comes before FR-63's empty and duplicate checks. Replacing it was the alternative, and both candidates were rejected: U+FFFD has no glyph in FR-85's font, so the PNG would draw a missing-glyph box, and `?` would publish a mark the author never wrote. The warning keeps the removal from being silent. Repeated occurrences group into one line under NFR-20 (D-55). |
| **FR-104** | **A PNG, SVG or PDF warns once per drawn text that holds characters the bundled font lacks, naming the text, the first such character as `U+XXXX` followed by the character itself where it shows on its own (`U+4E2D 中`; a mark, whitespace or an invisible character as the code point alone), how many distinct ones it lacks, and the format being written**, then renders normally (exit 0). It ends with what to do: an HTML page shows the text, or the author can transliterate it. The drawn texts are every plotted item's `name`, every sector, every declared ring, `--title` and `--subtitle`; the tool's own text and the default rings are in the subset, and a test keeps them there. A character is lacking when the face drawing the text shapes it to the missing glyph, so composition, decomposition and default-ignorable characters are the shaper's to resolve: `e` + U+0302 + U+0301 does not warn, since the subset draws `ế`, and neither does a joiner or a variation selector. **A text from a CSV cell warns at that cell's line and column** (NFR-10), a sector at the first row that wrote the spelling FR-9 draws, and several group into one line listing each position with its text (NFR-20). **A text from a flag names the flag**, once per `--rings` entry, and carries no line (NFR-11). **The HTML page does not warn:** a browser falls back to the reader's fonts and draws the character, so the box is the static output's alone (a PDF draws a lacking character as the face's missing-glyph box, as PNG and SVG do). It is the one divergence SC-7 cannot prevent, which is why it is announced (A-4, D-93). |
| **FR-97** | **A line break inside a quoted cell** (Excel writes one for Alt+Enter) **becomes one space in a label and a plain newline in prose.** Labels are the header names and the `name`, `sector`, `ring`, `qualifier`, `status`, `last_affirmed` and `exit_date` columns: every run of line breaks in one is a single space, applied before FR-76's trim and every match, so `moved` + line break + `in` is `Moved In`. Prose is `description`, `domain`, `rationale` and any other column: CR LF and a lone CR each become LF, so the detail panel and table can show the author's paragraphs. No warning: both are faithful to what the author typed. No carriage return reaches any output (D-79). |

#### 6.1.2 Required columns

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-5** | The header **must** contain `name`, `sector`, `ring`, and `description`; `quadrant`, the column's former name, is accepted for `sector`, and a header with both is a hard error naming both (D-101). A missing one is a hard error naming the column; when several are missing, one error names them all — `the header has no 'sector' or 'ring' column; …` (D-62). |
| **FR-6** | `name`, `sector`, and `ring` must be non-empty after trimming on every row (a blank record is not a row, FR-3). An empty one is a hard error naming the 1-based line number and the column. Empty values group per column under NFR-20 (D-62). |
| **FR-7** | `description` **may** be empty. The item renders normally; the detail panel omits the description field, and a warning is emitted. *(An empty description is a content gap, not a structural failure — D-12.)* |

| Column | Meaning | Values | Where it shows |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `name` | The item's name | Free text | Legend, detail-panel heading, marker hover tooltip |
| `sector` | Category — see §6.1.3; `quadrant` is accepted for it (FR-5) | Free text | Sector label, legend grouping |
| `ring` | Lifecycle position — see §6.1.4 | Whatever `--rings` declares | Radial position |
| `description` | The primary write-up of what the item is/does | Free text | Detail panel |

#### 6.1.3 `sector`

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-8** | `sector` is free text. Whatever distinct set of values appears in the CSV becomes that render's sector set — no fixed vocabulary, no config key. |
| **FR-9** | Values are grouped under FR-76's normalisation, so `Tool`, `tool`, and `Tool ` are one sector, not three. Variants are compared **tidied** (trimmed, with each run of whitespace inside one space; D-89), and the displayed label is the tidied form of the **ordinally first** of them — a stable choice that does not depend on CSV row order (D-29). A sector written only as `Tool ` therefore displays as `Tool`, and `Tool  Kit` as `Tool Kit`. |
| **FR-10** | Sectors render in **case-insensitive, locale-independent alphabetical order** (D-7), as FR-76 defines it. There is no override flag in this version — deferred rather than rejected (OQ-17). |
| **FR-11** | Near-duplicate names that FR-76's normalisation does not make equal — `Tool` vs `Tools` — are **not** normalised, merged, or flagged (D-8). |

#### 6.1.4 `ring`

Unlike `sector`, `ring` carries a meaningful order — a lifecycle progression — that cannot be inferred from the names alone. It is therefore declared explicitly rather than derived from the data (D-4).

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-12** | The ring set and its order are declared by `--rings "<Name1>,<Name2>,…"`, **edge-to-centre**: the first entry is the outermost band, the last the innermost (D-60). |
| **FR-13** | Omitting `--rings` yields the default `Assess,Trial,Adopt,Retire` — the in-house lifecycle described in §1 — so a radar already using it renders with zero configuration. |
| **FR-14** | Ring count is N, matching whatever `--rings` declares, using the same equal-width-band geometry as any other count. `--rings "Hold,Assess,Trial,Adopt"` gives the ThoughtWorks convention, which ThoughtWorks lists centre-first as Adopt, Trial, Assess, Hold. **A consequence worth stating where the option is offered:** the innermost band has the least room — about a tenth of the outermost, since capacity follows the radius of FR-33's tracks — and this convention puts **Adopt** there. Whether that is a problem depends on what `Adopt` is being made to mean, which is the advice-versus-estate distinction §1 already draws. On an **advice** radar — ThoughtWorks' own use, "we think the industry should take this up" — Adopt is a curated recommendation list, deliberately short and re-chosen each edition, and the innermost band suits it. On an **estate** radar — "we have adopted this, it is in service" — Adopt is the standing catalogue and grows monotonically, which the innermost band cannot hold: about 2 items per sector at four sectors against 10 in the second band from the centre, at natural size (D-86's growth lifts the innermost to about 5 at 2×, which helps a crowded edition but does not change the ratio). **The failure case is the cross**: estate semantics carried into the ThoughtWorks ring order, which is an easy mistake because the ring *names* are the familiar ones. The default set avoids it by putting the transient `Retire` at the centre and leaving `Adopt` in the second band from the centre. The tool does not object to any of this (§4); it warns per FR-89 when a band actually overflows, and OQ-16 would raise that innermost 2 to 8. |
| **FR-15** | Each comma-separated entry gets FR-76's trim at its ends, and each run of whitespace inside it becomes one space (D-89), so `"Hold, Assess, Trial, Adopt"` and `"Hold,Assess,Trial,Adopt"` parse identically, while a multi-word ring name keeps its words: `Early  Adopt` declares `Early Adopt`. |
| **FR-63** | The declared list must yield **at least one** ring, with **no empty entry** (`"Adopt,,Trial"`) and **no duplicate** after FR-76's normalisation, used for matching (`"Adopt,adopt"`). Each violation is a hard error naming the offending entry. A duplicate would make FR-16's match ambiguous and FR-32's band order undefined; an empty entry would draw a nameless band. Neither can be resolved by guessing what the author meant. |
| **FR-16** | A row's `ring` is matched against the declared list under FR-76, both sides normalised. |
| **FR-17** | A `ring` value outside the declared set is **not an error**. The row is omitted from the polar plot (there is no band for it), and, unless `--unplotted-rings` names the value (FR-107), a warning is emitted **naming the ring set's source** (D-61): `not declared by --rings`, `not declared by the rings key in radar.toml` for a `--config` file, named as given on the command line (FR-91), or `not one of the default rings` (FR-13). **The warning lists the declared rings, says where the row went, and says how to plot it** — add the value to the ring set's source; for the default set, pass `--rings` listing every ring wanted, since it replaces the default set rather than adding to it — so a typo such as `Adpot` shows beside the ring it missed (D-64). **The remedies follow the fact in one order:** how to plot it; then, **only in a run not given `--unplotted-rings`**, `to leave it off without this warning, add it to --unplotted-rings` (`to leave one off …` on a grouped line), since the warning is where an author with a deliberately off-radar ring learns the flag exists, while an author already using it knows it and is more likely looking at a typo (D-96); then, last, the `--table all` clause below. **Where the row went follows FR-106:** an HTML page under the default `--table radar` leaves the row off the page, and the warning adds that `--table all` lists it, but also publishes every other column; under `--table all` the row is in the table view, flagged `not on the radar`; a PNG, SVG or PDF has no table, so the warning says only that the row is not on the radar. Exit code stays 0 (D-5). |
| **FR-107** | **`--unplotted-rings "<Name1>,<Name2>,…"` names rings whose rows are left off the radar without FR-17's warning** (D-96). The list is parsed as `--rings` is: FR-90 first removes characters the output cannot display, with its warning naming the flag; each entry is tidied as FR-15 tidies it; and FR-63's checks apply, with its messages under the flag's name — at least one ring, no empty entry, and no duplicate after FR-76's normalisation — except that a list with no rings at all is `--unplotted-rings: no rings named`. **A row whose `ring` matches a named ring under FR-76, both sides normalised, is treated exactly as any row outside the declared set** — off the polar plot, unnumbered and out of the legend (FR-18), left off the page under the default `--table radar` and listed, flagged `not on the radar`, under `--table all` (FR-106), and not drawn in a PNG, SVG or PDF — **except that it raises no FR-17 warning**. Any other value outside the declared set still warns, so a typo such as `Adpot` is still reported. **A name the ring set declares is a hard error**, because a ring cannot be both drawn and left off; it names the entry and the ring set's source (NFR-10, D-61). The default set (FR-13) counts, since its rings are drawn as declared ones are: `--unplotted-rings: entry 2 'Adopt' is also in --rings, which draws it; remove it from one list`, and, without `--rings`, `--unplotted-rings: entry 1 'Adopt' is a default ring, so it is drawn; to leave it off, pass --rings with the rings to draw, without it`. Once `--rings` is given the default set is not drawn, so a default ring may then be named. Every violation is reported together, in entry order (NFR-16 tier one), and an entry repeating an earlier one is reported only as FR-63's duplicate. **The ring set is checked first:** a malformed `--rings` stops the run before `--unplotted-rings` is checked, since the overlap cannot be judged against a list that did not parse; each list reports all of its own violations, and the first command-line value to fail stops the run with every error recorded so far, while one from `--config` lets the later checks run (NFR-16 tier one, FR-91). **Across sources (FR-91):** when either list comes from `--config`, the overlap is checked after the merge, and the error names both sides by their effective source (NFR-10, D-61) — the entry as `--unplotted-rings` or as the `unplotted_rings` key with its file, and the ring set as `--rings`, as the `rings` key with its file, or as the default set — in the words FR-17 uses for the ring set's source, for example `the unplotted_rings key in radar.toml: entry 2 'Adopt' is also in --rings, which draws it; remove it from one list` and `--unplotted-rings: entry 1 'Adopt' is also in the rings key in radar.toml, which draws it; remove it from one list`. The default set keeps its own wording above, whichever source names the ring. **A named ring that no row uses is not a warning**, as an empty declared ring is not (FR-19). The flag has no short form (OQ-2). |
| **FR-18** | Unplotted rows are **not numbered and get no legend entry**. Item numbers remain contiguous across plotted items only. Under the default `--table radar` they have no table row or detail panel either; `--table all` lists them (FR-78, FR-106). |
| **FR-19** | An empty declared ring — one with no matching rows — still renders its band, label, and circle. |

#### 6.1.5 Optional columns

All are optional. FR-29 governs what happens when one is absent or empty.

| Column | ID | Rule | Values | Where it shows |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `qualifier` | **FR-20** | An exact match on `conditional`, normalised per FR-76, renders a **hollow** marker. **Every other value** — blank, `default`, or unrecognised — renders a **solid** marker. An unrecognised **non-blank** value emits a warning (`unrecognised qualifier value '…'`). A **blank** value, or an absent column, means *nothing was claimed* — no warning, field omitted (FR-29) — the same treatment FR-26 gives a blank `status` (D-35). Asymmetry is deliberate — D-6. | `default \| conditional` | Marker fill (G-3) |
| `qualifier` | **FR-21** | The rule keys off the **populated value**, never off any specific ring name, so it behaves identically under a different `--rings` list (a ring called `Use` or `Approved`). | | |
| `domain` | **FR-22** | Free text, rendered verbatim. | Free text | Detail panel |
| `rationale` | **FR-23** | Free text, rendered verbatim. | Free text | Detail panel |
| `last_affirmed` | **FR-24** | Expected as ISO-8601 `YYYY-MM-DD`. An unparsable value is rendered **verbatim** with a warning, not rejected (D-13). Not used for any computation in this version. | Date | Detail panel |
| `exit_date` | **FR-25** | Same parsing rule as `last_affirmed`. Shown **whenever present, regardless of ring** — meaningful on whichever ring represents exiting, but ring-agnostic like `qualifier`. | Date | Detail panel |
| `status` | **FR-26** | **Author-supplied per edition**, not computed by the tool (D-9). Matched per FR-76. An unrecognised **non-blank** value falls back to `No Change` with a warning. A **blank** value, or an absent column, means *absent* rather than `No Change` — no warning, the plain marker (FR-40), field omitted (FR-29) — mirroring FR-20's blank-versus-unrecognised split rather than contradicting FR-29 (D-35). | `New \| Moved In \| Moved Out \| No Change` | Marker treatment (FR-40) + legend badge, and — **when a value is present** — stated explicitly in the detail panel, **including `No Change`**, since "confirmed unchanged this edition" is itself worth showing |
| `link` | **FR-96** | **A web address for the item**, such as the product's home page. A value beginning `http://` or `https://`, compared case-insensitively after FR-76's trim, becomes a link: in the detail panel, and in the table view's `link` column, which FR-78 gives it as an optional column present in the header. The link opens in a new tab (`target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"`), says so to assistive technology, and its text is the address as written, trimmed. **Any other non-blank value is shown as plain text, never as a link, with a warning**, so a `javascript:` or `data:` address cannot become something a reader clicks (D-77). Blank or absent: no link (FR-29). The address is escaped for the HTML attribute context like any value (NFR-6), and nothing fetches it: it is a link the reader may follow, not a request the page makes (NFR-5). Static outputs (PNG, SVG, PDF) carry no links. | URL (`http`, `https`) | Detail panel, table view |

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-27** | Unrecognised columns are ignored, not an error: nothing is drawn from them, and the HTML page's table view and detail panel show them only under `--table all` (FR-78, FR-106). A column whose header cell is empty is not one of them; FR-93 governs it. One whose name nearly matches a known column warns (FR-108). A known column's table heading is its header cell as written, except the sector column's, which is always `Sector`, even under a `quadrant` header (D-101). The same CSV that feeds BYOR today keeps working unmodified, and a team may carry any extra column it finds useful (a revisit date, a survey note, a ticket reference) without the tool knowing about it in advance. |
| **FR-93** | **A column whose header cell is empty — an unnamed column — is dropped:** it reaches neither the table view (FR-78) nor the detail panel, under either `--table` (FR-106), and is never one of FR-5's required columns. **A row holding a non-empty value under it emits a warning** naming the line and the column's 1-based position; NFR-20 groups these by column position, counting values. A column empty on every row, such as Excel's trailing padding (D-59), passes silently (D-61). |
| **FR-108** | **A header that nearly names a known column emits one warning**, on the header's line at that column, naming the header as written and the column it probably meant: `line 1, rationalle: not a column radargen knows; did you mean 'rationale'?`. A header is a near miss when, under FR-76, it is not itself a known column name but is within **one edit** of one the header does not already have, or **two edits** when the header is longer than that name and the name is longer than four characters (so never for `name`, `ring` or `link`), counted as Damerau–Levenshtein distance, so a transposed pair is one edit. `state` is therefore not a near miss of `status`. Of several candidates, the fewest edits wins, then the first in the order `name`, `sector`, `ring`, `description`, `qualifier`, `domain`, `rationale`, `last_affirmed`, `exit_date`, `status`, `link`, then `quadrant`, which stands for the sector column, so a misspelt `quadrant` suggests `sector` (D-101). A header FR-4 rejects as a repeat warns once, at its first occurrence; a misspelt required column still fails FR-5, and the warning prints with the error. It applies to every format and under either `--table`. Every other unrecognised column stays silent (D-95, D-98). |
| **FR-28** | Two rows with the same `name` — the same under FR-76's normalisation, so `Terraform` and `terraform ` count as a pair — are both rendered as distinct items, and a warning is emitted. The tool does not deduplicate. **The warning names the earlier line and the earlier row's spelling, says names match ignoring case and extra spaces, and says both are drawn**, so rename or delete one if they are the same item; NFR-20's grouped form lists each name in its first row's spelling (D-64). |
| **FR-29** | An optional column that is absent from the header, or empty on a given row, causes that field to be **omitted** from the detail panel and table view for that item — never rendered as an empty label, a placeholder, or a dash. |

#### 6.1.6 Example CSV

Three of the ten columns are matched against a known set of values, normalised per FR-76 — though `ring`'s set is whatever `--rings` declares, and FR-17 tolerates values outside it. Everything else is free text.

```csv
name,sector,ring,description,qualifier,domain,rationale,last_affirmed,exit_date,status
Terraform,Tool,Adopt,"Declarative infrastructure-as-code tool for provisioning cloud and on-prem resources.",default,Infrastructure as Code,"Default IaC tool across all managed environments.",2026-06-01,,No Change
Pulumi,Tool,Adopt,"Infrastructure-as-code authored in a general-purpose language instead of a domain-specific syntax.",conditional,Infrastructure as Code,"Acceptable where a client mandates a general-purpose language over HCL.",2026-06-01,,New
Ansible,Tool,Trial,"Agentless configuration management and orchestration over SSH.",,Configuration Management,"Bounded trial on one managed estate; was Assess last edition.",2026-05-12,,Moved In
Chef,Tool,Retire,"Agent-based configuration management using a Ruby DSL.",,Configuration Management,"Superseded by the Ansible trial outcome; migration owned and scheduled.",2026-03-15,2027-01-31,
Kubernetes,Platform,Adopt,"Container orchestration platform for deploying, scaling, and managing containerised workloads.",default,Container Orchestration,"Default for all new containerised workloads.",2026-06-01,,
Trunk-Based Development,Practice,Adopt,"Developers integrate small, frequent changes into a shared trunk.",default,Delivery Practice,"Default branching model for managed delivery teams.",2026-06-01,,
Legacy Ticketing-Driven Change,Practice,Rejected,"Manual, ticket-driven change approval replacing automated policy gates.",,Operations Practice,"Declined during the 2026 review — see the domain ADR.",2026-01-10,,
```

The last row (`ring = Rejected`) exercises FR-17/FR-18: not a declared ring, so it is skipped from the graphic, with a warning, rather than failing the parse, and is listed, flagged, in the table view under `--table all` (FR-106). Every optional column is left empty at least once, exercising the "absent means don't render that field" behaviour.

### 6.2 Rendering

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-30** | Sectors are equal angles of the circle; the sector count derives from the distinct `sector` values present (FR-8). |
| **FR-31** | The 12 o'clock position, where ring labels sit, is aligned to a sector **divider**, never to a sector's centre. Since markers are always inset from a divider by a fixed angular margin (FR-33), this guarantees no marker can ever coincide with a ring label, at any sector count. |
| **FR-32** | Ring bands are equal-width radii, in the order `--rings` declares: the first declared ring is the outermost band and the last the innermost (FR-12). Band count derives from the declared ring count — N bands, not a fixed four. **Band widths are fixed by the ring count alone and never by how many items a ring holds** — a crowded ring does not grow at a sparse one's expense (D-47). The whole radar may grow, every band alike, by D-86's growth factor. |
| **FR-77** | **Start angle and rotation direction.** Sector dividers sit at `-90° + i × (360 / N)` for `i` in `0..N`, measured in SVG coordinates where 0° is 3 o'clock and angle increases **clockwise** on screen. Divider 0 is therefore at 12 o'clock, satisfying FR-31, and the sector **first** in FR-10's order occupies the span immediately **clockwise** of it, with successive sectors following clockwise. Without this, FR-31 pins a divider without saying which sector follows it or which way the order runs — six valid layouts at N=3, all satisfying every other requirement (D-36). |
| **FR-33** | Within a sector+ring band holding `n` items, item `j` (0-based, in the FR-34 order that numbers them) is placed at angle `start + span × (0.08 + ((j + 1) / (n + 1)) × 0.84)`, where `start` is the band's opening divider per FR-77 and `span` is the sector's angle, `360 / N`. The **8% inset at each end** is a proportion of the sector, not a fixed angle, so it scales with sector count; at N=1 the single divider is both edges and the inset applies to each side of the full 360° span, leaving a visible gap at 12 o'clock. Radial position is `inner + (outer - inner) × (0.38 if j is even else 0.68)` — **alternating by index parity, never by a PRNG**, which would break NFR-4. Legibility degrades as items per band grows; markers **overlap rather than being dropped, shrunk, or capped**, and the band's items stay fully listed in the legend and the table view, which is where a crowded radar is read (FR-89, D-48, D-86). This is not an error condition (D-36). |
| **FR-34** | Items are numbered sequentially, ordered by **sector** (FR-10), then **ring in the declared order**, which is the order the legend presents (FR-35) — then **`name` case-insensitively, locale-independently**, then `name` **ordinally**, tidied (FR-76, D-89) — the FR-9 tiebreak, so two names differing only in case do not fall through to row order the way D-29 stopped them doing for labels — then CSV row order as a last resort. Numbering follows presentation order so that a sector's numbers **ascend as the reader scans down its legend group** (D-33). The tiebreak chain is fully specified because output determinism (NFR-4) depends on it. |
| **FR-35** | A side legend maps number → name, grouped by sector and sub-grouped by ring, with rings in the **declared** `--rings` order — edge-to-centre, which for the default set is lifecycle progression, earliest stage first. The rule is positional, not semantic: it follows whatever list is declared without reading meaning into ring names (FR-21, D-4, D-60). This order is the one FR-34 numbers in, so a sector's numbers ascend as the reader scans down its group. Each sector group carries a **swatch in that sector's colour** (FR-67) beside its heading — the one place the colour→sector mapping is stated in words, which is why FR-66 leaves sector colour out of the key. The three levels — sector, ring, item — are distinguished by **indentation as well as type style**, each stepped in under the one above. Weight and letter-spacing alone leave the levels reading as an alternating flat list, which is what makes a long legend hard to scan. While the legend is hidden (FR-103), an item's sector is still given by its position and the sector labels at the circle's edge (FR-39), and in words by the table view's Sector column; that suffices. |
| **FR-74** | A legend row carries the number, the name, and — **inline after the name**, not on a line of its own — whichever of `qualifier` and `status` are present — `No Change` excepted, which FR-40 draws as the plain marker and no row shows (FR-50). The **ring is not repeated on the row**: it is already the subheading the row sits under, and restating it on every row is noise that costs a line's height across the whole legend. Where the trailing text does not fit beside a wrapped name it takes its own line rather than being truncated. **The inline trailing text continues the name's own text**, after a no-break space, so a reader's text spacing pushes it along rather than running the name into it (D-78). |
| **FR-75** | The row's **accessible name still carries the ring**, even though the visible row does not. Grouping conveys the ring to someone reading the legend as a shape; it conveys nothing to someone arriving at a single row via Tab or a screen reader's control list, where FR-50 requires the label to state the item's position. Visual de-duplication must not become an information loss in the accessibility tree. |
| **FR-36** | Hovering a marker shows its number and name as a native tooltip (an SVG `<title>`). A number on a dot is not identifiable without it. |
| **FR-37** | Hovering a marker highlights its legend row, and vice versa — **on hover, not only on click**. Scanning a long legend to match a numbered dot is real friction at the item counts a mature radar accumulates. |
| **FR-38** | Clicking a marker or legend row opens a detail panel showing `description` plus whatever optional-column data is present. Clicking the same target again, pressing Escape, or using an explicit close control closes the panel **and** clears the selection. There is no state where a panel is open but nothing indicates why, and no state the user cannot return to nothing-selected from. |
| **FR-72** | **Selection is indicated on the marker *and* the legend row, whichever of the two was activated** — the relationship is symmetric, so the indication must be. Selecting from the legend without marking the marker leaves the reader unable to tell which of N numbered dots the open panel describes, which is the question the legend exists to answer. |
| **FR-73** | The selected state is **visually distinct from the hovered state** on both marker and legend row, since the two can apply to different items at once. Selection is **not** drawn as a ring around the marker: rings already encode `status` (FR-40), and a second concentric ring would be read as one. |
| **FR-67** | Each sector takes the palette entry at its **index in the sorted sector order** (FR-10), cycling from the start when sectors outnumber palette entries. Colour is therefore a function of the sector names alone, which is what makes it reproducible across runs and machines (NFR-4). Repeated colours at high sector counts are tolerable only because of FR-39. |
| **FR-81** | **The built-in palette has eight series entries, in this order:** `#0072B2` blue, `#C25400` vermillion, `#00857A` teal, `#787832` olive, `#B5476B` rose, `#2F9626` green, `#C431B8` magenta, `#9D5A43` terracotta. The **order is normative, not just the set**: FR-67 assigns by index, so a three-sector radar uses only the first three, and the list is arranged most-separable-first so that every prefix is itself a good palette. Series values are **shared by both built-in themes**; only the neutral roles differ (FR-82). Ships as `docs/product/palette.sample.css`, which is **byte for byte what `--create-palette` writes** (FR-101) and so is itself a valid `--palette` file; `palette-proof.html` in the same folder carries the verification (§10). |
| **FR-82** | **The remaining colour roles**, which a `--palette` file must also give (FR-86): `--surface-1` (radar ground), `--surface-2` (legend, detail panel, table), `--text-primary`, `--text-muted`, `--stroke-ring`, `--stroke-divider`, `--marker-hollow` (FR-20's conditional fill, which is the surface behind the marker), and `--focus` (FR-48's indicator). Light: `#FFFFFF`, `#F4F5F7`, `#16191F`, `#585F6B`, `#7E858F`, `#848B96`, `#FFFFFF`, `#0B5FA5`. Dark: `#15181D`, `#1E222A`, `#E8EAEE`, `#A6AEBB`, `#6C7480`, `#666E7A`, `#15181D`, `#7FB8E6`. Every value is verified against SC-17: series entries and strokes clear **3:1** on both surfaces of their theme, text clears **4.5:1**. Ring circles and sector dividers take the 3:1 rule rather than being treated as decorative, because they are what make a band or sector readable as a region. **A number drawn on a solid marker or legend badge takes, per series entry, whichever of `#FFFFFF` and `#16191F` contrasts more with that entry**, decided when colours are bound rather than listed as a role, so it covers a `--palette` file's entries as well as the built-in eight. No single ink serves all eight: white on FR-81's green is 3.81:1, short of the 4.5:1 text floor, and `#16191F` falls short on all seven of the others. Chosen per entry, the built-in eight take white except green, which takes `#16191F`, and the lowest ratio is teal's 4.53:1 (D-54). |
| **FR-83** | **Palette separability degrades along the list, and this is a stated bound rather than a defect.** Minimum pairwise perceptual distance, normal vision / colour-vision deficiency: entries 1–3, ΔE 49.2 / 12.9; 1–5, 46.6 / 11.8; 1–7, 42.1 / 8.2; 1–8, 32.1 / 8.2. Eight is the ceiling because one colour set clearing 3:1 on **both** themes is confined to relative luminance 0.12–0.30, and within that band dichromats have roughly one usable hue axis — so no ninth entry exists that is distinct to both normal and deficient vision (D-41). Past eight sectors FR-67 cycles, so a colour repeats — but **not usually on adjacent sectors**. Because the last sector and the first are neighbours around the circle, a repeat lands on an adjacent pair only when the sector count is **one more than a multiple of eight** (9, 17, …); at 10 through 16 the repeats are separated by at least one other colour. That makes **N=9 the worst case**, which is the count D-41 chose eight entries to push the problem out to. FR-39 keeps either case legible rather than ambiguous. No warning is emitted — that would be the governance behaviour §4 rules out. |
| **FR-85** | **The pinned font is Inter, as two static instances — Regular (400) and SemiBold (600) — subset to Latin, Latin Extended, Vietnamese, Greek and Cyrillic (Google Fonts' `latin`, `latin-ext`, `vietnamese`, `greek` and `cyrillic` ranges, with their `-ext` ranges where Inter draws them) and vendored pre-subset.** Chosen for FR-70's constraint rather than for taste: the narrow arrangement puts 12.5px legend text on a phone and SC-6 asserts it stays legible, which is the brief Inter is designed against (large x-height, open apertures). Licensed **OFL-1.1**, which grants the redistribution NFR-7 needs and the embedding NFR-5 needs; the licence text ships beside the font files. Three qualifiers are load-bearing. **Static, not variable** — a variable font's metrics depend on axis coordinates, so a browser and `resvg` resolving an axis differently would reintroduce exactly the HTML-versus-PNG divergence FR-84 closes (D-43). **Two weights** — FR-35 separates the legend's three levels by indentation *and* type style, which two weights plus size and letter-spacing cover; every additional weight is bytes inlined into every published edition (NFR-5). **Subset once and vendored, not subset at build time** — a build step is one more thing that can differ between machines, which NFR-4 does not tolerate. **Latin Extended, not Latin-1 alone** — `latin` stops at Latin-1, so a Czech, Polish, Hungarian, Baltic or Turkish name would draw a missing-glyph box in the PNG while the browser fell back to a system font, breaking SC-7's parity with no warning; the extended range roughly doubles the inlined WOFF2 pair, from about 62 kB to about 117 kB (D-43). **Vietnamese, Greek and Cyrillic as well**, because Inter already draws them and they cost only bytes, which the regenerated subset measures; scripts Inter does not draw stay out (A-4, D-68). |
| **FR-86** | **A `--palette` file must define every colour role of FR-82 — all eight — or it is a hard error naming the missing role.** **Every value, roles and series entries alike, is a `#RRGGBB` hex colour** (case-insensitive), the form `palette.sample.css` uses. Anything else is a hard error naming the role, because FR-82's marker-number ink is computed from the value's luminance and cannot be computed from `rgb()`, a named colour or `#RGB` without a CSS colour parser the tool does not otherwise need (D-54). Partial definition is not filled in from the built-in palette: mixing a custom surface with a built-in text colour silently produces the contrast failure NFR-1 already disclaims responsibility for on custom palettes, and the author would have no way to see which colours came from where. This is the structure-is-fatal line of NFR-9 applied to a file rather than a row (D-44). |
| **FR-87** | **A `--palette` file's series entries are a list, not a fixed count.** It must define `--series-1`, and may define as many further entries as it likes, **numbered contiguously**; the count it declares is the palette size FR-67 cycles over. Five entries give a five-colour palette, twelve give a twelve-colour one. A **gap** (`--series-1`, `--series-2`, `--series-4`) is a hard error naming the missing index, or the first and last index of a run of them, since it cannot be told from a three-entry list with a typo. A single entry is legal and renders every sector in one colour — FR-39 keeps that readable, and §4 rules out warning about a choice the author may have meant. The free count is a **consequence of the file being a list**, not a feature offered for its own sake: a custom palette serves one theme and so has roughly twice the usable luminance band, but **more than eight sectors is a taxonomy problem the renderer should not be read as endorsing** (A-5). Nine or more entries are accepted without comment — §4 rules out warning about it — and are not the recommended answer to a crowded radar. |
| **FR-102** | **A `--palette` file is exactly one `:root` rule of custom-property declarations, and nothing else** (D-92). A byte-order mark at the start of the file is stripped, as FR-2 strips one; a NUL byte is refused as FR-61 refuses it, naming its line, before any other check. CR, LF and CRLF each end a line, and all three are whitespace. **Comments** (`/* … */`) are removed before anything else is read, each counting as whitespace and keeping its line breaks, so one may appear anywhere, including between a value and its `;`, and a line number in any diagnostic is the line of the file as written; an unclosed comment is a hard error naming the line it opens on. **What remains is one rule whose selector is exactly `:root`**, matched ASCII case-insensitively, whose body is declarations of the form `--name: value`, separated by `;`. The last `;` is optional, and an empty declaration (`;;`) is ignored. **Each of these is a hard error naming what it found and the line it is on:** any other selector, rule or at-rule (`:root[data-theme="dark"]`, `html`, `:root, html`, `@media`, `@import`), including a second `:root` rule; a rule with no closing `}`, named at the line it opens on; text outside the rule; a declaration with no `:` or an empty name; a property that is neither one of FR-82's eight roles nor `--series-N`, with N a positive integer written without leading zeros; and a property declared twice, naming both lines. Property names match exactly, since CSS custom-property names are case-sensitive, so `--Surface-1` is an unknown property rather than a spelling of `--surface-1`. Values are trimmed of surrounding whitespace and must then be `#RRGGBB` (FR-86), so `!important` is that error. **A value that runs onto a later line declaring a custom property (`--name:`) is a forgotten `;`:** a hard error on the declaration's line, saying to add the `;`; the value ends there, and the later line is read as its own declaration and checked in the same pass, so a name it misspells or repeats is reported at once (rg-8xm, rg-p9a). **A value is stored uppercased**, so `#ffffff` and `#FFFFFF` render the same bytes. **All of a file's problems are reported together**, as one flag's (NFR-16 tier one): grammar errors, FR-86's missing roles and malformed values, and FR-87's gaps, so an author fixes the file in one pass. **FR-86 and FR-87 are checked over the accepted `:root` rule's declarations only**: a rejected rule's declarations count for neither, and a file whose `:root` rule is missing, or swallowed by an unclosed comment, reports every role missing as well. A file that cannot be read or is not UTF-8 is a runtime failure under NFR-15. |
| **FR-103** | **The HTML page has a legend button, and `--legend shown\|hidden` sets where it starts** (RG-102). The button is an icon in the page controls beside the theme toggle and the same size, on every page with something plotted, a `--palette` page too. **Its name says what a press does**, *Hide legend* or *Show legend*, as visually hidden text, and it carries no pressed state (the rg-nuy ruling); **its icon shows the state**, a list while the legend shows and a crossed-out list while it is hidden, so a press always changes what a sighted reader sees, even where the legend is below the fold; the name is repeated as the button's hover tooltip (`title`), as the theme toggle's is. A press hides or shows the legend in **both arrangements at once**; **the key always shows**. Hidden, the wide legend turns invisible in place, so above FR-69's breakpoint, or below the hidden one, nothing else moves, and the radar may grow into its width up to its existing cap, the key spanning it with the radar but never wider; the narrow legend is removed. **While hidden, the page switches arrangements at FR-69's second, lower breakpoint** (D-97), so on a window between the two a press switches the arrangement, once. The button is outside both arrangements and keeps the focus a keyboard press gives it; where a click does not focus it (WebKit, and Firefox on macOS) and the switch hides what had focus, focus moves to it. **Focus never stays on what a press hid**: it moves to the button, including where a click does not focus a button. A panel opened from a legend row that is then hidden closes to the item's marker. `--legend` is matched case-insensitively, accepts `show` and `hide` without advertising them, and has **no short form**. It is **one warning, not an error, for PNG, SVG and PDF**, which always draw the legend; `--legend shown` there is silent, and so is a `legend` key from `--config`, whatever its value (FR-91, D-100). **Without script the legend always shows and there is no button** (FR-98's rule); **printing always shows the legend**, and leaves out the page controls, which do nothing on paper (rg-kw7); with nothing plotted there is no button and no hidden start; and the choice is **not remembered** between visits, as the theme is not. |
| **FR-88** | **A sector count above six emits one warning naming the count**, then renders normally (exit 0). It fires **once per render, not per row**, and is the tool reporting a limitation of **its own output** — sectors narrow, labels crowd a single circle with no collision rule (OQ-14), and past eight FR-67 begins cycling colours. It is **not** a judgement about the author's taxonomy, which is why it warns rather than refuses and why there is no cap and no `--force`. Six is the threshold because three to six is the range the geometry is comfortable at (A-5), and because a threshold worth warning at has to be defensible — items-per-band has one too, computed rather than assumed (FR-89). Ordering: this warning concerns the render as a whole and carries no CSV line, so it is emitted **before** the line-ordered row warnings, keeping NFR-11's order total. |
| **FR-89** | **A band holding more items than fit without overlapping emits one warning naming the sector, the ring, the count, and how many fit.** Capacity is computed, not guessed: the largest `n` for which FR-33's placement leaves no two markers closer than one marker diameter — a deterministic function of sector count, ring index, ring count and radius. **Before it warns, the radar grows** by the least of D-86's steps at which every two markers are a diameter apart, up to 2× and no further than keeps the wide arrangement showing on a 1440-pixel screen; capacity is evaluated at that growth, so the warning names only bands that still overlap, and how many fit at the growth drawn. Capacity is evaluated at the wide arrangement's scale; in the narrow arrangement markers are relatively larger (D-69), so a band can overlap there before it reaches capacity. It is evaluated against the **plain** marker footprint, FR-40's status rings being the minority case, so a band full of `New` items may overlap without warning; the threshold is a floor and the message says how many fit *comfortably* rather than claiming precision it does not have. One warning per overfull band, not per item. **Overlap is then the behaviour** — markers are not dropped, shrunk, moved to another band, or capped, since each of those misplaces or hides an item the CSV asserts (D-48, D-86). |
| **FR-39** | **Sector identity never depends on colour alone.** Angular position and the sector's edge label always disambiguate an item's sector, independent of whether that sector's colour is distinguishable at a glance. Necessary once the sector count exceeds the 3–4 colours that read reliably on their own — and once FR-67 starts reusing them. |
| **FR-40** | `status` is drawn on the marker itself, following the original ThoughtWorks convention rather than an icon or colour badge: **New** — a full ring around the marker; **Moved In** — a half-ring on the side facing the radar's centre; **Moved Out** — a half-ring on the side facing the outer edge; **No Change** or absent — the plain marker. |
| **FR-41** | A plain HTML table of the items is available as a toggle — the non-visual fallback, the only way to see unplotted rows, which it lists under `--table all` (FR-17, FR-106), and the easiest way to eyeball the whole dataset at once. Unplotted rows, where listed, are visibly flagged **`not on the radar`** — the words FR-17 and the accessible name use, so the flag a sighted reader sees is the one a speech-input user can say (WCAG 2.5.3, D-66) — not silently interleaved. |
| **FR-78** | **Under `--table all`, the table carries one row per CSV data row** — plotted and unplotted alike — with a column for the item number (**empty for unplotted rows**, which FR-18 gives no number), `name`, `sector`, `ring`, and then **every other named column in the header, `description` included**, in header order (D-80). Absent columns get no column; empty cells stay empty per FR-29. Plotted rows come first in FR-34's order, then unplotted rows in CSV order, under a visible flag rather than interleaved. **Table rows are activatable as legend rows are**, through a button in the name cell (FR-38, FR-48, FR-50), opening the same detail panel — this is the *only* route to an unplotted row's panel, since FR-18 gives it no legend entry and no marker to click (D-38). The detail panel is built from the item's table row, so each item's prose is in the page once (D-80), and a row or column the table leaves out is in no panel. **The default, `--table radar`, publishes less:** FR-106 sets which rows and columns it keeps. |
| **FR-106** | **`--table radar\|all` sets what the HTML page's table publishes, and so its detail panels; the default is `radar`** (D-95). **`radar` publishes only what the radar draws:** a row per plotted item, in FR-34's order, and the columns radargen reads — the item number, `name`, `sector` and `ring`, then whichever of `description`, `qualifier`, `domain`, `rationale`, `last_affirmed`, `exit_date`, `status` and `link` the header has, in header order (FR-5, §6.1.5). Every other row and column is left out of the page: a row FR-17 leaves off the radar has no table row and so no detail panel, and FR-17's warning says so and names `--table all`; a column FR-27 does not recognise is in no table cell and no panel, with no warning. **`all` publishes every row and every named column**, as FR-78 sets out, which was the only behaviour before this requirement. Rows and cells are written the same way under both, so `all`'s page is `radar`'s with the extra rows and columns added, its heading aside. **The heading, which names the table's region, says what it holds:** `Items on the radar` under `radar`, and `Every item` under `all`, as before. **With nothing plotted**, `radar`'s table keeps its heading, toggle, region and column headings and holds one row whose single cell spans every column and reads `Nothing is on the radar.`, so the region and FR-98's skip target stay; a CSV with no data rows reads the same (FR-42). Under `all`, a nothing-plotted page lists its flagged rows and a headers-only CSV keeps an empty body, as before. FR-93 drops unnamed columns under both. `--table` is matched case-insensitively and has no short form and no aliases. It is **one warning, not an error, for PNG, SVG and PDF** when it is `all` and for the radar PDF; the full PDF reads it (FR-112), since they have no table; `radar` there is silent, and so is a `table` key from `--config`, whatever its value (FR-91, D-100). |
| **FR-42** | A CSV with **zero data rows** (headers only) renders a valid empty state — ring circles, no sector dividers or labels — not a divide-by-zero or a crash. Since `sector` is required on every row that exists (FR-6), this is the only input shape where the sector count reaches zero. A CSV whose rows are **all unplotted** (FR-17) is a different shape and not an empty state: sectors, dividers, and labels render from the sectors those rows carry, the graphic simply holds no markers, the legend renders its sector groups empty (FR-18 gives unplotted rows no entry), and every row surfaces in the table view under `--table all`, while the default table says that nothing is on the radar (FR-106). With no sectors at all the legend is omitted, as the title is when it is empty. |
| **FR-43** | Sector counts of **1 and 2** render correctly: N=1 draws a single divider at 12 o'clock with one full-circle sector; N=2 draws two dividers. Neither is a special case in the geometry. |

### 6.3 Radar, legend, and key as one SVG document

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-44** | The radar, the legend, **and** the key (FR-66) are authored as a **single self-contained SVG document**, not an SVG radar beside an HTML legend. This is what makes PNG export cheap instead of requiring a full HTML/CSS rendering engine (D-14). |
| **FR-65** | `--title <text>` and `--subtitle <text>` render into the shared document above the radar, so every output format carries the edition's identity (G-9). Both are optional and independent: an omitted flag renders **nothing at all** — except on paper, where the title is always drawn (FR-111, D-105) — no placeholder, no reserved blank space, and the block below moves up. Neither value is derived from the output filename, the CSV, or the clock (D-24). Either may contain `{edition}`, which expands from the author's `--edition` (FR-92); only an explicit `-e today` lets the clock reach it, and that run echoes the date (D-58). **An empty or whitespace-only value counts as omitted** and renders nothing; on the command line it is how a value from `--config` is suppressed (FR-91). With no title, the HTML page's tab title and a visually hidden level-1 heading read *Technology Radar*, so the page still has a name and an outline; the drawn document is unchanged (D-75). **Both wrap at the width their arrangement leaves between the margins:** in the wide arrangement, in every format, the radar part's width less two margins; in the narrow arrangement, the narrow document's less two margins (D-99). |
| **FR-66** | The shared document includes a **key** to the marker encodings the reader cannot otherwise decode: hollow versus solid (`qualifier`, FR-20) and the three `status` treatments (FR-40). The key names each encoding in words beside a sample marker drawn the same way the radar draws it. It is rendered **whenever the radar is** — a fixed block, not one that varies with which encodings this dataset happens to use (D-25). Its entries run in reading order, in as many columns as fit the width the arrangement gives it, so a wide radar's key takes fewer rows; the content never changes with the columns. Sector colours are not part of the key; the legend already groups by sector with a colour swatch, and FR-39 means colour is never load-bearing anyway. |
| **FR-45** | The HTML output embeds this document; SVG export writes it out directly; PNG export rasterises it; PDF export draws it as a vector page. **One layout engine, two arrangements (FR-68), four output modes** — not separately-maintained renderings. Every format's graphic comes from the same code path; what varies between arrangements is where the blocks are placed, and what varies between formats is which arrangement is written out (FR-71). |
| **FR-46** | Long item names are **wrapped, not truncated** — the generator measures text and line-breaks into multiple `<tspan>`s itself. Full names matter more on a shareable static graphic than they would on an interactive page where a tooltip could cover for a truncated label. |
| **FR-79** | **The wrap is greedy first-fit, against a fixed maximum column width.** Words are taken in order and placed on the current line while they fit; the first that does not starts a new line. Greedy is chosen over a balanced algorithm because it is trivially deterministic and reproducible in any language — SC-7 asserts that HTML and PNG wrap at the same points, so the algorithm is part of the contract, not an implementation detail (D-39). A **single token wider than the column is broken mid-token** at the last character that fits and continued on the next line: FR-46 forbids truncating it, and the alternative — letting it overflow — would push it outside the `viewBox`. The legend column has a **fixed maximum width**, **one value for both arrangements**: the narrow document's width less its margins, so a legend wraps at the same points wide and narrow (D-56). Its rendered width is the lesser of that maximum and the longest measured line, so the document does not reserve space it never uses. The radar's sector and ring labels **do not wrap** — they are placed on one line at any length. The legend's headings are not radar labels and wrap at the column like names, with a hanging indent: a heading wider than the column less its indent continues on further lines at that same indent. A sector name is centred on its sector's middle angle and **pushed outward along it** by half its width × \|cos θ\|, so its inner end sits on the label circle's tangent at the label point (300 units out) — on the circle at 3 and 9 o'clock, beyond it elsewhere — whatever its length, and at any radar scale above about 0.3 a long name never crosses the outer ring (D-70). Below that scale a 6 o'clock label touches the ring's stroke whatever its length, a bound that predates the push. Label collision at high sector counts is accepted on the same terms as FR-33's marker crowding, not treated as an error. **Fit is judged at the layout width** (D-78): the shaper's advance plus WCAG 1.4.12's allowance, so a line that fits stays fit when a reader spaces the text out. |
| **FR-84** | **Text is measured through the same shaper that rasterises it.** `resvg` lays out text with `harfrust`; the generator measures with `harfrust`, at the version `resvg` resolves, over the same face, so FR-79's wrap points and FR-46's line breaks agree with the rasterised output **by construction rather than by coincidence** (D-67). Summing raw `hmtx` advances is **not** sufficient and is specifically ruled out: it drops GPOS kerning, diverging by up to 2.2% of a string's width — enough to move a wrap point, in either direction, and zero on strings that happen to carry no kerning pairs, so the error cannot be corrected by a constant and does not show up in a careless sample (D-42). **Letter-spacing is not the shaper's job** and is added by the generator as `n-1` gaps, `n` being the number of shaped clusters — a combining mark that stays a separate glyph shares its base's cluster, as `resvg` applies spacing (D-67). **Sizes and wrap points add 1.4.12's allowance on top of the shaped advance** (D-78); positions where text continues other text use the advance alone. |
| **FR-47** | `<foreignObject>` must **not** be used. See D-15. |

**Scope boundary — what is inside the shared SVG:** the title block (FR-65), the radar circle, its markers, the legend, and the key (FR-66) — the "shareable graphic" a PNG must reproduce. The detail panel and table-view toggle are auxiliary HTML/CSS/JS chrome around that graphic, present in the HTML output only — a static image has no interactive drill-down to reproduce.

**Consequence, accepted — per-item prose is HTML-only:** the per-item prose — `description`, `domain`, `rationale`, `last_affirmed`, `exit_date` — reaches the reader **only through the HTML output's detail panel and table view**. PNG, SVG and PDF carry the graphic, not the write-ups. This is deliberate rather than an oversight (D-26): rendering every item's prose into the shared document would make a forty-item edition's PNG mostly text, and would load the riskiest part of the build (text measurement, R-1) with the work least worth its cost. The formats divide by job — the image is for *showing* the estate's shape, the HTML for *consulting* it (UC-2 versus UC-3). SC-7 is scoped to match.

**Consequence, accepted — no inner scroll:** the legend has **no independent inner scroll**; the page scrolls as a whole. SVG has no equivalent of `overflow-y: auto` for arbitrary content. Nobody has asked for independent-panel scroll as a requirement — it was an artefact of prototyping in HTML, not a stated need.

### 6.4 Accessibility and responsiveness

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **NFR-1** | The HTML output targets **WCAG 2.1 AA**. **WCAG 2.2's target-size criterion (2.5.8) is claimed as well**, through FR-94 (D-66, D-81): legend rows and table buttons by their size, markers by its Equivalent exception. **Text spacing (1.4.12) is met by construction** (D-78): layout reserves the room spaced text needs, so nothing overlaps or is clipped. The table view (FR-41, FR-78) is a fallback for the *data*; it does not by itself make the interactive radar accessible, which is what FR-48–FR-50 are for. **The claim is scoped to the built-in themes.** Supplying `--palette` replaces the colours with ones the tool did not choose and does not validate (§6.6), so contrast under a custom palette is the author's responsibility and the AA claim does not travel with it. Stated here because NFR-1 read as unconditional while §6.6 disclaimed exactly this — one of them had to give, and it is not the one the tool can enforce. **The 2.5.8 claim is scoped the same way to narrow documents up to 480 units wide** (D-71): a dataset whose sector names widen the narrow arrangement past that does not carry it, and FR-95 tells the author. |
| **FR-98** | **With script, a skip link before the radar moves focus to the shown arrangement's first legend row** (WCAG 2.4.1), so a keyboard user reaches the legend without tabbing through every marker. It is visually hidden until focused. Without script it is absent, as is every control the script drives, so no control claims an action it cannot perform. It is a button rather than a link: the legend exists in both arrangements, so it has no single `id` to link to (architecture §5). **While the legend is hidden (FR-103) it reads *Skip to the table* and moves focus to the table view's toggle**, so the bypass holds in both states. |
| **FR-48** | Markers and legend rows are keyboard-operable: reachable by Tab, activated by Enter or Space with the same behaviour as a click, with a visible `:focus-visible` style on both. Table rows are reached through their name cell's native `<button>` (FR-50). **The focus indicator is drawn above every marker**, in a layer after the plot, so a marker later in document order cannot cover a focused marker's outline (D-66). |
| **FR-49** | Escape closes the detail panel and returns focus to the element that opened it. |
| **FR-50** | Markers and legend rows are exposed to assistive technology as interactive controls: `role="button"` with an `aria-label` carrying number, name, and **whichever of `qualifier` and `status` the legend row shows** (FR-74) — `No Change` is shown nowhere, being FR-40's plain marker, so it is absent from the name and the name holds exactly what the row shows — then position (sector and ring, per FR-75) — so a screen-reader user exploring the radar hears what FR-66's key explains, and the visible row text is in the name (WCAG 2.5.3). The "or a native interactive element" alternative earlier drafts offered is **struck for them** — `<button>` has no meaning inside an SVG, and FR-47 rules out the `<foreignObject>` that would be needed to embed one, so it named an escape hatch the graphic cannot reach. **Table rows are outside the SVG, so the argument does not hold there:** they stay native `<tr>` and `<td>`, keeping the row and column-header navigation that makes FR-78's cells readable, and the name cell holds a native `<button>` named by the visible name and, for an unplotted row, the visible `not on the radar` flag. `role="button"` on the `<tr>` would replace every cell with its label and leave the optional columns unreachable from the view FR-41 calls the non-visual fallback (D-66). **Every control states whether the panel is open for it:** `aria-expanded="true"` on the control that opened the panel while it is open and `"false"` on every other, with `aria-controls` naming the panel (WCAG 4.1.2). The state stays out of the accessible name. |
| **FR-94** | **Every control meets WCAG 2.2's target-size criterion (2.5.8) at every rendered width down to 320 CSS pixels**: legend rows and the page's HTML controls by being at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, markers through its *Equivalent* exception (D-81). A legend row's height meets 24 pixels at the smallest scale either arrangement reaches in that range, the narrow arrangement at 320 pixels included. A marker carries an invisible hit area centred on it, counter-scaled in the narrow arrangement as FR-70 counter-scales labels, so the drawn marker is not enlarged by it. Hit areas overlap wherever two markers' centres are closer than twice the hit radius — in most bands below FR-89's capacity, and in the narrow arrangement nearly always. They are drawn below every marker, so a visible marker always takes its own pointer events, and a hit area only fills empty space. **A marker meets 2.5.8 through its *Equivalent* exception** (D-81): the item's legend row and its table button open the same panel, and each meets 24 by 24 pixels on its own. The hit area makes a marker easier to hit, but it is not how a marker conforms: at the narrow end of the range a hit area holds no 24-pixel square, and where a neighbour's hit area comes within 12 pixels of a marker's centre, as on the interaction fixture, the spacing exception fails too. The table's buttons are HTML and meet it directly (D-66). **This holds for a narrow document up to 480 user units wide**, which is as far as D-71 sizes rows and hit areas for; a dataset whose narrow arrangement widens past that falls short at 320 pixels, and FR-95 says so. |
| **FR-95** | **A narrow arrangement wider than 480 user units emits one warning naming the label whose width sets the radar's width, a sector's or a ring's**, then renders normally (exit 0). It fires once per render and, like FR-88, is the tool reporting a limitation of its own output: the radar could not fit the narrow column at the least scale it is drawn at (D-71), so the narrow document widened past the width FR-94's targets are sized for, and on a 320-pixel screen legend rows and marker targets render below 24 pixels and legend text smaller than at 480. The fix is a shorter name for the label that sets the width, which is not always the longest name (a label at 6 o'clock reaches less far than one on a diagonal), and is why the warning names it. A ring name sits across the vertical axis and widens the radar too, so a long `--rings` entry can set the width with every sector name short, or with no sectors at all; the warning then names the ring and its source (D-73). It concerns the render as a whole and carries no CSV line, so it is emitted before the line-ordered row warnings (NFR-11). |
| **FR-105** | **An HTML page whose FR-69 breakpoint is past 1440 pixels emits one warning giving the breakpoint and naming the label whose width sets the wide radar's width, a sector's or a ring's**, then renders normally (exit 0). It fires once per render and, like FR-95, is the tool reporting a limitation of its own output: screens narrower than the breakpoint, 1440-pixel laptops included, get the narrow arrangement, so the radar beside its legend shows only on wider ones. D-86's growth stops at that bound, so only labels pass it: they draw at natural size and never wrap (FR-79), while the legend wraps at its column (D-56). The fix is a shorter name for the label that sets the wide radar's width at scale 1, named as FR-95 names its own (D-73); it can differ from FR-95's, since a diagonal label's reach includes the label circle's radius, which is larger at scale 1. **PNG, SVG and PDF do not warn:** they draw only the wide arrangement (FR-71). It sits beside FR-95's warning, not in place of it, and prints just before it (D-94); like FR-95 it carries no CSV line, so both print before the row warnings (NFR-11). It reads the breakpoint with the legend shown, not the lower one while it is hidden (D-97).
