Under `--legend hidden`, a laptop between the two breakpoints starts with the wide arrangement; the warning applies once the reader shows the legend. |
| **FR-80** | The HTML output sets `<html lang="en">`. WCAG 3.1.1 is a **Level A** criterion, so NFR-1's AA target already entails it, and nothing else in the document required it. The value is fixed rather than derived or flagged: A-4 already bounds entry text to scripts the pinned font draws, none of them right-to-left, and a flag for it would be configuration nobody has asked for. |
| **FR-51** | The HTML output is usable down to a phone-width viewport with no horizontal page scroll. Each arrangement scales fluidly (`width: 100%; height: auto` over its computed `viewBox`). **The wide arrangement's radar fills the window and nothing else does** (D-85): HTML draws the wide arrangement as four SVGs in a grid, the radar's growing to the viewport's width less the legend's column, or to the width at which it is as tall as the viewport below the page controls and the title, whichever is smaller, never below 0.8 of its own width; the legend draws at one pixel per unit and scrolls in a box as tall as the radar; the key draws at one pixel per unit at most, shrinking with the radar's column below its natural width; and the title, cut as wide as the radar part (D-97), draws at one pixel per unit at most, shrinking only where the whole arrangement is narrower than that; the page controls, detail panel and table share the radar's column and the legend's as their cap. PNG, SVG and PDF draw the wide arrangement as one document. The narrow one is capped at its own width. While the legend is hidden (FR-103) the wide legend keeps its column, invisible, and the radar spans both columns, capped as before, so it grows only where the legend was holding it back; the key spans them with it, never wider than the radar. The **wide** arrangement is shown above the breakpoint and the **narrow** one below it, selected by a CSS media query on `display` (D-16, FR-69); while the legend is hidden that is D-97's lower breakpoint, not FR-69's. |
| **FR-68** | The generator emits the shared document in **two arrangements** from one layout engine: **wide**, with the legend beside the radar, and **narrow**, with the legend below it. Same items, same numbering, same marker treatments, same key and title block — only the placement of the blocks differs, the width of the legend's panel, which in the narrow arrangement spans the column (the key's rule, plus the panel's inset either side) rather than ending at its widest row, and the width the title wraps at, the narrow document's rather than the radar part's (FR-65, D-99). Neither is derived from the other by CSS; both are laid out by the same code and differ in the translates it applies. HTML draws each arrangement in parts cut from its one document, each part translated to its own origin: the wide in four (D-85), the narrow in two, its body and its legend (RG-102). |
| **FR-69** | The HTML output embeds **both** arrangements and switches between them with a media query toggling `display`. `display: none` — not `visibility` or `opacity` — is required, because it is what removes the hidden arrangement from the tab order and the accessibility tree; without it every marker and legend row is exposed twice (D-16). The breakpoint value is an implementation detail, tuned to where the wide arrangement stops being legible rather than to any device class; `mockup.html` uses 900px. Since D-85 only the wide radar shrinks with the viewport, so the breakpoint is where it reaches 0.8 of its natural size beside a natural-size legend: `W − 0.2 × R` for a wide document `W` wide whose radar part is `R`. *(Since v0.90, a breakpoint past 1440 pixels warns: FR-105, D-94.)* *(Since v0.93, while the legend is hidden (FR-103) the page switches at a second, lower breakpoint, the same rule with the legend's width taken out, `R − 0.2 × R`, written as a second media query that applies only on screen with the script running: D-97.)* |
| **FR-70** | In the narrow arrangement the radar is **scaled down within the document**, to the largest factor, at most 1, at which its labels fit between the margins, its drawn geometry never below 0.3 of an ungrown radar's (a radar grown by D-86's factor `g` may scale down to 0.3 / `g`, so a phone draws it as it would an ungrown one); the document widens only when even 0.3 does not fit (D-71). The radar needs roughly 700 user units to hold its sector labels; a document that wide renders 12.5px legend text at about 6px on a phone, which fails the legibility SC-6 asserts. Text is authored at its natural size and the radar is scaled to fit around it. Radar-internal labels are counter-scaled so ring and sector names survive the reduction. Markers, their numbers and the radar's strokes scale with the radar but **never below 7/9 of their natural size**, so a 9-unit marker number draws at no less than 7 units while the radar's geometry keeps shrinking (D-69). FR-94's hit area is not floored; it is counter-scaled like the labels. |
| **FR-71** | **PNG, SVG and PDF export the wide arrangement.** A static image has no viewport to respond to, and the wide arrangement is the one shaped for a slide (G-7). The narrow arrangement exists only inside the HTML output. |
| **FR-100** | **`--legend-columns <N>` flows a static output's legend into N columns** (D-90). It applies to PNG, SVG and PDF; **it is ignored, not an error, on HTML output**, whose legend scrolls beside the radar (D-85), as FR-57 is for `--width`. N is 1–4 inclusive, default **1**, which is the single column FR-71 exports without the flag; anything outside is a hard error from clap's ranged value parser, and a `legend_columns` key from `--config` is held to the same range with the same message (FR-91). On paper the page sets the legend's columns and `--legend-columns` does not apply (FR-111). This is the wide arrangement with its legend block re-flowed, not a third arrangement: FR-45, FR-68 and FR-71 hold with that one difference in the legend's placement (SC-7). The columns stand left to right beside the radar, each the width of the single legend column, on **one legend panel** that spans them all, separated by the panel's own horizontal inset; the legend's heading tops the first column, and each later column's first sector heading stands level with the first column's. **Reading order and numbering do not change**: rows run down the first column, then the next (FR-35), numbered as FR-34 numbers them. **A column break falls only between sector groups**, never inside one, so each sector keeps its heading, swatch and ring subheadings together and nothing is repeated. With at least N sector groups there are exactly N columns, none empty; with fewer, one column per group. Of the possible sets of breaks, the one whose tallest column is shortest is taken, a column's height measured as drawn at the layout width (FR-79, D-78), headings and wrapped rows included; among equals, the one whose first break is earliest, then whose second is, and so on, so the arrangement is a function of the content alone (NFR-4). The document is as tall as the title block plus the taller of the radar with its key and the tallest legend column, and as wide as the columns make it; `--width` scales that document as usual, and FR-64 and D-87's limits apply unchanged. |

### 6.5 Output formats

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-52** | Output format is inferred from the `-o` file extension — `.html`, `.png`, `.svg`, `.pdf` — not a separate flag. Matched case-insensitively, so `.PNG` works. An unrecognised or absent extension is a hard error. |
| **FR-62** | `-o` **overwrites an existing file without prompting** — editions are re-rendered constantly during authoring, and a confirmation prompt would break the non-interactive use the tool is built for. It does **not** create missing parent directories: a mistyped path should fail loudly rather than silently produce a tree nobody meant to make. A missing parent directory is a hard error naming the path. **The output naming a file the run reads — the input CSV, the `--config` file or the palette — is a hard error** too, compared as files rather than as names, so a typo cannot replace the data the radar was rendered from however the path is spelled (D-74). |
| **FR-53** | **HTML**: the shared SVG embedded in a self-contained file with the full interactive layer (§6.2, §6.4) as inline JS/CSS. |
| **FR-54** | **SVG**: the shared document (FR-44) converted directly, its text outlined (D-88), no rasterisation. Vector output stays crisp at any zoom or print size and opens in PowerPoint, Figma, and Illustrator for touch-up without the original CSV. |
| **FR-55** | **PNG**: the shared document rasterised via a pure-Rust renderer — [`resvg`](https://github.com/RazrFalcon/resvg) is the library-first choice, avoiding a headless-browser dependency entirely. |
| **FR-110** | **PDF**: the shared document (FR-44) as a **one-page vector PDF**, written directly from the document, with no rasterisation and no intermediate SVG (D-103). **The page is the document's size**: its MediaBox is FR-57's intrinsic size at 72 points per 96 CSS pixels, so `--width` sets the page width as it sets an SVG's, and the height follows; paper is FR-111's. The MediaBox is that size rounded up to whole points, and the ground covers the whole page, so no viewer shows a light edge on a dark page. A side past the 14,400 points (200 inches) viewers accept is written with a `/UserUnit` that brings it under, so the page keeps its size without being refused or cropped. PNG's pixel caps and small-text warning do not apply (D-87): a vector page has no pixels to run out of and zooms. **Its text is text**: every run is drawn in an embedded subset of the bundled Inter face (FR-85), each glyph where the shaper places it (FR-84), with a ToUnicode map, so a reader can select, copy and search the names the CSV holds. A halo is drawn as stroked glyph outlines, not text, so each name is text once. The exception is FR-104's: characters the bundled font lacks draw as boxes and copy as nothing, and a link is not an annotation. **Untagged**: it carries no structure tree until FR-114; its catalog sets `/Lang (en)` and asks viewers to show the document title, and its info dictionary holds `/Title` (FR-65's title, or *Technology Radar*, D-75), `/Subject` (the radar's name, as the SVG's `<desc>` carries it) and `/Producer (radargen X.Y)` (SC-25). **Reproducible**: no creation or modification date and no file identifier, so a render is a function of its inputs (NFR-4); only the embedded font is compressed. |
| **FR-111** | **`--paper letter\|a4\|none` puts a PDF on paper** (D-105). Landscape Letter (11 × 8.5 in) or A4 (297 × 210 mm), with 15 mm margins. For the radar PDF it is opt-in: `none`, the default, is FR-110's drawing-sized page, which a reader can scale to print, and lets a command line override a file's `paper`. **The first page holds the title, the radar, the key and the legend's first part, and nothing else.** The legend continues on the following pages in as many columns as fit, breaking only between rows; a heading is never last in a column; a column that starts inside a sector or ring repeats that heading marked *(continued)*; and each continuation page opens with *Legend (continued)*. On paper the legend is drawn at 16/15 of the radar PDF's size, with rows at least 26 px tall since paper has no touch target, so its smallest text prints at 8 pt; the key keeps its size; the radar scales down to fit beside them, no smaller than the narrow arrangement's floor (FR-70). **Every page has a footer**: the title, and the subtitle when given, at the left, and *Page n of m* at the right. **The title is always drawn on paper** — FR-65's, or *Technology Radar* — with the subtitle under it when given. On paper a title past three lines, or a subtitle past two, is cut with `…` so the radar keeps its page; the PDF's `/Title` and the HTML page keep it whole. A legend row taller than a page is drawn whole and warned about. `--width` and `--legend-columns` do not apply on paper. Values match case-insensitively. Config key `paper` (FR-91). |
| **FR-112** | **`--pdf radar\|full`: the full PDF** (D-104). `radar`, the default, is FR-110 or FR-111. `full` adds, after the leading pages, the HTML page's table (FR-78, FR-106: the same rows, columns, headings, cells and order, `--table` applying) on pages of its own, always on paper: Letter unless `--paper a4`. **The table never shares a page with the radar or legend.** Its first page opens with the HTML table's heading (`Items on the radar` or `Every item`); every later page repeats it marked *(continued)* with the sector of its first row and the item numbers it holds (`Items on the radar (continued): Platforms, 41–78`), so a reader can turn to an item as in a dictionary. **The header row repeats on every page.** Body text is 9 pt, the item's name and the header row semibold. The short columns radargen reads — `qualifier`, `status`, `last_affirmed` and `exit_date` — are a muted line under the item's name (the dates after their headings) rather than columns of their own, so the prose gets the width (the maintainer's ruling, 2026-10-23); a 2 px rule marks each change of sector and the start of the unplotted rows. The other label columns (number, name, sector, ring, link) take the width their content needs, up to a cap, and never break a word, an address aside; the prose columns (`description`, `domain`, `rationale` and any other) get at least about 21 characters each, so a CSV with every column radargen reads fits Letter, and share the rest by how much each holds. A column heading breaks only at a space or after an underscore, and its column is never narrower than its longest piece. When even the narrowest labels leave the prose less than that, the PDF is still written and a warning names `--table radar`. Cells wrap between words, and inside a word only when it alone is too wide (FR-79); prose keeps the author's line breaks (FR-97); a link is its address as text (FR-96). **A row is never split across pages** unless it alone is taller than a page; then it is split between lines, its first part ending *continues on the next page* and each later part naming the item *(continued)*, with a warning naming its line. With nothing plotted under `radar`, the table keeps its heading and header row and reads *Nothing is on the radar.* Page 1 of a full PDF names the page its table starts on. Config key `pdf`. `--pdf full` from the flag with a non-PDF output is a warning. `full` and `paper` `none` from the same source are a usage error; across sources the command line wins, as FR-59's flags displace a file's opposing key. |
| **FR-113** | **A box carries its character** *(planned, PR 21c; D-106)*. A character the bundled font lacks still draws as a box with FR-104's warning, but as a drawn path inside marked content whose `/ActualText` is the character it stands for, so it searches and copies as that character rather than as nothing. |
| **FR-114** | **PDF/UA-1** *(planned, PR 21c; D-107)*. Both PDFs, drawing-sized or on paper, are tagged and conform to PDF/UA-1 (ISO 14289-1): a structure tree built from the radar's logical parts across pages (the title, the radar as a `Figure` whose `Alt` is D-75's name, the key and legend as lists under their headings, the table as a table with column headers); every text in marked content; decorative paint, footers, continued headings and repeated header rows as artifacts; the title displayed; `/Lang (en)`; and XMP metadata identifying PDF/UA-1. A validator checks every PDF the tests write (SC-32). The claim assumes English content. |
| **FR-56** | `--theme light\|dark`, default `light`, matched case-insensitively via clap's `ignore_case` so `--theme Dark` works (a `theme` from `--config` is matched the same way, FR-91) — the same courtesy FR-52 extends to `.PNG`. **Applies to PNG, SVG and PDF only** — all are fixed snapshots with no viewer-side choice, so the tool must resolve colours at generation time. Light is the default as the safer assumption for slide decks and PDFs. **Ignored, not an error, on HTML output**, which carries both palettes and lets the viewer choose via `prefers-color-scheme` plus an in-page toggle: a button named by what a press does, **Switch to dark theme** while the light palette shows and **Switch to light theme** while the dark one does. It shows an icon of the theme a press switches to, a moon while the light palette shows and a sun while the dark one does, its name kept as visually hidden text and repeated as the button's hover tooltip (`title`) for a sighted mouse user (rg-7gd), and the icon hidden from assistive technology. It carries no pressed state, so a sighted reader never has to decode one; it follows the viewer's preference until pressed, and appears only when the page's script runs (FR-98's rule). |
| **FR-57** | `--width <pixels>`, default the shared SVG's native width. Height is **always** computed from the SVG's own aspect ratio and is never a second flag — the radar+legend document is not fixed 1:1 (legend height varies with item count and wrapping), so an independent `--height` could silently distort it. Applies to SVG and PDF too, where it sets the embedded intrinsic size. **Ignored, not an error, on HTML output.** |
| **FR-64** | `--width` accepts **320–10000** inclusive; anything outside is a hard error, enforced by clap's ranged value parser so the message is standard usage output. A `width` from `--config` is held to the same range with the same message (FR-91). The floor is where the radar stops being legible at all; the ceiling stops a fat-fingered digit turning into a multi-gigabyte rasterisation. Neither bound is a considered aesthetic judgement — they exist so the failure is a usage error rather than an unreadable file or an out-of-memory kill. |
| **FR-58** | **Printing the HTML output directly is not a supported path.** The interactive HTML carries toolbar chrome, a detail panel, and a table toggle that are not meant for paper, and no print stylesheet is specified beyond keeping the page controls off paper (FR-103, rg-kw7); PNG, SVG or PDF export is the answer for print and slides, and PDF (FR-110) for a document to share or print — that is what they exist for (G-7). |

**Explicitly excluded from this version:** JPEG output (D-17), batch/glob multi-radar rendering (D-18), and auto-computed `status` by diffing editions (D-9). The `status` *column* itself is in scope (FR-26); only its automatic computation is deferred.

### 6.6 CLI

*The tool, the crate, and the command it installs are all **`radargen`** (D-32). Exact flag spelling (`--width` vs `-w`) is settled at implementation planning; what is fixed here is the set of options and their behaviour.*

**No subcommand.** The tool does one thing — one CSV in, one file out — so the input is a positional argument and there is no verb to name (D-28). Three asides, `--create-template`, `--create-palette` and `--create-config`, write a file to start from — a CSV, a palette and a config file — and are each given alone (FR-99, FR-101, FR-109). `--help` and `--version` come from clap and are not enumerated below.

| Argument / flag | Applies to | Default | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `<csv-path>` | all | *(required, unless `--create-template`, `--create-palette` or `--create-config`)* | Positional. Path to the input CSV (§6.1). Exactly one; there is no glob or multi-file form (FR-60, D-18). Read errors are fatal (FR-61). |
| `-o, --output <path>` | all | *(required, here or in `--config`, unless `--create-template`, `--create-palette` or `--create-config`)* | Output path; format inferred from the extension (FR-52). Overwrites; does not create parent directories (FR-62). |
| `--title <text>` | all | *(none)* | Rendered above the radar in the shared document. Omitted renders nothing (FR-65). |
| `--subtitle <text>` | all | *(none)* | Second line of the title block — the edition date in practice, though the tool attaches no meaning to it and never supplies one (FR-65, D-24). |
| `--rings "<N1>,<N2>,…"` | all | `Assess,Trial,Adopt,Retire` | Edge-to-centre ordered ring list (FR-12–FR-15). |
| `--unplotted-rings "<N1>,<N2>,…"` | all | *(none)* | Rings whose rows are left off the radar, as an undeclared ring's are, but without FR-17's warning; none may be in the ring set, the default set included (FR-107, D-96). |
| `--theme <light\|dark>` | PNG, SVG, PDF | `light` | Resolves the static snapshot's colours at generation time (FR-56). |
| `--legend <shown\|hidden>` | HTML | `shown` | Where the page's legend starts; the page's button shows or hides it either way. PNG, SVG and PDF always draw the legend, with one warning on `hidden` (FR-103). |
| `--table <radar\|all>` | HTML | `radar` | What the page's table, and so its detail panels, publishes: the plotted rows and the columns radargen reads, or every row and named column. PNG, SVG and PDF have no table, with one warning on `all` (FR-106). |
| `--width <pixels>` | PNG, SVG, PDF | SVG's native width | Sets output width; height auto-computed (FR-57). Range 320–10000 (FR-64). |
| `--legend-columns <N>` | PNG, SVG, PDF | `1` | Flows the legend into N columns beside the radar, breaking only between sectors (FR-100, D-90). Range 1–4. |
| `--paper <letter\|a4\|none>` | PDF | `none` for the radar PDF (one page the size of the drawing); Letter for `--pdf full` | Puts a PDF on landscape Letter or A4 pages: the radar page first, the legend continuing, every page footed (FR-111, D-105). |
| `--pdf <radar\|full>` | PDF | `radar` | `full` adds the HTML page's table on pages of its own (FR-112, D-104). |
| `--palette <file.css>` | all | built-in palette | **Ships (D-44, closing OQ-6).** Replaces every colour role defined normatively in FR-81 and FR-82 (`--series-1` onward, `--surface-1`, `--text-primary`, and the rest). Format: CSS custom-property declarations, the representation the HTML output already uses internally. **Exactly one set of colours, not a light/dark pair.** **Mutually exclusive with `--theme` — supplying both is a CLI error** (FR-59), since a single-variant palette leaves nothing to choose between. On HTML output, `--palette` means the theme toggle and `prefers-color-scheme` handling are absent from that build; the page declares `color-scheme: dark` when the file's `--surface-1` is darker than its `--text-primary`, and `light` otherwise, so the browser's own controls and scroll bars match the palette. Every role of FR-82 must be present (FR-86) and series entries must be contiguous from `--series-1` (FR-87), with as many entries as the author wants — which is how a radar exceeds FR-83's eight-colour ceiling. Palette **correctness** — contrast, colour-vision-deficiency safety — remains the **user's responsibility**; the tool validates that the file is complete and well-formed, never that its colours are legible. `--create-palette` writes the starting point, which `docs/product/palette.sample.css` reproduces (FR-81, FR-101); the file's grammar is FR-102. |
| `--create-template <path>` | — | *(none)* | Writes a starter CSV with every column and a few example rows, renders nothing, and never replaces a file; given alone (FR-99, D-83). |
| `--create-palette <path>` | — | *(none)* | Writes the built-in light palette as a `--palette` file to edit, with each neutral's dark value beside it, renders nothing, and never replaces a file; given alone (FR-101, D-91). |
| `--create-config <path>` | — | *(none)* | Writes a starter `--config` file with every key commented out, each with its default and what it does, renders nothing, and never replaces a file; given alone (FR-109, D-102). |
| `-c, --config <file.toml>` | all | *(none)* | **v1.1 (D-57).** Reads `input`, `output`, `title`, `subtitle`, `rings`, `unplotted_rings`, `theme`, `width`, `legend_columns`, `legend`, `table`, `paper`, `pdf`, `palette` and `edition` from a TOML file; a flag on the command line overrides its key (FR-91). Never discovered implicitly. With an `input` key, the CSV need not be given on the command line (FR-60). `--create-config` writes a file to start from (FR-109). |
| `-e, --edition <YYYY-MM-DD>` | all | *(none)* | **v1.1 (D-58).** The edition's date, which `{edition}` in `output`, `title` or `subtitle` expands to (FR-92), or `today` for the local date, which the run echoes. Without `today` the tool never reads the clock. Overrides the `edition` key (FR-91). |

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **FR-60** | The input CSV is a **required positional argument**, unless `--create-template` (FR-99), `--create-palette` (FR-101) or `--create-config` (FR-109) is given, or `--config` is, whose `input` key can name it instead (FR-91); given with `--config`, it overrides the key. Exactly one is accepted. There is no subcommand (D-28), no stdin form, and no way to pass several files. Invoked with no arguments, the tool prints usage and exits non-zero. |
| **FR-59** | `--theme` and `--palette` are mutually exclusive on the command line, enforced via clap's `conflicts_with`/`ArgGroup` so the error message is standard usage output, not hand-rolled validation. **Across sources the command line wins** (D-100): `--palette` drops the file's `theme` key and `--theme` drops its `palette` key, as any flag overrides its key (FR-91); the dropped key is still checked for its type and name, but nothing reads its value. **A `theme` key and a `palette` key in the same file** conflict: clap cannot see them, so the rule is checked after the merge, and it is a usage error with clap's exit status, one line saying to remove one (FR-91). |
| **FR-91** | **`-c, --config <file.toml>` supplies flag values from a TOML file.** Its keys are `input`, `output`, `title`, `subtitle`, `rings`, `unplotted_rings`, `theme`, `width`, `legend_columns`, `legend`, `table`, `paper`, `pdf`, `palette` and `edition`, and each means what its flag means; `input` names the CSV, which the positional argument gives on the command line. **A flag given on the command line overrides the same key**, key by key. An empty `--title ""` or `--subtitle ""` overrides with nothing, which is how a config value is suppressed (FR-65). The file is read only when named: nothing is discovered from the working directory or the home directory, because a render must not depend on where it was run (NFR-4). **Values from the file pass the same checks as their flags, with their flags' exit status and messages in clap's words, one line each, naming the key and the file**, even though clap never sees them: FR-52, FR-62, FR-63, FR-64, FR-100's range, FR-56's case-insensitive `theme`, FR-103's case-insensitive `legend`, FR-106's case-insensitive `table`, FR-86, FR-87, FR-102, FR-107, and FR-59's exclusion within the file; across sources the flag wins instead (FR-59). **Every key is checked, whether or not a flag overrides it,** for its type and for what clap checks on its flag — FR-56's, FR-103's and FR-106's names, FR-64's and FR-100's ranges, and the comma rule below — so a file is valid or not on its own; those problems, FR-59's exclusion and a missing `-o` are usage errors, with clap's exit status. FR-52, FR-62, FR-63, FR-86, FR-87, FR-102 and FR-107 read what a value names, so they run on the effective value after the merge, with their flags' exit status; one that fails on a value from the file does not stop the others, so every problem the file causes is reported in one run unless a command-line value fails first, since one that fails on a flag stops the run as before (NFR-16). A run whose errors include a usage error exits with clap's usage status, and otherwise a failed run exits 1 (NFR-8). An echoed key or value is cut at 40 characters, and past 20 key problems the rest are counted rather than listed, so a file of junk prints a screen, not a flood. **A format-specific value from the file is silent where it does not apply:** `legend` and `table` on a PNG, SVG or PDF, as `theme`, `width` and `legend_columns` already are on HTML (FR-56, FR-57, FR-100); the flags keep FR-103's and FR-106's warnings (D-100). **Types are strict.** `edition` is a string, a date written `YYYY-MM-DD` or `today`, checked exactly as `-e` checks it (FR-92); `rings` is an array of strings, edge to centre, each entry trimmed as FR-15 trims, so `rings = []` is FR-63's no-rings error; `unplotted_rings` is an array of strings in the same form, and `unplotted_rings = []` is FR-107's no-rings error; FR-107's overlap is checked after the merge and names both sides by their effective source, as FR-107 words it. **Accepted gap:** a file's `unplotted_rings` cannot be cleared from the command line, because `--unplotted-rings ""` is FR-107's no-rings error rather than an empty override, so an author who wants a render with the warnings back edits or copies the file; a comma inside an entry of either is a hard error, because the flag could not express it and the two forms must mean the same. `width` and `legend_columns` are integers and the rest are strings. A wrong-typed value, such as `rings = "Adopt,Trial"` or `width = 1920.0`, is a hard error naming the key, and so is **an unknown key**, so a typo like `ring = …` cannot be ignored silently. A duplicate key is a TOML parse error. A file that cannot be read or does not parse is a runtime failure under NFR-15, naming its line where the parser gives one. The file is decoded as the CSV is — one byte-order mark stripped, UTF-8 required, a NUL refused (FR-2, FR-61) — and one larger than 1 MiB is that runtime failure too, since a config is a few hundred bytes and the cap stops a wrong path from being read whole. **Paths in the file (`output`, `palette`):** a relative path resolves against the file's own directory, so a config means the same thing wherever it is invoked from — the directory of the path as given to `-c`, not canonicalised, so a config reached through a symlink resolves against the link's folder; an absolute path is used as written; `~` is not expanded, since that is the shell's job and no shell reads the file, and a message about such a path says so; an empty `output` or `palette` is a usage error naming the key. **A path from the file may name any file, so it is guarded** (D-100): one that exists must be a regular file, so a pipe, a terminal, `/dev/stdin` or a folder is a runtime failure naming the key, never read or waited on; and a palette the file names is never quoted in its errors, which say what is wrong and on which line without the file's text, since the path may lead anywhere, a secret included. The output is checked against every file the run reads (FR-62, D-74). Paths on the command line resolve against the working directory as usual. **A diagnostic about a value from the file names the key and the file** (NFR-10), and all of a file's problems are reported together (NFR-16 tier one). `-o` is required from one source or the other, and with neither it is a usage error. **The CSV is the `input` key** (the maintainer's decision, 2026-10-22), so a scheduled job can run `radargen -c radar.toml` alone: a string path, resolved against the file's folder as `output` and `palette` are, held to a regular file by ruling B, and read through the same 64 MiB cap as a positional CSV (NFR-15). A CSV on the command line overrides it, and with neither it is a usage error, worded as the missing output is: `no input: name the CSV on the command line, or add an input key to radar.toml, such as input = "radar.csv"`. A diagnostic about the CSV names the key and the file when the key named it (`the input key in radar.toml (data/radar.csv): cannot be read: …`), and D-74 compares the output with the input wherever each came from. Unlike a palette's, a CSV's content is not withheld from messages: warnings quote its cells and names by design (NFR-10), so the palette's no-quote rule does not apply to it. |
| **FR-92** | **`-e, --edition <YYYY-MM-DD>` names the edition being rendered, and `{edition}` in `output`, `title` or `subtitle` expands to it**, whichever source the value came from. The date must be a valid calendar date in that form, or it is a hard error, and it expands exactly as written, so dated filenames sort in date order. **A placeholder is `{`, an ASCII letter, then any ASCII letters, digits or underscores, then `}`, matched case-sensitively**; any other brace is literal text, and placeholders in any other value are literal too. **A value containing `{edition}` with no `--edition` given is a hard error naming the value.** **When placeholders are in use**, meaning `--edition` is given or the value contains `{edition}`, **any other placeholder in it is a hard error naming the placeholder**, so a typo such as `{editon}` is caught rather than published. Otherwise a value such as `--title "Radar {beta}"` renders as written, as it did before this requirement existed, so adding it breaks no working command (NFR-12). **An `--edition` that no value uses is a warning**, because it usually means a placeholder was forgotten; the render proceeds. **Order:** FR-90 removes undisplayable characters first, then `{edition}` expands, then `output` resolves against its source (FR-91), then every other check runs, so FR-52 reads the expanded extension and FR-62 checks the expanded path. The tool still never reads the clock (D-24), so re-rendering an old edition with its own `--edition` reproduces it byte for byte (NFR-4). **`-e today`** (exactly that word, lowercase; any other word is an invalid date) is the one exception, at the maintainer's decision (D-58): the machine's local calendar date, in the time zone `TZ` names or else the system's, read once at the start of the run, after which the run behaves exactly as if that date had been typed, unused warning included. The run echoes it with its zone as a warning, `edition 2026-10-22 (today, America/Vancouver); pass -e 2026-10-22 to reproduce this render`, since stderr has no class between warning and error (NFR-9); the zone is its IANA name, else `TZ` as written. **When no zone can be found** — a `TZ` that names none, such as a misspelt `America/Vancuover`, or no `TZ` and a system zone that cannot be determined — the date is UTC's, the echo names `UTC`, and a warning before it says why: `TZ 'America/Vancuover' names no time zone radargen can find, so -e today used UTC; set TZ to a zone such as America/Vancouver`. Falling back silently would date a scheduled job's edition a day off without a word; refusing to render was rejected because the date is still echoed and can be corrected with an explicit `-e`. **`edition` is also a `--config` key** (FR-91, the maintainer's decision of 2026-10-22), which `-e` overrides as any flag overrides its key. Everything here reads the **effective** edition, from either source: the clock is read only when it is `today`, the echo names its source (from the key, `the edition key in radar.toml: edition 2026-10-22 (today, …); pass -e 2026-10-22 …`), the in-use rule and the unused warning (`the edition key in radar.toml (2026-09-01): no value uses it; …`) read it, and a value holding `{edition}` with neither source is the missing-edition error, whose remedy adds `, or add an edition key to radar.toml` when a config file is read. An `edition` key that is not a date or `today` is a key problem, a usage error collected with the others (NFR-16), worded as clap words `-e`'s; a value that needs it is dropped unreported, as a broken key's dependants are. **Rulings (PR 19b):** an invalid date is a flag's error, so it is clap's usage error (NFR-8); the placeholder errors are not usage errors and exit `1`. The placeholder errors are NFR-16 tier-one checks across sources, in FR-91's order (`output`, `title`, `subtitle`): one on a value from the file is recorded, the value is dropped so no later check reports its unexpanded text, and the later checks run; one on a command-line value stops the run with every error so far. Within one value every problem is reported, a missing `--edition` first, then each unknown placeholder in order. The value is named by its source with its text: a title or subtitle quoted (`the subtitle key in radar.toml ('Edition {edition}')`), and from the file cut at 40 characters (FR-91); an output path as written, as every other output error names it. An `-o` that is not UTF-8 is read for placeholders as text, which is exact since they are ASCII; it is used as given unless `{edition}` would have to change it, which is a hard error, since replacing the text would change its other bytes. |
| **FR-99** | **`--create-template <path>` writes a starter CSV to fill in, and renders nothing** (D-83). The file has a header naming every column §6.1 reads, in §6.1's order, then a few example rows that between them show each kind of value: every default ring (FR-13); a `qualifier` of `default`, of `conditional` and blank; each `status`, and a blank one; dates as `YYYY-MM-DD`; a link; and blank optional columns. The rows are examples to replace or delete, and their descriptions say what the columns do. Rendered unchanged, the file draws every row with no warning. Like every output it is UTF-8 with no byte-order mark and LF line endings (NFR-17), and it is the same on every run. **It never replaces a file:** an existing path is a hard error naming it, and nothing is written. It is created only if nothing is at the path, a dangling symlink included, and a failed write removes it; it is written in place rather than renamed in as `-o` is (NFR-14), since a no-replace rename is unsupported on some filesystems and there is no earlier file to protect. Otherwise the run prints nothing and exits 0. The flag is given alone; with a CSV, `-o` or any other flag it is a usage error. |
| **FR-101** | **`--create-palette <path>` writes a starter `--palette` file to edit, and renders nothing** (D-91). The file is **the built-in light palette** (FR-81, FR-82) as one `:root` rule that satisfies FR-86, FR-87 and FR-102: `--series-1` to `--series-8` in FR-81's order, then FR-82's eight roles in FR-82's order, one declaration per line. **Each neutral role's line names its dark-theme value in a comment after the declaration's `;`**, so an author wanting a dark palette swaps each value for the one beside it; series entries carry no dark value, since both built-in themes share them. A comment at the top says what the file is, that it is exactly one set of colours (which is why `--palette` excludes `--theme`, FR-59), that every value must be `#RRGGBB`, that contrast becomes the author's responsibility (NFR-1), and the palette guide's address, `https://github.com/evensolberg/radargen/blob/main/docs/palette.md`, which explains each role, where it is drawn and the contrast each needs. **Given unchanged to `--palette`, the file is accepted with no warning, and a PNG, SVG or PDF rendered with it is byte-identical to the same render with the built-in light theme.** The HTML differs only as `--palette` makes every HTML differ: no theme toggle and no `prefers-color-scheme` handling (§6.6). `docs/product/palette.sample.css` holds exactly the bytes the flag writes (FR-81), and a test keeps the two equal. Otherwise it follows FR-99 exactly: UTF-8 with no byte-order mark and LF line endings (NFR-17), the same on every run, **never replacing a file** — an existing path, a dangling symlink included, is a hard error naming it, and nothing is written — written in place rather than renamed in (NFR-14), with a failed write removing it; the run prints nothing and exits 0. The flag is given alone; with a CSV, `-o`, `--create-template` or any other flag it is a usage error. |
| **FR-109** | **`--create-config <path>` writes a starter `--config` file to fill in, and renders nothing** (D-102). The file holds **every FR-91 key, in FR-91's order, each commented out** as `# key = value`, under a line saying what the key does and its default where it has one: `rings` as FR-13's default set, `theme`, `legend_columns`, `legend` and `table` as FR-56's, FR-100's, FR-103's and FR-106's defaults, and the keys without a fixed default as an example of their type (`width`'s line says its default is the drawing's own width). Among them are `# input = "radar.csv"`, `# edition = "today"` and `# subtitle = "Created {edition}"`, whose line says it needs `-e YYYY-MM-DD`, `-e today` or the `edition` key (FR-92); `theme`'s and `palette`'s lines say to use one, not both (FR-59). A comment at the top says what the file is, that `-c` reads it, that a flag on the command line overrides its key, and that a relative path in it is read from the file's folder (FR-91). **Read unchanged by `-c`, it is an empty file:** no key is set and a render is byte-identical to the same render without `-c`. **With every key line uncommented but one of `theme` and `palette`**, it has no key problem (FR-91). It says sector, never quadrant (D-101). `docs/product/config.sample.toml` holds exactly the bytes the flag writes, and the tests read it. Otherwise it follows FR-99 exactly: UTF-8 with no byte-order mark and LF line endings (NFR-17), the same on every run, **never replacing a file** — an existing path, a dangling symlink included, is a hard error naming it, and nothing is written — written in place rather than renamed in (NFR-14), with a failed write removing it; the run prints nothing and exits 0. The flag is given alone, and has no short form; with a CSV, `-o`, `-c`, another `--create-*` flag or any other flag it is a usage error. |

**Three outcomes, not a matrix of interacting flags:** neither flag → built-in light theme; `--theme dark` → built-in dark theme; `--palette <file>` → exactly what the file contains.

```sh
# A starter CSV to fill in, with every column and a few example rows (FR-99)
radargen --create-template radar.csv

# A palette to edit, starting from the built-in light colours (FR-101)
radargen --create-palette corporate-palette.css

# A config file to uncomment, with every key and its default (FR-109)
radargen --create-config radar.toml

# Today's default radar (the built-in in-house ring set), rendered to HTML
radargen entries.csv -o 2026-09-01.html

# A PNG sized for a slide deck, dark theme, labelled so it stands alone (FR-65)
radargen entries.csv -o 2026-09-01.png --theme dark --width 1920 \
  --title "Technology Radar" --subtitle "Edition 2026-09-01"

# The same edition as an editable vector file, for touch-up in Figma/Illustrator
radargen entries.csv -o 2026-09-01.svg --theme dark

# A different organisation's ring set (the ThoughtWorks convention), same tool.
# Spaces after the commas are fine — each entry is trimmed (FR-15)
radargen entries.csv -o edition.html --rings "Hold, Assess, Trial, Adopt"

# A CSV that doubles as a register: rows in these rings stay off the radar,
# without a warning, while any other undeclared ring still warns (FR-107)
radargen entries.csv -o edition.html --unplotted-rings "In Use, Rejected"

# A corporate colour scheme instead of the built-in palette (OQ-6).
# --theme is omitted deliberately: combining the two is a CLI error (FR-59)
radargen entries.csv -o edition.html --palette corporate-palette.css

# A radar's settings kept in a file beside the CSV, so every edition renders
# the same way (FR-91), with the date given once per run (FR-92). radar.toml:
#   output   = "editions/{edition}.html"
#   title    = "Technology Radar"
#   subtitle = "Edition {edition}"
#   rings    = ["Retire", "Adopt", "Trial", "Assess"]
radargen entries.csv -c radar.toml -e 2026-09-01

# The same from a scheduled job, dated by the local clock, which the run
# echoes so the render can be repeated with an explicit -e (D-58)
radargen entries.csv -c radar.toml -e today

# A job whose file names everything, the CSV and the date included (FR-91):
#   input    = "entries.csv"
#   output   = "editions/{edition}.html"
#   subtitle = "Edition {edition}"
#   edition  = "today"
radargen -c radar.toml

# A long legend flowed beside the radar for a slide (FR-100)
radargen entries.csv -o 2026-09-01.png --width 1920 --legend-columns 3
```

## 7) Non-Functional Requirements

| ID | Requirement |
| --- | --- |
| **NFR-1** | *(stated at §6.4)* WCAG 2.1 AA for the HTML output. |
| **NFR-2** | Single compiled binary. No runtime dependency install — no `node_modules`, no interpreter. |
| **NFR-3** | CLI invocation only (§6.6). |
| **NFR-4** | **Deterministic output, at two strengths.** For **HTML and SVG** — the text formats — the same CSV and flags produce a **byte-identical** file on every run and on every supported platform (NFR-13). For **PNG**, the guarantee is **pixel-identical**: the same decoded image every time, which may differ in bytes between encoder versions. No generation timestamp is embedded (D-19); the output version is, under NFR-12's rule (D-52). All ordering rules are total (FR-10, FR-34) and locale-independent. The split follows the guarantee's own purpose — output is diffable in git across editions — which is a property of text: a binary diff of two PNGs tells a reader nothing either way, so byte-identity there would constrain the build without serving the reason the guarantee exists (D-40). |
| **NFR-17** | **Determinism mechanics**, without which NFR-4 is an aspiration rather than a requirement. **Numbers:** every coordinate and computed value is rounded to **3 decimal places** at emission, half away from zero, formatted with a `.` decimal separator, no exponent, no thousands separator, and no locale involvement. Polar layout means `sin`/`cos`, which are **not** bit-identical across architectures; rounding at emission absorbs that difference, since the discrepancy is on the order of 1e-13 user units and the third decimal place is 1e-3. A value landing exactly on a rounding boundary could still split, which is possible and vanishingly unlikely — accepted rather than engineered around. **Line endings** are **LF** in every text output, on every platform including Windows. **Encoding** is UTF-8 with **no BOM** — the tool strips one on input (FR-2) and never writes one. **PNG:** no encoder settings are pinned and no bit-exactness is claimed, per NFR-4 and D-40. |
| **NFR-18** | **Released as open source under GPL-3.0-or-later**, published to crates.io as `radargen` (D-32) and to a public repository. The copyleft is the point: a modified or redistributed `radargen`, and any program linking it as a library, must ship its own source. **What the licence does not touch is the user's work** — running the tool imposes nothing, and the CSV, the rendered HTML, the PNG and the SVG are the author's alone, since they are the tool's *output* and not derived works of its code. Every crate in the dependency tree is permissive (MIT, Apache-2.0, BSD, Zlib) and so compatible in this direction; GPL-3.0 specifically, because Apache-2.0's patent clause is compatible with GPLv3 and not with GPLv2. The vendored font keeps its own OFL-1.1 terms with its licence text alongside (FR-85) — a font is aggregated with the binary, not a derived work of it. |
| **NFR-19** | **The shipped documentation states the range the tool renders well** — three to six sectors comfortably, seven or eight busy, beyond eight a sign the categories want revisiting (A-5) — in both the README and `--help`'s description of the input. FR-88 tells an author who has already run it; documentation tells one deciding what to put in the CSV, which is the cheaper moment to find out. The same documentation notes that **the tool grows a crowded radar itself, in steps and up to 2× (D-86)**, and that past that a crowded radar is made more readable by raising `--width` or by fewer items in the crowded band — output size is the author's lever beyond the growth, for D-48's reasons. It also states that the **innermost ring has the least room**, roughly a tenth of the outermost, so where the lifecycle allows a choice the sparsest stage belongs at the centre (FR-14) — and it **recommends reading `Adopt` as "we recommend this" rather than "everything we run"** (D-49), with a worked five-ring example — `--rings "Assess,Trial,Adopt,In Use,Retire"` — for organisations that want to track the estate alongside the advice (D-50). All of it is advice about content, which §4 keeps the tool from enforcing: the documentation says what renders well and the author decides. Neither is a constraint: §4 rules out enforcement, and both surfaces say what the tool does rather than what the author should do. |
| **NFR-20** | **Repeated warnings are summarised: one line per warning *class*, enumerating the distinct values that triggered it.** Not one line per row, and not one per value — a single unrecognised-ring line names every undeclared value it saw, each with its count and first line: `41 rows use a ring not in --rings (Adopt, Trial) — In Use (40, first line 4), Adpot (1, line 37); they are left off the page; to plot one, add it to --rings; to leave one off without this warning, add it to --unplotted-rings; --table all lists them, but also publishes every other column`, as an HTML page under the default `--table radar` gives it, with the ring set's source and where the rows went worded as FR-17 gives them. **Naming the values is the load-bearing part**, since it is what separates a deliberate `In Use` from a typo'd `Adpot`; a bare count would report that something went unplotted without saying that one of them was a mistake, which is the only actionable half. The same shape serves every class — unrecognised qualifiers, unrecognised statuses, unparsable dates, duplicate names — with values listed in order of first occurrence and **no cap**, because truncating the list would discard exactly the rare value most likely to be the error. A class with no value to enumerate, such as FR-7's empty description, FR-90's removed character or FR-104's missing glyph, lists its **positions** instead, also uncapped; FR-104's each carry their text, since the box is drawn where a removed character is not. A class with a single occurrence prints the ordinary per-row message instead. No flag turns grouping on or off, and `--quiet` was considered and rejected (D-51). D-51 also rejected a targeted `--ignore`; FR-107's `--unplotted-rings` **is** that targeted form, accepted in D-96: it removes FR-17's warning for the rings it names and nothing else, and every other value in the class still warns and groups as above. Under `--table all`, FR-78's table view still carries every unplotted row in full (FR-106). **Row-level hard errors group by the same rules** (NFR-16 tier two, D-62), **except that every line is listed**, with runs of consecutive lines written as ranges. FR-4's mismatched rows are one class that enumerates each distinct number of values with its row count and its lines — `201 rows have a different number of values from the header's 5 columns, 1 of them unnamed — 4 values (200 rows: lines 2–201), 6 values (1 row: line 202); …`. The line ends with the likely cause of each kind of mismatch present: for fewer values, a missing value or an unquoted line break; for more, a comma that needs the value in double quotes, or trailing empty cells when every value past the header's last column, in every such row, is empty under FR-76; and, first, when no data row matches the header, the header itself. FR-6's empty values are one class **per column** — `3 rows have no value in the ring column — lines 3, 5, 8; every row needs a ring, so fill them in or delete the rows`. FR-108's near misses are one class listing each header with the column it probably meant, in column order — `2 header names are not columns radargen knows — rationalle (did you mean rationale?), exit date (did you mean exit_date?)`. FR-4's duplicate headers are not grouped. |
| **NFR-5** | No network access is required to render **or** to view any output. The HTML file makes zero external requests: markup, CSS, JS, and fonts are inlined. |
| **NFR-6** | **All CSV-derived text is escaped for its output context, and the contexts are these five:** (1) **SVG/XML text node** — escape `&`, `<`, `>`; (2) **XML attribute value** — the same plus `"` and `'`; (3) **HTML attribute value** — the same, and never left unquoted; (4) **JavaScript string or JSON island** — JSON-encode, and additionally escape `<` so the sequence `</script>` cannot appear, since **`</script>` terminates the block regardless of HTML entity escaping** and is the one context where the obvious escaping is the wrong escaping; (5) **CSS custom-property value** (`--palette` only) — reject any value containing `;`, `}`, or `<` rather than escaping it, since no safe escaping exists for arbitrary text in a CSS value position. A value crossing into a second context is escaped for **both**, innermost first. Values are emitted as text or data, never as markup, script, or CSS. Characters the output cannot display never reach these contexts: FR-90 removes them on input. A `description` containing `<script>`, `&`, `"`, or `]]>` renders as literal characters. This is a security requirement, not a cosmetic one: output is emailed to clients and opened from disk, and the CSV is authored upstream of this tool. |
| **NFR-7** | **Fonts are pinned, not inherited.** The generator **loads a known font to measure text** (FR-46), the shared SVG declares that same stack explicitly rather than `system-ui`, and PNG rasterisation renders through it — so the same input renders identically regardless of what is installed on the rendering machine. Required for NFR-4 to hold for **every** format, not only PNG: wrap points are computed from real metrics and baked into the HTML, so an unpinned font changes the HTML bytes too. |
| **NFR-8** | **Exit codes.** `0` — render succeeded, with or without warnings. Non-zero — nothing was written: `2` for a usage error, clap's or one FR-91 checks as clap would (a key's type, name or range, `theme` and `palette` both in the file, no input or no output), and `1` for every other failure; a run with both kinds exits `2`. Warnings go to stderr; the rendered file goes to `-o`, never to stdout. |
| **NFR-9** | **Hard errors** (non-zero exit, no output): unreadable or non-UTF-8 input, or a NUL byte (FR-61), a CSV with no header or a non-comma separator (FR-1, FR-3), missing required column or both `sector` and `quadrant` (FR-5), empty `name`/`sector`/`ring` (FR-6), malformed row or duplicate header (FR-4), unrecognised or absent `-o` extension (FR-52), missing output parent directory or the output naming a file the run reads (FR-62), an empty/duplicated `--rings` or `--unplotted-rings` entry (FR-63, FR-107), an `--unplotted-rings` entry the ring set declares (FR-107), an incomplete `--palette` file, a gap in its series, a value that is not `#RRGGBB`, or a file outside FR-102's grammar (FR-86, FR-87, FR-102), an unknown or wrong-typed `--config` key or a value its flag would refuse, an empty `output` or `palette` key, a comma inside a `rings` or `unplotted_rings` entry, a path from the file that is not a regular file, or no CSV or no `-o` in either source (FR-91), an `{edition}` with no `--edition`, an unknown placeholder or an invalid `--edition` date (FR-92), `--width` out of range (FR-64), conflicting flags, or `theme` and `palette` both in the config file (FR-59), invocation with no arguments at all (FR-60), a `--create-template` or `--create-palette` path where something already is (FR-99, FR-101). **Warnings** (exit 0, output written): unrecognised `ring`, unless `--unplotted-rings` names it (FR-17, FR-107), unrecognised `qualifier` (FR-20), empty `description` (FR-7), unparsable `last_affirmed` or `exit_date` (FR-24, FR-25), unrecognised `status` (FR-26), duplicate `name` (FR-28), a value under an unnamed column (FR-93), a header that nearly names a known column (FR-108), a `link` that is not an `http` or `https` address (FR-96), a character the output cannot display (FR-90), a drawn text with characters the bundled font lacks, in a PNG, SVG or PDF only (FR-104), sector count above six (FR-88), a band holding more items than fit (FR-89), a narrow arrangement wider than 480 units (FR-95, HTML only), a wide arrangement that needs a screen past 1440 pixels (FR-105, HTML only), `--legend hidden` or `--table all` given on the command line for a PNG, SVG or PDF (FR-103, FR-106, D-100), a PNG drawn at FR-64's width ceiling because its document is wider, or drawn with its legend text too small to read (D-87), an `--edition` that no value uses (FR-92), the date `-e today` used, and a time zone it could not find (D-58). FR-90's and FR-108's warnings are raised as the CSV is read, before its rows are checked, so they also print beside a row-level hard error that fails the run. **Runtime failures (NFR-15) join the hard-error list**, and NFR-16 governs how many of either are reported per run. The dividing line is consistent: **structure is fatal, content is not** — the tool refuses what it cannot parse or place, and tolerates what it can render but doubts (D-5, D-12, D-27). |
| **NFR-10** | Every warning and error concerning a row names the **1-based CSV line number** and the column it concerns, if it concerns one (FR-4's mismatched number of values concerns the whole row). **That number is the physical line on which the record begins, counting the file's first line as line 1 — the header's line, unless blank records precede it (FR-3)** — not the record's ordinal. The two are equal until a quoted newline appears and diverge permanently afterwards, and the physical line is the one the author's editor can navigate to, which is the whole point of printing it (D-37). **Malformed quoting (NFR-15) is a file-level error, not a row's:** it names the line and column of the fault — where an unclosed quote opened, or where text follows a closing quote — which in a multi-line record can be later than the line the record begins on (D-59). A diagnostic spanning two records — FR-28's duplicate `name` — is reported **at the later line, naming the earlier**, so that NFR-11's ascending order stays well defined. Errors raised before or independently of row parsing — flag validation, output-path problems — name the argument or path instead. **A diagnostic about a configurable setting names its effective source**: the flag, the `--config` key with its file (FR-91), or the built-in default, as FR-17 does for the ring set and FR-90 for a flag value (D-64). A diagnostic concerning the render as a whole (FR-88) names the quantity it reports rather than a line. **A column named by position is given as its 1-based number and its spreadsheet letter**, `column 6 (F)`, since a spreadsheet labels columns by letter and the author would otherwise count across. **Messages say *value* and *column*, never *field*.** **Inside a quoted value, an invisible format character** — any of FR-76's Cf set except U+200C, U+200D and the tag characters, which are content in emoji and joined scripts — **is shown escaped**, `'Ad\u{200b}opt'`, because after FR-76's trim an interior one is the only way a visibly correct value fails to match (D-64). |
| **NFR-11** | Warnings are emitted in **ascending CSV line order**, and within a line in column order as declared by the header. **Row-level hard errors (NFR-16 tier two) follow the same order, whichever stage raised them.** Errors print as one block after the warnings, never interleaved with them. Within a line, an error naming no column position precedes those naming one: on a row's line, FR-4's mismatched number of values; on the header's line, FR-5's missing columns, which then precede FR-4's duplicate headers in column order. Under NFR-20's grouping, of warnings and errors alike, a class sorts by the **first** line it occurs on, and the values enumerated within its line sort the same way, which keeps the order total and independent of hash iteration. Diagnostics are part of the tool's observable output: golden-file tests (SC-8, SC-10) cannot assert against a set that arrives in hash-map order. |
| **NFR-12** | Releases follow [semver](https://semver.org). **Any change that alters rendered output for unchanged input is at least a minor bump**, so a re-rendered edition that diffs can be explained by a version rather than investigated. **HTML and SVG output carry the output version** — `major.minor`, never the patch — as `<meta name="generator" content="radargen 1.2">` in the HTML `<head>` and as the comment `<!-- radargen 1.2 -->` immediately before the SVG root element. The patch is left out because a patch release does not change output, so a full version would make every patch release diff every edition. The line therefore changes exactly when this requirement permits output to change, and a re-rendered edition's diff names the version that explains it. PNG carries none: NFR-4 holds it to pixel identity, where a byte diff explains nothing (D-52). |
| **NFR-13** | **Supported platforms: macOS, Windows, and Linux**, in that order of priority (SC-8 checks them against each other from 1.0.x, D-82) — macOS is where the tool is developed and where the originating radar is published, so it is the platform a regression is caught on first. All three are tier-1 for the byte-identical guarantee of NFR-4 on the formats that carry it. x86-64 and aarch64 both count on each. Named because SC-8 tests output "on a different machine" and §8 cites Windows support when declining qiwi/tech-radar, while nothing in the document said which platforms were in scope. |
| **NFR-14** | **Output is written atomically** (the `-o` file; `--create-template`, `--create-palette` and `--create-config` write in place, FR-99, FR-101, FR-109). The generator writes to a temporary file in the **same directory** as `-o`, flushes it, and renames it into place — so a failure part-way through leaves any previous file at that path **byte-unchanged**. Without this, NFR-8's promise that a non-zero exit means "nothing was written" is false for any failure after the first byte, and the destroyed file is a published edition (D-37). The same-directory requirement is what makes the rename atomic; a temp file elsewhere risks a cross-filesystem copy that is not. |
| **NFR-15** | **Runtime failures are hard errors** (non-zero exit, nothing written), alongside NFR-9's input and flag errors: output path not writable or naming a directory, no space left, rasterisation failure, font load or measurement failure, a `--palette` file that cannot be read, is not UTF-8, or is larger than 1 MiB (FR-102; a palette is a few hundred bytes, so the cap catches a wrong path, plan RG-018A R4), a CSV larger than 64 MiB (FR-61; a radar's CSV is a few kilobytes and the cap stops a wrong path, such as a device that never ends, from being read whole, rg-w5k), a `--config` file that cannot be read, is not UTF-8, is larger than 1 MiB or does not parse (FR-91), and a CSV whose quoting is malformed — an unterminated quote, or text after a closing quote, which FR-4's field-count and duplicate-header checks do not cover. NFR-9's taxonomy read as closed while naming no failure that arises from the machine rather than the input, so every one of these had no specified behaviour. |
| **NFR-16** | **Diagnostics are batched in three tiers, not reported one at a time.** (1) **File- and flag-level errors stop the run before any row is read** — an unreadable or non-UTF-8 input, an input larger than its cap (64 MiB for the CSV, 1 MiB for a `--palette` or `--config` file; NFR-15), or a NUL byte (FR-61), malformed quoting (NFR-15), a CSV with no header or a non-comma separator (FR-1, FR-3), a bad `-o` (FR-52, FR-62, including `-o` naming the input), a malformed `--rings` or `--unplotted-rings` (FR-63, FR-107), an out-of-range `--width` (FR-64), a malformed `--palette` file (FR-86, FR-87, FR-102), conflicting flags (FR-59), a bad `--config` key or value, and FR-92's placeholder and date errors (FR-91, FR-92): nothing can be parsed, so there is nothing to collect. Within a single flag, every violation is reported together, as FR-63 already requires. **Across `--config` and the command line (FR-91):** every key problem is collected first; the checks then run in their usual order, and one that fails on a value from the file records its errors and lets the later checks run, while one that fails on a command-line value stops the run with every error recorded so far, as the first failing flag always has. A file that cannot be read or parsed stops the run alone. (2) **Row-level hard errors are collected across the whole file** and reported together before exiting non-zero — every missing required column (FR-5) at once, every empty `name`/`sector`/`ring` (FR-6), every malformed row (FR-4). They are **grouped under NFR-20** (D-62). (3) **Warnings are collected and reported, then the render proceeds** and exits 0. FR-90's and FR-108's warnings are raised before tier (2)'s check, so when tier (2) fails the run they print before its errors. Fixing a 200-row export one error per run is the failure mode this exists to prevent; FR-5's singular "naming the column" and FR-63's "each violation" had pointed opposite ways, and this resolves them toward collecting (D-37). |

## 8) Relationship to existing tools

| Tool | Role | Boundary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Stock BYOR clone** (`/Volumes/SSD/Source/_Apps/build-your-own-radar/`, a local clone of ThoughtWorks' [build-your-own-radar](https://github.com/thoughtworks/build-your-own-radar)) | Current interim renderer | This tool replaces it once validated. No fork of BYOR itself; the clone stays where it is and is retired when SC-1 is met — **archived rather than deleted**, since publishing `radargen` (NFR-18) makes the thing it replaced worth keeping legible. |
| **This tool** | CSV → HTML/PNG/SVG/PDF radar renderer | Rendering only. No catalogue, no decision links, no policy rules. |
| **ArchMan TSR** (specced, not built) | Full relational standards catalogue + decision linking | Where the *authoritative* catalogue and ADR relationships eventually live. This tool may become, or feed, TSR's render layer later — not committed to now. |
| **[qiwi/tech-radar](https://github.com/qiwi/tech-radar)** (evaluated, declined) | Node-based generator; its `aurora` renderer supports 2–8 variable sectors/rings, CSV input, a cross-highlighting sidebar legend, light/dark theming, and auto-derived New/Moved-In/Moved-Out via snapshot diffing (`autoscope`) | Closest prior art. Declined — see below. |

This tool and TSR are not in tension: TSR is data-model-and-relationships; this is presentation. If TSR lands first, this tool's CSV contract is a plausible export format from it rather than redundant with it.

**Why qiwi/tech-radar was evaluated and declined.** It independently arrived at several of the same design choices as this PRD — variable sector count, CSV input, sidebar legend, light/dark toggle, auto-tracked movement between editions. That is genuine validation that the shape of this tool is right, and worth citing as prior art rather than dismissing. Two mismatches are against decisions made deliberately, not incidental gaps:

- **Output is a `dist/` directory** (per-scope pages, a snapshot timeline, optional assets) built for static-site hosting — not the single self-contained `.html` file G-6 requires.
- **Requires a Node.js ≥22 runtime**, and its README lists macOS/Linux only, no Windows — the opposite of NFR-2, which was chosen specifically to avoid this class of dependency.

Left unresolved because the decision didn't turn on them: whether it visually distinguishes Adopt — Default from Adopt — Conditional, and whether arbitrary extra CSV columns surface anywhere in its UI.

## 9) Decision log

*Decisions from the 2026-08-25 brainstorming session and subsequent reviews, recorded so they don't need re-litigating. Each explains a requirement in §6/§7 rather than restating it. "Added on this review" means the revision in §15 that first names the decision.*

| ID | Decision | Rationale |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **D-1** | **Renderer, not a catalogue.** | A prior Rust CLI + TOML catalogue design (2026-08-09) was rejected for duplicating ArchMan TSR's relational catalogue and decision-linking. Scoping narrowly to rendering avoids reopening that objection. |
| **D-2** | **Stack: Rust.** | Matches the existing cargo-based toolbelt; a single binary over a `node_modules` tree (NFR-2). |
| **D-3** | **CLI parsing: clap.** | Standard, well-maintained, library-first over hand-rolled parsing. Its `conflicts_with`/`ArgGroup` also gives FR-59 a standard usage error for free. *Extended in v0.39:* values from `--config` never pass through clap, so FR-91 re-applies FR-56, FR-59 and FR-64's checks to them after the merge, with clap's wording. |
| **D-4** | **Rings configurable via `--rings`, defaulting to the in-house four.** | Originally specced as hardcoded, on the reasoning that rings carry defined lifecycle semantics and quadrants don't. Revisited once the tool was reframed as a standalone entity. **The default is the lifecycle Assess → Trial → Adopt → Retire, declared edge-to-centre as `Assess,Trial,Adopt,Retire`** (D-60) rather than the more widely published ThoughtWorks set because an in-house radar governs an estate the publisher actually operates: it needs a terminal state with an owner and an exit date (*Retire*, paired with `exit_date`), where an industry-advice radar needs a cautionary one (*Hold*). Neither is more correct in general — they answer different questions — so the tool ships the in-house default and lets `--rings` express the other. *Noted in v0.29, refined in v0.30, and luck rather than design:* the default is also the **geometrically fortunate** one **for the job it is built for**. The innermost band holds about a tenth of the outermost, and an estate radar's `Adopt` — everything currently in service — accumulates monotonically; the default leaves it in the roomier second band from the centre and puts the transient `Retire` at the centre. The ThoughtWorks order inverts that, which is right for **its** semantics, where `Adopt` is a curated recommendation rather than an inventory, and wrong for an estate that borrows the ring names (FR-14). None of this entered the original choice, which was made on the semantics alone — the geometry followed the semantics rather than the other way round, which is the reassuring direction for it to follow. The semantics turned out not to require hardcoding: `qualifier` and `exit_date` were already keyed to whether a value is *populated*, never to a ring's literal name (FR-21, FR-25). What *does* need an explicit mechanism, and doesn't for quadrants, is **order** — a lifecycle progression can't be recovered by sorting names the way alphabetical works for quadrants. Hence an explicit ordered list rather than an inferred one. Omitting the flag reproduces the originating radar's behaviour exactly. |
| **D-5** | **Unknown ring values: tolerated, not rejected** (FR-17). | Teams track things the declared rings have no slot for — `Rejected`, a locally-named retirement state. The CSV and table should hold them even though the graphic can't plot them. A declined or locally-tracked technology is still a decision worth keeping: the catalogue, not the radar, is the authoritative record, so it can legitimately hold states the radar has no band to draw. Failing the render over one would make the graphic's limitations dictate what the catalogue is allowed to record. D-4 governs the *drawing*, not the *parsing*. *Revisited in v0.91 (D-95):* the CSV still holds such rows and the render still tolerates them, but the page's table lists them only under `--table all`; by default FR-17's warning is where they surface. *Since v0.92 (D-96),* a row whose ring `--unplotted-rings` names has no warning either, so under the default `--table radar` only the command records it. |
| **D-6** | **`qualifier`: asymmetric fallback, not either/or** (FR-20). | Silently rendering unrecognised input as Conditional would assert a specific, unearned claim — "acceptable only under stated circumstances." Falling back to solid on a row that should have said `default` costs nothing but a warning. *Narrowed in v0.36:* a **blank** qualifier no longer warns — only an unrecognised non-blank value does. FR-21 makes the rule ring-agnostic, so the blank warning could not be confined to the rings where the distinction means anything, and fired mostly on Trial and Assess rows where blank is correct; the PRD's own seven-row example raised it three times. Solid is already the safe fallback, so a blank makes no unearned claim, and FR-26 had reached "blank means nothing was claimed, no warning" for `status` (D-35). A forgotten `conditional` on an adopted item is indistinguishable from a correct blank without checking ring names, which FR-21 rules out — so it is a review-checklist item, not a diagnostic. **Renamed from `adopt_type`** because that name implied a ring literally called "Adopt" was required, when the mechanism never checked the ring's name at all. |
| **D-7** | **Sector order: case-insensitive alphabetical, no override** (FR-10). | Originally first-seen CSV order; changed because that isn't stable across editions unless the (still-unspecified) export step emits rows in the same order every time. Otherwise quadrants could visually rotate edition to edition with no underlying change — undermining a published, dated, side-by-side-comparable artefact. Alphabetical is deterministic from the names alone and needs no flag or config file. **Trade-off accepted knowingly:** a deliberately non-alphabetical order (ThoughtWorks' own Tools/Techniques/Platforms/Languages sequence) becomes inexpressible without renaming quadrants. It also means any existing radar whose category order isn't already alphabetical reorders once on migration — for the originating case, `Tool, Platform, Practice` becomes `Platform, Practice, Tool`. A one-time visible consequence, not a bug (see R-4). Rings get the opposite treatment for a reason, not inconsistently: quadrant order is genuinely meaningless, so resolving it automatically costs nothing; ring order is load-bearing and cannot be resolved automatically at all. *Corrected in v0.37:* "genuinely meaningless" overstated it — this entry itself concedes ThoughtWorks' sequence becomes inexpressible, and R-4 books a visible reorder against the originating radar. The reasoning above supports *deriving* the order from the names; it never supported refusing an override, and an explicit `--quadrants` would be exactly as stable, being the mechanism D-4 adopts for rings. The real grounds for shipping without one are smaller and sufficient: no configuration, no new validation or failure modes (what a listed-but-absent or unlisted quadrant does), and **adding the flag later is additive** — a minor bump under NFR-12, with the alphabetical default unchanged for anyone who omits it. So the override is deferred, not rejected (OQ-17). *Since v0.103, quadrants are sectors (D-101), and the deferred flag would be `--sectors`.* |
| **D-8** | **Near-duplicates not caught** (FR-11). | `Tool` vs `Tools` is a CSV-authoring mistake at a scale where an unexpected extra sector is obvious in the output and fixable at source. Fuzzy matching or a distinct-count warning would solve a problem nobody has hit. |
| **D-9** | **`status` is manual, author-supplied per edition** (FR-26). | Matches BYOR's own convention. Auto-computing it by diffing editions is deferred with no pull-forward request against it. |
| **D-10** | **Case-insensitive matching throughout, with trimming** (FR-76). *Scope widened by D-34 — this decision originally named only `quadrant` and `ring`.* | `Tool`/`tool`, `Adopt`/`ADOPT`, `New`/`new`, `Default`/`default` are the same value. Casing or a stray trailing space shouldn't split one quadrant into two or fail a match a human reads as obviously fine. |
| **D-11** | **BOM stripped on read** (FR-2). | *Added on this review.* Excel emits UTF-8 with BOM by default. Without stripping, the first header parses as `﻿name` and FR-5 fails on a file that looks correct in every editor — a confusing failure with a one-line fix. |
| **D-12** | **Empty `description` warns rather than failing** (FR-7); empty `name`/`sector`/`ring` is fatal (FR-6). | *Added on this review, resolving an ambiguity in "every row must have all four."* The first three are structural — without them an item cannot be identified or placed. A description is content: the item still plots correctly, so blocking a whole edition on one missing write-up is disproportionate. |
| **D-13** | **Unparsable dates are rendered verbatim with a warning** (FR-24). | *Added on this review.* Consistent with D-5's tolerance. Nothing computes on these dates in this version, so a malformed one costs only its own display; rejecting the edition over it would be the governance behaviour §4 rules out. |
| **D-14** | **Legend authored in SVG, not HTML/CSS** (FR-44). | Prototyping the legend as a flexbox div was faster; the real tool doesn't inherit that constraint. One SVG document is what makes PNG export cheap instead of requiring a browser engine to rasterise an HTML layout. |
| **D-15** | **`<foreignObject>` ruled out** (FR-47). | It would let the legend stay real HTML — flexbox, native scroll, free text wrap — dodging the wrapping and scroll costs. But `resvg` and other lightweight rasterisers don't support it, because rendering embedded HTML needs exactly the browser-engine dependency choosing `resvg` avoids. It would fix the HTML output and break PNG export, defeating the point of unifying the two. |
| **D-16** | **Two arrangements, one breakpoint: legend beside the radar when there is room, below it when there isn't** (FR-51, FR-68–FR-71). | *Corrected on this review, having been wrong in v0.3 and v0.4.* Those versions claimed that "because radar and legend are one SVG document, side-by-side is not achievable" — and that is simply false. Placing the legend beside the radar in one document is a different translate, the same operation already used to place it below. The real constraint is narrower: **one document means one arrangement, fixed at generation time**, because the generator computes the `viewBox` and `viewBox` is not a CSS property, so a media query can re-arrange nothing — it can only choose between things already drawn. Both costs were then weighed properly for the first time. A side-by-side-only document does not stack on a phone, it *scales*: a ~1200-unit document in a 380px viewport renders at about a third size, and the legend becomes unreadable. A stacked-only document makes a poor slide, which is what G-7 exists for, and R-3's tall-PNG problem is the same complaint from the other end. Emitting both and letting the HTML pick costs roughly twice the graphic's markup in that one format, and `display: none` handles the accessibility duplication for free (FR-69). Rejected alongside: having the HTML re-run layout in JavaScript at view time, which would solve it elegantly and put a second layout implementation in a second language — the divergence D-14 and FR-44 exist to prevent. |
| **D-17** | **JPEG: decided against** (§6.5). | PNG only. Lossy DCT compression degrades small text and flat colour visibly, with no file-size win on non-photographic content. Anyone who needs JPEG converts the PNG rather than the tool shipping a second encoder. |
| **D-18** | **Single CSV in, single output file out** (§6.5). | No batch or glob input, unlike qiwi's multi-scope model. Rendering several radars means running the tool several times. Revisit only if that friction becomes real. |
| **D-19** | **No generation timestamp embedded in output** (NFR-4). | *Resolved on this review* — earlier drafts hedged with "aside from a generation timestamp, if included at all," which left NFR-4's byte-identical guarantee conditional on an undecided detail.
