Editions are already dated by filename and by the commit that adds them, so an embedded timestamp adds nothing and would break every diff. If provenance is wanted later it becomes an explicit opt-in flag. *Revised in v0.37:* NFR-12 had extended this to a version string "exactly as a timestamp would", which is a false analogy — a timestamp changes on every run, a version only on a release. The output version is now embedded (D-52); the timestamp stays out. |
| **D-20** | **Location: `/Volumes/SSD/Source/Rust/radargen`**, its own repo. | *Corrected in v0.3, renamed in v0.10 to match D-32.* Earlier drafts said "alongside BYOR, under `/Volumes/SSD/Source/_Apps/`", which conflicted with where this PRD actually lives. The BYOR clone stays where it is and is retired when SC-1 is met; it is not a parent directory for its replacement — **that** is the decision here, and the directory's leaf name is incidental to it. The rename from `TechRadar` was done while the repo held only documentation, on the reasoning that a rename never gets cheaper than before the first `Cargo.toml`. |
| **D-21** | **CSV richness is surfaced, not just passed through** (FR-22–FR-26). | The concrete, demonstrable improvement over BYOR: showing domain/rationale/last-affirmed/exit data that BYOR silently drops. |
| **D-22** | **PNG and SVG export are both in scope, not deferred** (FR-54, FR-55). | PNG via `resvg` rasterising the shared document. SVG is nearly free given PNG already requires that document to exist in valid standalone form — this *is* that document, unrasterised — and independently useful for vector editing and print. |
| **D-23** | **Output escaping is a stated requirement** (NFR-6). | *Added on this review.* Previously unstated in any form. The stated delivery path is "email an HTML file to a client," and the CSV is authored upstream — that combination makes unescaped interpolation a real injection vector, not a theoretical one. Cheap to get right from the first line of the generator; expensive to retrofit across three output paths. |
| **D-24** | **Edition identity comes from `--title`/`--subtitle`, not from the filename or the clock** (FR-65). | *Added on this review.* UC-1 publishes dated editions and UC-3 pastes the PNG into a slide deck — where the filename does not travel with the image, leaving an unlabelled diagram of numbered dots. Deriving a subtitle from the `-o` basename was considered and rejected: it makes the *content* of the output depend on *where* it is written, so rendering to a temp path and moving the file would change the graphic. Injecting today's date was rejected outright — it breaks NFR-4 for the same reason D-19 rejects a timestamp. Explicit flags leave the caller in charge and the output a pure function of its inputs. *Extended in v0.39:* FR-92's `{edition}` lets the date be written once per run, but it still comes from the author's `--edition`, never from the clock, so this decision stands. *Amended in v0.102, at the maintainer's decision:* an explicit `-e today` takes the local date (D-58). It is opt-in and echoes the date it used, so the output stays a pure function of inputs the author can see and repeat; without it the clock is never read. |
| **D-25** | **The encoding key lives in the shared SVG, not the HTML chrome** (FR-66). | *Added on this review.* FR-20 and FR-40 define encodings — hollow for conditional, rings for movement — that nothing then explains. In HTML the detail panel covers for that; in a PNG the reader has no way to learn what a half-ring means, which undercuts G-7's whole claim that the image is shareable. Rendering the key only when the dataset uses an encoding was considered and rejected: it makes the graphic's height vary in one more dataset-dependent way, and an absent key row is itself information ("no conditional adoptions this edition") that a reader cannot distinguish from an unexplained diagram. |
| **D-26** | **Per-item prose is an HTML-only affordance; SC-7 is scoped to the shared graphic** (§6.3). | *Resolved on this review.* SC-7 as written required "no information present in one format but silently missing from another," which the §6.3 scope boundary directly contradicted — the detail panel is HTML-only, so PNG and SVG never carried `description`, `rationale`, or the dates. Rendering that prose into the shared document was the alternative: it would honour the old wording, and it would make a forty-item PNG mostly body text while loading R-1, the riskiest part of the build, with the least valuable work. The formats divide by job, so the criterion divides with them: parity is asserted over the *visual*, and the split is stated in §6.3 rather than left for an implementer to discover. |
| **D-27** | **Input is strictly UTF-8; encoding and I/O failures are fatal** (FR-61). | *Added on this review.* Sits deliberately opposite D-5's tolerance, on the same structure-versus-content line NFR-9 draws: a file that will not decode has no rows to be lenient about. No `--encoding` flag and no transcoding — guessing a code page silently corrupts names in ways nobody notices until a client reads them. The mitigation is the message, not a fallback: name the line and say "re-save as UTF-8", because the likely cause is an Excel export and a raw decoder error tells that author nothing. Same reasoning as D-11, one layer lower. |
| **D-28** | **No subcommand; the CSV is a positional argument** (§6.6). | *Added on this review.* The examples used `radar render entries.csv` while the flag table defined no subcommand — and, separately, never defined the input argument at all. Resolved toward the simpler shape: one input, one output, nothing else the tool does, so there is no verb worth naming. Reserving `render` against a hypothetical future `diff` (D-9) would tax every invocation now for a command that may never exist; adding a subcommand later is a breaking change made deliberately, which NFR-12 already accounts for. |
| **D-29** | **Sector label casing is row-order-independent** (FR-9). | *Corrected on this review.* D-7 removed CSV row order from quadrant *ordering* because the export step is not guaranteed to emit rows stably — then FR-9 reintroduced exactly that dependency for the displayed *label*, so a reordered export could flip `Tool` to `tool` between editions with nothing underlying having changed. Picking the ordinally first variant is stable from the names alone. Which casing wins is arbitrary; that it cannot change without the data changing is the point. |
| **D-30** | **Released in two slices: v1.0 replaces BYOR, v1.1 adds the static formats** (§14.1). | *Added on this review.* §14 costed 10–15 days as one indivisible lump, with no point at which BYOR could be retired. SC-1 and SC-2 — the criteria that actually justify the project — are met by HTML alone; PNG and SVG serve UC-3, which is real but not blocking. Slicing by risk instead (spike → shared SVG → all three formats) was considered, and the text-metrics spike stays first either way, since R-1 gates both slices. The split is a release boundary, not a build order: D-22's point that SVG is nearly free once PNG works still holds, which is why the two ship together rather than as separate slices. |
| **D-31** | **The narrow arrangement scales the radar, not the document** (FR-70). | *Added on this review, found by building it.* Stacking the full-width document was the obvious reading of "legend below the radar", and prototyping it showed the obvious reading fails: the radar's quadrant labels force roughly 700 user units of width, so a phone scales the whole document to about half and 12.5px legend text lands near 6px. SC-6 had been asserting legibility that neither the v0.3 design nor the prototype actually delivered. Text is the thing that must stay readable — a legend you cannot read makes the radar beside it useless — so the narrow document is authored at a text-appropriate width and the radar is scaled to fit inside it, with its own labels counter-scaled. The cost is a small radar on a phone: marker numbers are legible but the graphic is a thumbnail, and the legend and table view are what a phone reader actually uses. *Amended by D-69:* marker numbers are held at 7/9 of their size, since scaling them with the radar left them illegible. *Amended by D-71:* when the radar cannot fit the narrow column even at 0.3, the narrow document widens to hold it. |
| **D-32** | **Named `radargen` — crate, repository, and command alike** (§6.6). | *Resolves OQ-1, which is retired from §11.* Checked available on crates.io, as were `radarkit`, `blipmap` and `sweepline`; `azimuth`, `blip`, `bearing`, `astrolabe`, `quadrant`, `ringmap` and `ringside` were all taken, and `orrery` is already a diagram language — close enough in domain to be a genuine confusion risk rather than a coincidence. Literal beats evocative here: the name is read at install time and in a build file, not admired. **Nothing containing "techradar"**, deliberately — techradar.com is a large consumer publication, and "Technology Radar" is ThoughtWorks' name for the artefact this tool replaces, which §8 spends a section distinguishing itself from. A single name for crate, repo and binary was chosen over a shorter `radar` command: `radargen` says what it does at the call site, and a bare `radar` on `PATH` is the sort of generic name that collides with something eventually. The working directory was renamed to match in v0.10, so crate, repo, directory and command are now one name (D-20). |
| **D-33** | **Item numbers follow the legend's reading order, not the declared ring order** (FR-34). | *Added on this review.* FR-34 numbered by the declared centre-to-edge order while FR-35 presents rings reversed, so within each quadrant the legend's numbers **descended** as the reader scanned down it — the outer ring's 12, 13, 14 above the innermost ring's 1, 2. Neither requirement is wrong read on its own, which is why the contradiction survived four revisions. Reversing FR-35 instead was rejected: presenting the lifecycle earliest-stage-first is a deliberate reading order that FR-35 argues for, whereas the plot has no opinion about which direction its numbers run — a marker is identified *by* its number, not by that number's place in a sequence. So presentation order wins and numbering follows it. *Since v0.42* `--rings` is declared edge-to-centre (D-60), so the declared and presented orders coincide and the rule needs no reversal; it stands as written. `mockup.html` was corrected to match, and its comparator switched off `localeCompare` at the same time — locale-sensitive by definition, so it could not have delivered the locale-independence FR-10 and FR-34 both ask for. |
| **D-34** | **One normalisation rule for every matched value** (FR-76). | *Added on this review.* D-10 settled case-insensitive matching with trimming, but only `quadrant` and `ring` said so in their own requirements; `qualifier` and `status` said "case-insensitively" and nothing about whitespace. One trailing space out of one Excel export would therefore group correctly on one column and raise `unrecognised qualifier value ' conditional'` on another — a warning naming a value that looks correct, which is close to undiagnosable from the message alone. Stating the rule once and pointing every column at it beats restating it per column and drifting again. |
| **D-35** | **A blank `status` means absent, not `No Change`** (FR-26). | *Added on this review, resolving a three-way contradiction.* FR-26 sent any unrecognised value — blank included — to `No Change` and said the panel states it explicitly; FR-29 said an empty optional column is omitted from the panel entirely; FR-40 listed "No Change **or absent**" as distinct inputs to the same plain marker. The document's own example CSV leaves `status` blank on three of seven rows, so this fired on the fixture. Resolved by FR-20's precedent rather than by fresh reasoning: FR-20 already splits blank from unrecognised-non-blank for `qualifier`, warns differently on each, and treats blank as "nothing was claimed". `status` gets the same shape, which also makes FR-29 true without exception. The alternative — keeping the `No Change` fallback and exempting `status` from FR-29 — would assert on the author's behalf that an item was reviewed and found unchanged, which is exactly the unearned claim D-6 refuses to make for `qualifier`. *Corrected in v0.36:* this precedent was not mirrored exactly — `qualifier` warned on blank where `status` does not. FR-20 now matches FR-26, so the mirror holds. |
| **D-36** | **The prototype's geometry is ratified, not re-derived** (FR-33, FR-77). | *Added on this review, closing OQ-8.* OQ-8 was recorded as six valid layouts with nothing to choose between them. That was wrong about the state of the evidence: `mockup.html` already picks one and §10 credits it with validating the result at 3, 4 and 5 quadrants. Its choices are transcribed rather than re-opened — `-90°` start with the FR-10 order running clockwise, `(j+1)/(n+1)` angular distribution, an 8% proportional inset, and radial alternation by index parity. Each is arbitrary in isolation and none is arbitrary now: they are the ones that were looked at. The parity rule is named explicitly because "varied" invited a PRNG, which NFR-4 forbids outright. Re-deriving these from scratch would have produced a different radar for no stated gain and discarded the one artefact that had actually been eyeballed. |
| **D-37** | **Diagnostics: collect where collecting helps, and print the line the editor can find** (NFR-10, NFR-14, NFR-16). | *Added on this review, closing OQ-10.* Three sub-questions, resolved on one principle — the diagnostic exists to get the author to the fix. Fixing a 200-row export one error per run is the failure this prevents, so row-level errors collect; file- and flag-level ones cannot, since nothing parses. "1-based line number" resolves to the **physical** line rather than the record ordinal because the physical line is what an editor's Go To Line reaches, and the two diverge permanently after the first quoted newline. Atomic write (NFR-14) is not a new promise but an existing one made true: NFR-8 already said a non-zero exit means nothing was written, and a truncate-and-stream implementation makes that false for every failure after the first byte — with a published edition as the casualty. |
| **D-38** | **Table rows are activatable, and the table carries every column** (FR-78). | *Added on this review, closing OQ-11.* FR-38 reaches the detail panel from a marker or a legend row; FR-18 gives an unplotted row neither. So the prose of a row the graphic cannot plot — the `Rejected` case D-5 exists to keep — was reachable from nowhere, in a document whose §4 insists the catalogue may legitimately hold states the radar cannot draw. Making table rows activatable is the cheapest repair and costs one `role` attribute; the alternative, rendering unplotted rows' prose inline in the table, duplicates the panel. *Revisited in v0.61 (D-80):* the table now carries the prose and the panel reads it from there, so it is in the page once. *Superseded in v0.46 in mechanism, not in purpose:* the row now activates through a native button in its name cell (D-66). Showing every present column follows FR-27's promise that a team may carry any extra column it finds useful — a column the tool ignores everywhere is one the tool silently discarded. *Revisited in v0.91 (D-95):* every row and column is now `--table all`'s, and the default table publishes only what the radar draws, so an unplotted row's panel is reachable under `--table all`; the extra column is still the author's to publish, by one flag, rather than discarded. |
| **D-39** | **Greedy wrapping, and a mid-token break for the token that does not fit** (FR-79). | *Added on this review, closing OQ-14.* SC-7 asserts that HTML and PNG wrap at identical points, which makes the algorithm part of the contract rather than an implementation detail — so it has to be named, and the one to name is the one that is trivially reproducible. Greedy first-fit is that; balanced wrapping looks better and requires both implementations to agree on a cost function. The overlong single token is the case FR-46's "wrapped, **not** truncated" appeared to forbid resolving: it cannot be truncated, and letting it overflow pushes it outside the `viewBox`, so it breaks mid-token — ugly, bounded, and the only option left standing. |
| **D-40** | **Byte-identity for the text formats; pixel-identity for PNG** (NFR-4, NFR-17, SC-8). | *Added on this review, closing OQ-9's substantive half.* NFR-4 promised byte-identical output for every format, which for PNG means pinning the deflate implementation, compression level, per-scanline filter strategy and chunk set — since identical pixels encode to different bytes under any of those. That collides with NFR-12: a routine dependency bump would change output bytes with no version bump to explain the diff, so the policy would be violated by a `cargo update` nobody thought twice about. Holding it would mean vendoring an encoder and auditing every bump forever. The alternative was weighed against the guarantee's own stated purpose — "so output is diffable in git across editions" — and that purpose is textual. A binary diff of two PNGs is unreadable whether or not the bytes match, so byte-identity there was buying a constraint that served nothing. PNG therefore promises the same *image*, verified by decoding rather than hashing (SC-8). The cost is real and accepted: a committed PNG can show a spurious git diff after an encoder bump even though the picture is unchanged. That is a cosmetic annoyance in a folder of dated editions, where the previous edition is not re-rendered anyway. NFR-17 collects what the text formats *do* need — fixed rounding, a fixed decimal separator, LF endings, no BOM — none of which was stated anywhere while NFR-4 depended on all of it. |
| **D-41** | **Eight series colours, ordered most-separable-first, shared across both themes** (FR-81–FR-83). | *Added on this review, closing OQ-12.* Five was the first proposal and was measurably clean (ΔE 46.6 normal / 11.8 deficient), but FR-67 cycles, and cycling at N+1 quadrants puts one colour on two **angularly adjacent** sectors — which reads as a defect even though FR-39 makes it harmless. Eight pushes that to nine quadrants, past the point where the categories themselves want rethinking. The ordering matters more than the count: FR-67 assigns by index, so palette order *is* usage frequency, and front-loading the most separable colours means the three- and four-quadrant cases — the originating radar and the ThoughtWorks convention — get the best of the set. Two alternatives were measured and rejected. **Per-theme series values** widen the usable luminance band and reach ΔE 33.6 to normal vision, but re-admit the violets and browns that collide with vermillion and olive, collapsing the deficient-vision floor to 3.5; they would also cost the single-set shape that makes the shipped file usable as `--palette` unchanged. **Optimising purely for the deficient-vision floor** reaches 13.5 but yields three browns and two magentas — robust to measure, poor to look at. The accepted cost is that the floor drops from 11.8 to 8.2 at the sixth entry, where green has to stay bright enough to clear olive under deuteranopia; FR-83 states that bound rather than hiding it. |
| **D-42** | **Measure with `rustybuzz`, not with raw `hmtx` advances** (FR-84). | *Added by the text-metrics spike, which R-1 and §14.2 put before any layout work.* R-1 said "real font metrics via `fontdb`/`ttf-parser` are needed", and that turned out to be an incomplete instruction rather than a wrong one: those give per-glyph advances, and a string's width is not their sum. `resvg` shapes with `rustybuzz`, which applies GPOS kerning; the naive sum does not. Measured on Libertinus Sans at 12.5px, `Terraform` came out 0.79px wide and `WWWWWWWW` 2.1px narrow — 2.2% — while `Pulumi` and `Internationalisation` agreed exactly, because they carry no kerning pairs. That combination is the dangerous one: no constant factor corrects it, and a spot check on the wrong string finds nothing. Measuring through the same shaper makes the agreement structural, which is what R-1's response asked for; after the change the worst divergence across the corpus is 5e-6 px. The alternative — reimplementing GPOS against `ttf-parser` — would be a second shaper to keep in step with the one that actually draws, which is the divergence D-14 and FR-44 exist to prevent, one layer down. `rustybuzz` is already inside `resvg`'s dependency tree, so it costs no new supply-chain surface. *Annotated in v0.46:* `resvg` 0.48 shapes with `harfrust`, so the shaper is now `harfrust` (D-67). |
| **D-43** | **Inter, two static instances, subset to the scripts it draws, vendored** (FR-85). | *Added on this review, closing R-7's licensing half.* The font had to satisfy four things at once: redistributable *and* embeddable (NFR-5 inlines it into every HTML file, NFR-7 ships it in the binary), legible at 12.5px because FR-70 scales the radar rather than the text, deterministic (NFR-4), and small enough to inline into an artefact that gets committed per edition and emailed to clients. OFL-1.1 settles the first. Inter is designed for the second — the narrow arrangement's legend is exactly the case it was drawn for. **Static instances** answer the third: a variable font resolves metrics from axis coordinates, and a browser and `resvg` disagreeing about an axis default would reopen the divergence FR-84 just closed, one layer up. **Two weights and a script subset** — Latin at first, widened by the amendments below — answer the fourth, and the subset is vendored rather than generated because a build step is another thing that can differ per machine. *Amended in v0.40:* the subset is Latin **and Latin Extended**. Measured, `latin` alone stopped at Latin-1 and left Latin-script names such as Dvořák, Łukasiewicz or a Turkish İ as tofu in the PNG; adding `latin-ext` costs about 55 kB of WOFF2 (about 73 kB once base64-inlined) per HTML edition, accepted because the alternative was a silent SC-7 failure on names A-4 claims to cover. Source Sans 3 was the runner-up and remains the swap if Inter's voice is wrong for client-facing work; the mechanism is font-independent, so changing it costs a re-measure, not a redesign. *Amended in v0.46:* Vietnamese, Greek and Cyrillic join the subset (D-68). |
| **D-44** | **`--palette` ships, with completeness required and series count free** (FR-86, FR-87, closing OQ-6). | *Added on this review.* OQ-6 asked whether the flag was worth building when HTML colours can already be hand-edited and an SVG recoloured in a design tool. It ships on the argument OQ-6 itself identified: **a rasterised PNG cannot be re-themed at all**, which is the case a text editor does not answer, and client-facing editions are the ones most likely to want a house palette. *A second argument was drafted and withdrawn* — that a custom palette's wider luminance band makes it the answer to more than eight quadrants. It is technically true and was the wrong thing to build a justification on: A-5 holds that beyond eight the taxonomy is what wants fixing, and colour is not even what fails first there (a 12-quadrant radar has 30° sectors and twelve labels around one circle, and OQ-14 records that labels have no collision rule at all). A free series count remains the right *behaviour*, because capping the list would hard-error on a legitimate file for no benefit — but it is a consequence of the format, not a capability the document recommends reaching for. Completeness (FR-86) goes the other way deliberately: every *role* is required, because filling a missing role from the built-in palette would mix two palettes silently and produce precisely the contrast failure NFR-1 disclaims on custom palettes — the author would see a wrong colour with nothing indicating where it came from. Requiring all eight roles while leaving the series list open is not an inconsistency: the roles are a fixed vocabulary the renderer must resolve, and the series are a list whose length is the author's choice. |
| **D-45** | **GPL-3.0-or-later, published** (NFR-18, closing OQ-7). | *Added on this review.* Three options were weighed against what the author actually wanted, which was that anyone building on this has to open their own work in turn — a copyleft requirement, not the permissive `MIT OR Apache-2.0` the Rust ecosystem defaults to. Permissive was rejected on that ground alone: it would let a vendor wrap the renderer and sell it closed, which is the outcome the author is licensing *against*. MPL-2.0 was the middle option — file-level copyleft, almost never blocked by corporate policy — and was rejected as weaker than the stated intent: it permits linking into proprietary software. GPL-3.0's usual objection, that copyleft is invasive, is mostly inert for this shape of tool: **`radargen` is a CLI binary, so running it obliges nobody**, and a rendered radar is output rather than a derived work. The obligation lands only on someone redistributing a modified tool or linking the crate — exactly the boundary intended. The accepted cost is real and worth stating: **blanket enterprise GPL bans exist**, and a client whose procurement carries one may be unable to run the tool themselves even though merely running it imposes nothing on them. The mitigation is that the *output* is unencumbered — the published HTML and PNG travel to a client with no licence attached at all, which is how the radar actually reaches its readers (UC-2, UC-3). Verified before deciding: all 63 crates resolved in the dependency tree are MIT, Apache-2.0, BSD or Zlib, so nothing downstream conflicts. |
| **D-46** | **Warn above six sectors; document the range** (FR-88, NFR-19, A-5). | *Added on this review.* A-5 had put the expected ceiling at eight, which quietly borrowed FR-81's **palette** number for a **legibility** claim — two different quantities. Eight is where colours run out; the geometry is uncomfortable well before that, since a sector narrows from 120° at three quadrants to 45° at eight while quadrant labels crowd one circle under no collision rule (OQ-14). So the palette's eight entries are re-cast as headroom — the busy end of the range still gets distinct colours — and the expectation drops to three-to-six. Whether to *warn* was the harder call, because §4 rules out governance. Resolved on what the warning is about: FR-88 reports that **this tool** will not render this well, which describes the output, where §4's prohibition is on judging the *input* ("exactly one Default per domain"). The distinction is written into §4 so it is not re-argued. Two things keep it honest. It fires **once per render**, not per row, so it does not add to the per-row volume crumb `rg-m59` already flags. And the threshold is defensible — a warning nobody can justify is one everybody learns to ignore. *Corrected in v0.26:* this decision originally added that items-per-band could not be warned about, its limit "moving with band radius and name length, so there is no number to stand behind". Half of that is wrong. The limit does vary per band, but with **radius and sector angle**, both known at layout time, and not with name length at all — a marker carries a number, not a name. It is computable, and FR-89 now computes it. |
| **D-47** | **Ring bands are never sized by their content** (FR-32). | *Added on this review, after the idea was raised and measured. D-86 grows the whole radar, every band alike, so this stands; its second reason, that boundaries move between editions, now applies to D-86 in quarter steps, a cost D-86 accepts.* The proposal is intuitive: let a crowded ring take radial width from a sparse one, so a quadrant holding six items in Adopt and one in Assess spends its radius where the items are. **It was rejected for two independent reasons, and the first is that it does not work.** Marker capacity in a band is set by the **arc length at the two radial tracks** of FR-33 — that is, by their *radii* — and a band can only widen by pushing its inner edge inward, where arc is scarcer. The two effects very nearly cancel: the tracks sit at `inner + 0.38w` and `inner + 0.68w`, so capacity tracks `2·inner + 1.06w`, which for a ring 2 widened from 65px to 131px comes to **198.9 either way**. Measured at eight quadrants, an adopt-heavy quadrant given a 131px Adopt band held **four** markers — exactly what the 65px band held, against the twelve it needed. **Capacity is angular, not radial**, and no redistribution of radius manufactures angular room: the same band at three quadrants holds roughly seventeen, at eight roughly seven. If a ring is overfull the answers are fewer quadrants or fewer items, not a fatter band. **Equal-area bands were measured too**, as a data-independent alternative, and are worse overall — `[2,7,7,8]` against equal width's `[2,4,9,13]` at eight quadrants, trading the outer rings' capacity for the inner one's. The second reason stands on its own even where the first does not: content-sized bands are **data-dependent**, so ring boundaries move between editions and an item that did not move renders at a different radius — precisely what D-7 refused for quadrant ordering, on an artefact §1 and UC-1 describe as dated, immutable and side-by-side comparable. *Recorded because the idea is a natural one that will occur again.* An earlier pass at these numbers was wrong — it assumed every marker carried FR-40's status ring and added an invented gap — and was corrected against `mockup.html` before this decision was written; the figures here use a plain `r=10` marker (crumb `rg-cbx`). |
| **D-48** | **An overfull band overlaps; the canvas is not grown to fit it** (FR-33, FR-89). | *Superseded in part by D-86, which grows the canvas in steps and within a screen-width bound, and answers both reasons below; the rest stands.* *Added on this review, the question being what happens after the warning.* Four responses were considered. **Dropping or capping** items is out: the CSV asserts an item and FR-18 already reserves "not drawn" for a row the graphic *cannot* place, so silently hiding one it merely finds crowded would make the graphic's comfort dictate the record. **Shrinking markers** buys little and costs the number legibility FR-36 relies on. **Growing the canvas** is the interesting one, because unlike D-47's ring resizing it genuinely adds angular room — arc is `r × θ`, so a larger radius lengthens every arc, and capacity is linear in it: doubling the radar from 260 to 520 units takes an eight-quadrant quadrant from 28 markers to 58. It is still rejected, for two reasons. **At a fixed output size it is conservation, not a gain** — in a 1920px-wide PNG that same doubling shrinks the marker from 29px to 14px, so the reader trades marker size for marker count and gains no legibility. It pays off only when the *output* grows too, which `--width` already controls and the author is better placed to judge (NFR-19 documents it). **This argument is about growing the canvas with the marker fixed in *user* units**, where size and count trade against each other; it does not reach the separate proposal of fixing the marker in *output* units, which breaks that trade and is open as OQ-16. And **automatic growth would make the document's geometry data-dependent**, so an edition that gained one item would redraw every other marker at a different radius — the same defect D-47 rejects for ring widths, and D-7 for quadrant order, on an artefact meant to be compared side by side. So the graphic overlaps, and the fallbacks that already exist carry the load: every item keeps its legend row (FR-35) and its table row (FR-41, FR-78), the tooltip names it (FR-36), and cross-highlighting finds it (FR-37). A crowded radar becomes a picture you read *through the legend*, which is the honest degradation. **Adding radial tracks** to a band was raised and left unmeasured — a first attempt spaced them below one marker diameter and made capacity worse, which proves only that the attempt was wrong; crumb `rg-cbx` requires geometry claims to be validated against `mockup.html` before they are written down, and this one has not been. |
| **D-49** | **The documentation recommends a curated `Adopt`, and the tool enforces nothing** (NFR-19). | *Added on this review. Its capacity figures are at natural size; D-86's growth raises them up to about 2.5×, which leaves the argument intact.* Whichever ring order is used, the radar reads best when `Adopt` names **what the organisation recommends** rather than **everything it runs** — a shortlist re-chosen each edition, not an inventory. Three things already assume it. **A-2** puts item counts "in the tens, not the hundreds", which an exhaustive estate would exceed on its own. **§4** puts the authoritative catalogue outside this tool, so the radar is a *view* of it and a view may legitimately be a subset. And **FR-14's geometry** rewards it: an inventory in the innermost band overflows at about 2 items per quadrant, a shortlist does not. **The cost, stated rather than hidden:** the persona table has an engineer asking whether a technology is Adopt or Trial, and under curated semantics an absent item means "not featured this edition", *not* "not in use" — so the radar answers *should I use this?* and the catalogue answers *do we run this?*. That division is consistent with §4 but it is a real narrowing of what a reader can conclude from absence, and the documentation says so. **D-50 gives back the second question** for anyone who wants it on the radar, by adding an `In Use` ring — which needs no tool change. This is **advice, not a rule**: §4 forbids the tool policing content, nothing validates it, and an author who wants an exhaustive radar gets one — with FR-89's warnings when a band fills up. |
| **D-50** | **A separate `In Use` ring is the recommended way to keep both meanings, and needs no tool change** (NFR-19). | *Added on this review, resolving D-49's stated cost.* If `Adopt` is curated advice, the estate question — *do we actually run this?* — has no ring to answer it. Splitting the two restores it: `--rings "Assess,Trial,Adopt,In Use,Retire"` gives the lifecycle **Assess → Trial → Adopt → In Use → Retire**, where `Adopt` stays a recommendation and `In Use` carries the inventory. **This already works.** FR-12 and FR-14 take any ring count, and FR-15 keeps a ring name's words, so `In Use` needs nothing built — it is the case arbitrary ring counts exist for (D-4), and a useful check that the flexibility is real rather than theoretical. The geometry cooperates: at four quadrants `In Use` lands in the second band from the centre, holding about 7 items per quadrant today and 26 under OQ-16, while the accumulating ring stays clear of the innermost band. `Retire` does tighten to about 1 per quadrant at five rings against 2 at four, which is tolerable precisely because it is the transient stage — and 6 under OQ-16. Recommended, not required: §4 keeps the tool out of the author's vocabulary, and `--rings` renders whatever is declared. **There is a second shape of the same idea, and it also needs no tool change:** leave `In Use` **out** of `--rings` and keep the rows in the CSV anyway. FR-17 tolerates a ring value outside the declared set, so those items leave the graphic entirely, take no number (FR-18), and surface in the table view carrying every column they have (FR-78) — the estate is tracked and consultable without crowding the picture. Since v0.91 that needs `--table all`, since the default table publishes only what the radar draws (FR-106, D-95). D-5 already anticipates exactly this, citing a locally-tracked `Rejected`; `In Use` is the same pattern at larger volume. **It is not silent, and the volume is the catch:** FR-17 warns per unplotted row, so a forty-item inventory prints forty warnings on every render. That is the case which makes crumb `rg-m59`'s verbosity question bite rather than itch. Declaring the ring (the first shape) costs a band and warns nothing; omitting it (the second) costs the warnings and keeps the graphic clean. *Revisited in v0.92 (D-96):* naming the ring in `--unplotted-rings` (FR-107) gives the second shape without the warning. |
| **D-51** | **Summarise repeated warnings; add no suppression flag** (NFR-20). | *Added on this review, resolving the first and third questions of crumb `rg-m59`.* D-50's recommended pattern — an undeclared `In Use` ring holding the estate — prints one FR-17 warning per row, so a forty-item inventory prints forty warnings on **every** render, for a configuration the author chose deliberately and will not change. A `--quiet` or `--no-warnings` flag was the obvious fix and was rejected twice over. It is **not a new capability**: NFR-8 already routes warnings to stderr, so `2>/dev/null` does the same thing, and a flag only makes it discoverable. And it is **the wrong shape** — blanket suppression discards the duplicate-name and unparsable-date warnings, which are accidents worth hearing, in order to silence the ring warning, which is not. The volume, not the warnings, is the problem: grouping fixes it without a flag, keeps every distinct signal, and helps every class `rg-m59` complains about rather than only this one. The cost is that SC-10's golden files assert on the grouped form, which is a one-time change made before any exist. *Question (b) of `rg-m59` — whether a blank `qualifier` should warn at all — was left open here and closed in v0.36: it does not (FR-20, D-6).* **A targeted `--ignore "In Use"` was weighed separately and also rejected**, though it aims better than `--quiet`, silencing only what the author declared expected and leaving a typo visible. Three things told against it once grouping was in place. It now removes **one line rather than forty**, so its value fell with the problem it solved. That line **documents a lossy operation** — FR-17 drops those rows from the graphic, and the warning is the only trace; suppressing it in a build script hides the loss from whoever inherits the script. And it **splits ring vocabulary across two flags**, so a reader must consult both to know what the tool will do with a value. NFR-20's enumerated line gets the real benefit — a typo stays distinguishable from an intention — without any of that. *Revisited in v0.92 (D-96):* `--unplotted-rings` is that targeted form, accepted because the one grouped line prints unchanged on every render for a choice the author made, so the author learns to skip it, and a typo enumerated inside it is skipped with it. D-95 does not enable the reversal; it makes the loss more complete, since by default the rows leave the page too, which D-96 records as an accepted limit. `--quiet` stays rejected. |
| **D-52** | **Embed the output version, `major.minor` only** (NFR-12). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-i9o`'s first question.* NFR-12 had refused a version string "exactly as a timestamp would" break diffs, and the analogy was false: a timestamp changes every run, a version only on a release — which is precisely when NFR-12 allows output to change. Embedding it serves NFR-12's own stated purpose, a diff explained by a version rather than investigated, more directly than leaving the reader to correlate against a changelog. **The full version was rejected for a reason the original refusal missed:** a patch release does not change output, so a patch number in the file would make every patch release diff every edition and every golden file — the one kind of diff NFR-12 says should not happen. `major.minor` changes exactly when output may. Opt-in provenance (D-19's fallback) was weighed and is unnecessary once the default carries the useful part. PNG is exempt because NFR-4 compares it by decoded pixels, where a version in a metadata chunk would be invisible to the comparison and to every reader. |
| **D-53** | **Fold with Unicode's default lowercase mapping; order by code point** (FR-76, FR-9, FR-10, FR-34). | *Added on this review, closing OQ-9's residue and crumb `rg-wld`.* Two implementations — the prototype and the Rust tool — could number one CSV differently above ASCII, which NFR-4 forbids. **Folding:** Unicode's default, locale-independent lowercase mapping, as Rust's `str::to_lowercase` implements it. Full case folding was the alternative; it matches more caseless variants (`ß`/`ss`) but the difference only bites on input A-4 keeps out, and the lowercase mapping is what the scaffold's `normalise()` already does. Locale rules (Turkish dotted/dotless I) are excluded by NFR-4's locale-independence. **Ordering:** Unicode code point, which equals UTF-8 byte order and so is Rust's native `str` comparison. The prototype's UTF-16 code-unit order was the other candidate and inverts against it only for a supplementary-plane character versus one in U+E000–U+FFFF; matching the prototype would mean a custom comparator in the shipping tool to agree with a page that does not ship. `mockup.html` already documents the caveat and can match with `Array.from(s)` if it is ever re-run as a reference. |
| **D-54** | **Marker-number ink is chosen per series entry** (FR-82, SC-20). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-smm`.* The mockup drew every marker number in white. That clears 4.5:1 on seven of FR-81's entries but gives 3.81:1 on green, at 9px, where WCAG 1.4.3 has no large-text allowance. No single ink passes all eight, because the series sits in the luminance band D-41 confined it to, and that band straddles the point where white and near-black tie. Two alternatives were rejected. **Darkening the green** reopens D-41: green is bright so it stays apart from olive under deuteranopia, and every palette-proof measurement would need redoing. **A ninth role** cannot help, because a single value is exactly what fails. Choosing per entry is a pure function of the entry's value, with an exact tie going to white, so it is deterministic (NFR-4) and needs no new `--palette` role. It does need the value to be parseable, which is why FR-86 requires `#RRGGBB`. |
| **D-55** | **Remove characters the output cannot display on input, and warn** (FR-90, NFR-9). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-jyx`.* The escaping layer had swapped them for U+FFFD silently. That had two faults: nothing told the author, and FR-85's Inter subset has no U+FFFD glyph, so the PNG drew a missing-glyph box while the browser fell back to a system font, which breaks SC-7's parity. Removal plus a warning is "content is not fatal" applied the usual way. A hard error was rejected on that same line: the character decodes, so it is content, not structure. The same rule covers `--title`, `--subtitle` and `--rings` values, which reach the same output. The escaping functions keep their replacement as a backstop that validated input never reaches. |
| **D-56** | **One legend maximum width, derived from the narrow document** (FR-79, FR-70). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-3pp`.* `mockup.html` capped the wide legend at 440 but wrapped the narrow one at 380, its 420-wide document less two 20-unit margins. Two wrap layouts would mean building the legend twice, which breaks the architecture's one set of blocks placed two ways, the thing that makes FR-68 true by construction. Deriving the narrow width from a 440 legend was the other route, rejected because it widens the narrow document to 480 and renders phone text at about 9.8px instead of 11.2px, the shrinkage FR-70 exists to prevent. The cost is that a wide legend wraps a little earlier than the mockup's. |
| **D-57** | **A config file, named explicitly, with command-line precedence** (FR-91). | *Added on this review, at the maintainer's request.* Keeping a radar's settings in a file makes editions consistent, and the setting that matters most is `--rings`: getting it wrong silently re-bands the radar, where a wrong title is only cosmetic. D-51 rejected `--quiet` because a shell redirect already did its job. A wrapper script would also hold flags, but it passes strings through unchecked, while the tool validates a config's keys and values, so a typo fails loudly. **Explicit, not discovered:** an implicit `radargen.toml` in the working directory would make output depend on where the command ran, which NFR-4 rules out. **Command line wins**, so per-run values stay visible. **Paths resolve against the file**, so the file means the same thing from any directory. **The CSV stays positional**, so every command shows what it renders. *Amended in v0.102, at the maintainer's decision (2026-10-22):* the CSV may also be the file's `input` key, so a scheduled job runs `radargen -c radar.toml` with `edition = "today"` and no other argument; automation outweighs seeing the input in the command, and the positional CSV still overrides the key. **Unknown keys are fatal**, for the same reason FR-86 refuses partial palettes. Placed in **v1.1**, so the v1.0 scope does not move; adding it is a minor bump under NFR-12. `-c` and `-e` are provisional under OQ-2, chosen to match the scaffold's convention of a short form for every flag. |
| **D-58** | **Dates in values come from `--edition`, through one `{edition}` placeholder** (FR-92). | *Added on this review.* The request was an output pattern such as a dated filename, filled automatically. **Filling it from the clock was rejected**: a subtitle from today's date breaks NFR-4 and D-24 outright, and even a clock-dated filename means an old edition can no longer be re-rendered under its own name. An explicit `--edition` keeps the convenience, one date per run instead of two, and keeps every render reproducible. **A single ISO token, not strftime codes:** `%M` is minutes in strftime and month is `%m`, a slip easily made in exactly this use, and ISO dates sort correctly as filenames. Other layouts can be added later as further tokens without breaking this one. **Unknown placeholders are fatal when placeholders are in use**, because a typo would otherwise be published; outside that, braces stay literal so no v1.0 command breaks (NFR-12). **An unused `--edition` warns** rather than passing silently, because the likely cause is a forgotten placeholder. Literal braces in titles are rare enough that no escape is defined; one can be added additively if needed. **Amended in v0.102, at the maintainer's decision (2026-10-22): `-e today`.** The rationale is automation: a scheduled job, in CI or cron, can run `radargen radar.csv -c radar.toml -e today` with no script to work out the date. Clock-filling was rejected above because it breaks reproducibility; this keeps reproducibility by making the clock an explicit opt-in and **echoing the date it used**, so any render can be repeated with that `-e`. Nothing else reads the clock, so D-24 holds for every other command. The date is local, in the time zone `TZ` names or the system's, and the echo names that zone; on a CI runner it is usually UTC. A zone that cannot be found gives UTC's date with a warning saying so, never silently (FR-92). The word is matched exactly, lowercase; other words, `yesterday` or `Today`, stay invalid dates. The echo is a warning line, since stderr has no other class for a line the author should act on (NFR-9). **Also decided by the maintainer, 2026-10-22: `edition` is a `--config` key** (FR-91). The date was first kept off the file because a date is per run; with `today` that reasoning gives way to automation, since a job's file can say `edition = "today"` and the job needs no flag at all. `-e` overrides the key, and the echo names the key and its file, so a render from the file is as reproducible as one from the flag. |
| **D-59** | **Read the CSV as Excel writes it: skip blank records, ignore empty header cells, name the separator, and point quoting errors at the fault** (FR-1, FR-3, FR-4, NFR-10). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-lfd`, from the PR 05 plan's review (RG-005).* Each rule closes a case where a file that looks right to its author failed with a message they could not act on. **Blank records:** Excel writes a cleared row inside its used range as `,,,` — at the end, between records, or above the header when the sheet starts lower down. Read literally, FR-3 made the first of those the header, FR-6 reported an empty `name` on a line the author cannot see, and FR-4 failed an empty or whitespace-only line on its field count. Skipping them loses nothing, and keeping their lines in the count keeps NFR-10's numbers on what an editor shows. Blankness is tested before FR-90's removal, so a record whose only content is an undisplayable character still reaches FR-90's warning and FR-6, as in SC-10, rather than vanishing silently. **Empty header cells:** a stray cell to the right of the data pads every line, header included, so two empty header cells would collide under FR-4 as `column 6 '' repeats column 5` — a message naming nothing the author can find. An empty cell names no column, so it cannot repeat one. Whether such a column is otherwise acceptable is left to PR 06 (crumb `rg-rnc`) under FR-5 and FR-78. *Since v0.43* FR-93 drops it (D-61). **The separator:** Excel's CSV formats use the system list separator, which is `;` in decimal-comma locales, so such a file arrives as one column. It would fail every row with a comma on FR-4 and then FR-5 for a header that visibly names `name`. A one-column header can never satisfy FR-5, so refusing it at the header loses nothing. **A `--delimiter` flag was not added**, for D-27's reason: the message is the mitigation. **Quoting errors:** NFR-10's record-start line is right for row diagnostics, but an unclosed quote or text after a closing quote is a fault at one place in the file, and in a multi-line record the record's first line can be several lines above it. Go To Line must land on the stray `"`, which is D-37's reason for printing a line at all. These are tier one (NFR-16), since nothing after them parses. **Spaces around quotes were left as RFC 4180 has them** — part of the field — rather than dropped: Excel never writes them; a hand-typed `x, "y"` keeps its quotes visibly in the output, and `"x" ,y` is refused as text after a closing quote. |
| **D-60** | **`--rings` is declared edge-to-centre, the order a radar is read in** (FR-12, FR-32, FR-35). | *Added on this review, at the maintainer's request.* Until v0.41 the flag was centre-to-edge, an order nothing recorded a reason for: it is how ThoughtWorks lists its rings (Adopt, Trial, Assess, Hold), where the centre is the strongest recommendation. The in-house radar reads the other way, as a lifecycle from the edge inward, and the mismatch had already cost three times. FR-35 had to present the legend in the reverse of the declared list; FR-34 numbered in the declared order while the legend ran reversed, so numbers descended down every group until D-33 caught it four revisions later; and v0.13 found §1 and G-2 describing the default outside-in while FR-12 declared it inside-out, so a maintainer following §1 would have got a silently reversed radar. Declaring edge-to-centre makes the default `Assess,Trial,Adopt,Retire` — the lifecycle as people say it — and makes the declared order the legend's and the numbering's, so the reversal goes. **Cost, accepted:** the ThoughtWorks set is written `Hold,Assess,Trial,Adopt`, backwards to anyone copying it from a BYOR configuration. One convention or the other must read reversed, and the tool's default should not be the one. Changed before any layout code depended on band order; `src/rings.rs` only parses the list. |
| **D-61** | **Drop unnamed columns, trim invisible format characters, and name the ring set's source** (FR-93, FR-76, FR-15, FR-17, NFR-20). | *Added on this review, resolving crumbs `rg-lme` and `rg-dy8` and the question D-59 left on `rg-rnc`, ahead of the PR 06 plan.* Each closes a case where the author is told something they cannot act on. **Unnamed columns:** D-59 let an empty header cell past FR-4 so Excel's trailing padding would parse, and left open whether such a column is accepted. A column with no name has no heading in the table and no label in the detail panel, so there is nothing to show it under. **A hard error was rejected** because it restores the failure D-59 fixed: a file that looks right, refused over a column its author cannot see. The warning when it holds data is what separates padding from a forgotten heading; without it the value would vanish silently. **Format characters:** a second byte-order mark or a zero-width space pasted in with a header name survived both FR-76's trim (it is not whitespace) and FR-90 (it is displayable). FR-5 then reported `name` missing from a header that visibly has it, and a `ring` of `Adopt` plus U+200B went unplotted as undeclared. Trimming Cf at the ends closes that for every compared value at once. **Removing them throughout was rejected:** a zero-width joiner inside an emoji sequence is content, and stripping it would make two different names duplicates. **Trimming header names alone was rejected** too, since it leaves the same confusion on `ring` and `quadrant`, and FR-76 exists so that there is one rule. Displayed item text is unchanged; the two labels FR-76 already trims, FR-9's quadrant and FR-15's ring, lose boundary format characters too. **The tag characters are exempt** because they end a subdivision flag: England's and Scotland's flags are U+1F3F4 followed by tag characters, so trimming them at the end of a value would leave both as the same bare black flag, the very duplicate this decision rejects. Which characters are Cf follows the Unicode version of the pinned dependency that supplies the category, as the lowercase mapping already does, so NFR-4 holds for a given build. **The ring set's source:** FR-17's warning said `--rings` whatever supplied the rings, so a render using the default set or a config file's `rings` sent the author to a flag they never passed. Naming the source says which to edit. A neutral `not a declared ring` was rejected for leaving them to remember where the set came from. |
| **D-62** | **Group repeated row-level hard errors the way warnings are grouped** (FR-4, FR-5, FR-6, NFR-11, NFR-16, NFR-20, SC-10). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-pcd`, raised in the PR 05 plan review and widened to FR-6 in the PR 06 plan review.* NFR-16 collects every row-level error so a 200-row export is not fixed one error per run, but it said nothing about presentation, so one systematic cause printed one identical line per row: an extra trailing comma on the header only made every row an FR-4 error, and a `ring` column left blank throughout made every row an FR-6 error. Two hundred identical lines bury the one that differs, which is the failure D-51 fixed for warnings. **NFR-20's shape is reused rather than a second one invented:** one line per class, uncapped, values or positions in first-occurrence order, and the ordinary message for a class seen once, so a small file reads as it did. **One difference: an error lists every line, not a count and the first line.** A warning's rows are still rendered, and reach FR-78's table view under `--table all` (FR-106); an error writes nothing, so a line left out of its message is a line the author finds only on the next run, which is the loop NFR-16 removes. Ranges keep a 200-row line short. FR-4's class enumerates the numbers of values it saw, because the number is what separates a systematic cause from a stray comma, as the ring value separates `In Use` from `Adpot`. Its closing cause follows from what it saw, which also settles crumb `rg-w18`: the per-row hint sent every over-long row to quoting, wrong for Excel's trailing padding, and every short row to a missing value, wrong for a header with one comma too many — which is invisible in a spreadsheet, so the message also counts the header's unnamed columns. FR-6's class is per column, since each column is its own fix; FR-5 names every missing column in one error, since four lines for a file whose header row was deleted or overwritten with data say one thing. **FR-4's duplicate headers stay separate:** each names a different pair, and there are at most a few. **Leaving errors ungrouped was rejected:** a long list is at least complete, but it buries the one different line. NFR-11's order is extended to errors from both stages so that the merged list reads top to bottom, with the header line's errors in a defined order, and errors print after the warnings so the last thing on screen is why the run failed. **Cost, accepted:** hard errors need a grouping pass like the warnings' one, built before SC-10's golden files exist. |
| **D-63** | **The ordinal tiebreaks compare trimmed values, not raw ones** (FR-76, FR-9, FR-34). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-40l`, raised in the PR 06 plan review.* FR-76 said the ordinal tiebreaks of FR-9 and FR-34 compare the raw values, and FR-9 said its variants compare trimmed; for ` tool` against `Tool` the two pick different labels. **Trimmed wins for both**, because a raw comparison lets the trim's own target decide the result: a space sorts before every letter, so a stray leading space would choose a quadrant's label and move an item ahead of the name it duplicates, which FR-76 exists to stop. The values are trimmed and not folded, so `Tool` and `tool` still order by case and do not fall through to row order, which is what D-29 and FR-34's ordinal step are for. Names equal after the trim, such as ` Kafka` and `Kafka`, fall through to CSV row order; FR-28 already warns about them as duplicates, and NFR-4 holds because row order is part of the input. PR 06 already trims FR-9's comparison. **Raw values were rejected:** they break such ties by whitespace the author cannot see, and FR-76 promises a stray space changes nothing. |
| **D-64** | **Diagnostics name what the author needs to act, in the author's words** (FR-17, FR-28, NFR-10). | *Added on this review, resolving crumbs `rg-85c`, `rg-3z2`, `rg-bvl`, `rg-fju`, `rg-fp5` and `rg-s71`, raised in the PR 05 and PR 06 reviews.* Six small gaps with one cause: messages said what the tool saw, in its own terms, and stopped. **Vocabulary:** *field* is CSV's word and the author's is *value* under a *column*. **Position:** a spreadsheet labels columns by letter, so `column 6 (F)` saves counting across. **Duplicates:** the warning pairs each name with the earlier row's own spelling and states the matching rule, because `kafka` paired with line 3's `Kafka` reads as a false alarm until the author knows case is ignored. **Default rings:** `not declared by the default ring set` named nothing the author declared and gave no next step, so it lists the rings, which also puts a typo beside the ring it missed. **Invisible characters:** after D-61 trims the ends, an interior zero-width character is the only way a visibly correct value fails to match, so it is escaped where the value is quoted; U+200C and U+200D are left alone because they are content in emoji and joined scripts. **Provenance:** FR-17, FR-90 and NFR-10 each named a setting's source in their own words, so NFR-10 now states the rule once. **Cost, accepted:** every message string changes before SC-10's golden files pin them, which is the cheapest point to change them. |
| **D-65** | **Refuse or skip three inputs that otherwise fail far from their cause** (FR-1, FR-3, FR-61). | *Added on this review, resolving crumbs `rg-3ok`, `rg-ei7` and the message half of `rg-q7h`.* **NUL:** UTF-16 without a byte-order mark is valid UTF-8 full of NULs, so it passed FR-61 and failed as a field-count error on every other row plus a large FR-90 warning; no CSV holds a NUL, so one is refused as not UTF-8 text. **A line holding only a byte-order mark** above the header, as concatenated exports leave, became the header, so FR-5 reported `no name column` on a file that visibly had one; FR-3's blank test now counts FR-76's format characters as it counts spaces. Characters FR-90 removes still make a record non-blank, so D-59's protection against silent skipping holds. **Separator:** `"name" ;ring` was reported as text after a quote and `"name";,ring` as a one-column header, so a `;` or tab right after a closing quote is now named as the separator. **Wider detection was deferred:** a `;` export whose header cell holds an unquoted comma still splits into two columns and fails as a field-count error, and catching it needs a heuristic over the whole header that nobody has yet needed. |
| **D-66** | **Controls are native where they can be, state their panel, and are big enough to hit** (FR-41, FR-48, FR-50, FR-94, NFR-1, SC-17, SC-20). | *Added on this review, resolving crumbs `rg-6ny`, `rg-1uw`, `rg-owp`, `rg-r48` and `rg-pdt`, raised in the PR 04 and PR 06 accessibility reviews.* **Table rows:** FR-50 put `role="button"` on every control because `<button>` cannot live in an SVG, and that reason never applied to the table; on a `<tr>` it removed the row's cells from the accessibility tree, so a native `<button>` in the name cell replaces it. **Expanded state:** FR-38's panel opened and closed with no state exposed, which WCAG 4.1.2 requires. **One phrase:** the mockup's `not plotted` and FR-17's `not on the radar` named one thing two ways, and 2.5.3 wants the visible words in the accessible name. **Focus:** SVG paints in document order, so a later marker could cover a focused marker's ring; drawing focus in a top layer, on its own Surface1 backing, makes SC-20's surface contrast the contrast that is actually seen. **Target size:** legend rows rendered about 19px at the breakpoint and narrow-arrangement markers about 11px, so NFR-1 could not claim 2.5.8; an invisible hit area enlarges the target without enlarging the marker, which leaves OQ-16 and crumb `rg-6w7`'s legibility question for the layout PRs. **Cost, accepted:** the legend gets taller, one-line rows growing from 24 to 36 units, half again (D-71 sized them for a narrow document up to 480 units wide), and each marker carries a hit area and an indicator beside its control. *Amended by D-81:* a marker meets 2.5.8 through the Equivalent exception, not through its hit area alone. |
| **D-67** | **Measure with `harfrust`, the shaper `resvg` now uses** (FR-84, annotating D-42). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-mbv`.* `resvg` 0.48 replaced `rustybuzz` with `harfrust`, so D-42's two premises — agreement by construction, and no new supply-chain surface — became false while the measurement still agreed, empirically, to the pixel. Switching to `harfrust` at the version `resvg` resolves restores both: one shaper, already in the tree. `rustybuzz` is dropped. The oracle test against `resvg`'s own layout stays, since it is what would catch the next such change. |
| **D-68** | **Cover every script Inter draws; leave out the scripts that need a second font** (A-4, FR-85, R-7, D-43 annotated). | *Added on this review, at the maintainer's request.* A-4 stopped at Latin, so a Greek or Cyrillic name, or a Vietnamese one with stacked diacritics, drew as missing-glyph boxes in the PNG with no warning, though Inter draws all three. Adding them means a wider subset and its measurement, and nothing else: `harfrust` already shapes them, and D-53's folding and code-point order already apply above ASCII. **The other scripts were weighed and left out.** Arabic and Hebrew need a second font, fallback in both measurement and `resvg`, and the bidirectional algorithm for mixed-direction names when wrapping and drawing; CJK fonts run to megabytes, so each render would have to subset to the glyphs it uses, and CJK needs line breaking without spaces; Indic scripts need a second font. Each is days of work for input nobody has asked for, and each changes how fonts are embedded. Crumbs `rg-q2b` and `rg-rta`, which only matter for those scripts, wait with them under `rg-kzl`. **Cost, accepted:** a larger inlined font in every HTML edition, measured when the subset is regenerated. |
| **D-69** | **The radar's marks floor** (FR-70, FR-89, FR-94). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-6w7`.* The narrow arrangement scales the radar to about 0.58 (380 of 660 units), which shrank a 9-unit marker number to about 5.2 units, roughly 4 px on a 320 px screen, though the number is a marker's only link to its legend row. **Three options were weighed:** scale markers with the radar, which leaves the numbers illegible; counter-scale them like the labels, which is legible but grows markers 1.74× against the geometry, so a band holds far fewer before overlapping; or a floor. The maintainer chose the floor at 7/9: numbers draw at 7 units (about 5.3 px at 320 px, 6.5 px at 390 px), and markers grow 1.35× against the geometry at `s` = 0.576 — about a quarter fewer per band before overlap — rising as `s` falls. Labels are unaffected: FR-70 already keeps them at natural size. FR-69's breakpoint is unchanged: it is set by the legend's 12.5 px (SC-6), and at the breakpoint markers are at scale 1. FR-89's capacity stays a wide-arrangement figure. OQ-16's output-pixel marker remains a v1.1 question. *Amended by D-71:* the quadrant labels, not the 660-unit floor, usually set the narrow radar's width, so the scale is typically 0.3–0.6 rather than 0.58, and at 0.3 markers grow about 2.6× against the geometry. |
| **D-70** | **Long sector names clear the outer ring** (FR-79). | *Added on this review, resolving crumb `rg-kic`.* The mockup and PR 10a centred each name on the label point, so a long name at 3 or 9 o'clock or on a diagonal reached back across the outer ring (at N = 2 any name over about 80 units). **Three options were mocked up:** centred, as today; anchored by the inner end, which needs an end anchor and jumps at 12 and 6 o'clock; and pushed out radially. The maintainer chose the push-out: no new anchor, short names barely move, and the block widens by the push, which its size accounts for. The inner end lands on the label circle's tangent at the label point, so a label's box comes no closer to the centre than 300 *s* − 11.625 (its ascent) for *s* ≤ 1, which clears the outer ring at any radar scale above about 0.3; below that a 6 o'clock label touches the ring's stroke whatever its length, a bound that predates the push. In the same change every ring name's halo is drawn before any ring name, so at many thin bands a halo no longer erases the previous name's descenders (the maintainer notes that 20 rings is a taxonomy problem, but the fix costs nothing). The mockup is superseded on label placement. |
| **D-71** | **The narrow fit: the largest radar scale that fits, never below 0.3, and room for the document to widen** (FR-70, FR-94, FR-95, NFR-1, SC-6, SC-17). | *Added on this review, from the PR 12 plan review.* The narrow radar is drawn at the largest scale, at most 1 and at least 0.3, at which it fits the column between the narrow document's edges less the radar's own inset: the radar block sits 10 units in, so its widest label's ink lands on the 20-unit margin and the column is 400 units. 0.3 is D-70's bound, below which a 6 o'clock label touches the outer ring's stroke. **When even 0.3 does not fit, the narrow document widens** to the radar plus its insets. Quadrant labels never wrap (FR-79) and draw at natural size (FR-70), and a long one pushes outward on a diagonal or at 3 and 9 o'clock, so this happens for ordinary radars, not only exotic ones: measured with the vendored font, the document widens once a quadrant label passes about 100, 130 and 170 units (12 px SemiBold) at two, three and four quadrants. Thoughtworks' four names fit; *Infrastructure Platforms, Tools, Practice* widens it to about 458. *(Since D-78's spacing allowance, Thoughtworks' four widen it to about 460, within the headroom, and the three-name set to about 522, past it.)* **FR-94's targets are therefore sized for a narrow document up to 480 units wide**: at 320 px that is 2/3 px per unit, so legend rows are at least 36 units and hit areas 18 units in radius (D-66's 32 and 16 assumed 420). Widening up to 480 costs nothing. Past it, about 130, 165 and 210 units of label at two, three and four quadrants, a 320 px screen renders targets below 24 px and legend text at 12.5 × 320 / W px, so NFR-1's 2.5.8 claim does not hold for that dataset and FR-95 warns, naming the quadrant whose label sets the radar's width. **Rejected:** no headroom, which the plan first proposed on the wrong premise that only names over ~360 units widen, and which would fail 2.5.8 for common datasets; shrinking quadrant labels in the narrow arrangement, which costs FR-70's legible labels on a radar already small on a phone; wrapping them, which is FR-79's to decide and the largest change; letting the radar overflow, which clips content; and refusing the CSV, which turns a layout limitation into a fatal error on valid input. |
| **D-72** | **Bidirectional formatting characters are removed with the controls** (FR-90). | *Added from the PR 13 security review, at the maintainer's request.* U+202A–U+202E (the embeddings, the pop and the overrides) and U+2066–U+2069 (the isolates) are format characters, not controls, so FR-90 did not remove them and FR-76 trims format characters only at a value's ends. Inside a name they reached the drawn text, the `aria-label` and the tooltip, and could make it display as something it is not: `invoice` + U+202E + `fdp.exe` shows as `invoiceexe.pdf`, the trick behind the Trojan Source vulnerability (CVE-2021-42574). Not an injection, since escaping holds, but a spoof the author cannot see in most editors. **Removing them costs nothing today:** A-4 keeps right-to-left scripts out of scope, and the pinned font draws none of them. The left-to-right, right-to-left and Arabic letter marks (U+200E, U+200F, U+061C) are kept: they only nudge neutral characters such as punctuation and cannot reorder letters. **Revisit when right-to-left support is taken up** (crumb `rg-kzl`): genuine Arabic or Hebrew names need the isolates, though rarely the overrides. Rejected: keeping them, which leaves the spoof; escaping them, which has no meaning for a character that only affects display. |
| **D-73** | **FR-95 names a ring when a ring sets the width** (FR-95). | *Added from the PR 14a plan review, at the maintainer's request.* The radar's width is the furthest any label reaches either side of the centre, and ring names count as well as quadrant names: they sit across the vertical axis, halo included. A 70-character `--rings` entry with one short quadrant measured 750 units in the narrow arrangement, and so did a header-only CSV with no quadrants at all. FR-95 as first written named "the quadrant whose label sets the width", which would send the author to shorten a name that is not the cause, or name nothing. **The warning names the binding label, quadrant or ring**, and a ring with the flag it came from, since that is where the fix is made. Rejected: a generic message naming no label, which is FR-95's purpose undone; and keeping quadrants only, which leaves a ring-driven width silent. |
| **D-74** | **`-o` naming the input CSV is refused** (FR-62, NFR-9, NFR-16). | *Added from the PR 14a security review, at the maintainer's request.* FR-62 overwrites without prompting, so `radargen radar.html -o radar.html`, a plausible slip with a CSV saved under the wrong extension, replaced the data with the page rendered from it. **It is a tier-one error when `-o` exists and is the input**, compared by file identity (device and inode; volume and file index on Windows), so `./`, `..`, symlinks, a case-insensitive filesystem's spellings and hard links are all caught. Comparing canonicalised paths was the first design; the final review rejected it, because a filesystem where canonicalisation fails would let the check pass silently and the input be replaced. Rejected: allowing it, which loses data silently; and prompting, which FR-62 already rules out. **Extended to every file the run reads** (security review of PR 19a, 2026-10-18): the output may not be the `--config` file or the palette either, since FR-91 lets a committed config name the output. |
| **D-75** | **A page with no title still has a name, and the radar is a named group** (FR-65, NFR-1). | *Added from the PR 14 decisions, at the maintainer's request.* With `--title` omitted the page had an empty `<title>` and no heading before the radar's markers. **The tab title and a visually hidden `<h1>` read *Technology Radar*,** and the drawn document stays empty, as FR-65 says. With a title, the SVG's own level-1 heading is the only one, so the outline never doubles. **The radar is a `role="group"` named *Technology radar: 3 quadrants, 4 rings, 6 items*** (*3 sectors* since v0.103, D-101), counting declared rings and plotted items, so assistive technology announces its size before the user explores it. Rejected: `role="img"`, which flattens the subtree and hides every marker's name; `graphics-document`, which some screen readers present as one opaque graphic; and an always-present HTML `<h1>`, which would duplicate the drawn title or hide the SVG heading that standalone SVG and PDF need. |
| **D-76** | **An item's name is shown trimmed** (FR-76). | *Added at the maintainer's request, from the CSV guide's review.* FR-76 listed only the quadrant label and the ring name as trimmed for display, while the layout already trimmed `name` in the legend and the marker's label. The code was right: a leading or trailing space in a name cell is almost always an export accident, it is already ignored when FR-28 matches duplicate names, and showing it would misalign the legend's text for no reader's benefit. **The name is trimmed wherever it is displayed** (legend, marker label and tooltip, and from PR 15 the detail panel and table), by FR-76's trim, so format characters at the ends go too and case is untouched. The other free-text columns (`description`, `domain`, `rationale`) stay verbatim, since their whitespace may be deliberate and they are not set in a fixed-width column. Rejected: showing names verbatim, which would make the code obey a rule nobody wanted. |
| **D-77** | **A `link` column, `http` and `https` only** (FR-96, NFR-5, NFR-6). | *Added at the maintainer's request.* The ThoughtWorks radar links each blip to what it describes, and the CSV could already carry a URL as inert text (§4, FR-27). Recognising the column makes it clickable where a reader looks for it: the detail panel and the table. **Only `http` and `https` become links.** The CSV is authored upstream and the page is emailed and opened from disk, so a `javascript:` address would run script on a click and a `data:` one could present a forged page; refusing every other scheme is simpler and safer than listing the dangerous ones. A refused value is still shown as text with a warning, so nothing the author wrote disappears silently. **Opening in a new tab** keeps the radar where the reader left it, with `noopener noreferrer` so the opened page cannot script the radar or learn where it was opened from. **Not a request:** NFR-5 forbids the page fetching anything, and a link fetches nothing until the reader follows it. **Not on the static outputs,** which cannot be clicked, and not on the radar marker or legend row, which already open the panel (FR-38); a second action on one target would be ambiguous. Rejected: `url` as the name (`link` says what it is to an author); any scheme allowed (unsafe for the reason above); linking the item's name, which in the legend and table is already the control that opens the panel. |
| **D-78** | **Headroom for WCAG 1.4.12 text spacing** (NFR-1, FR-74, FR-79, FR-84, SC-17). | *Added from the PR 14c plan review, at the maintainer's request.* Under 1.4.12 a reader may set letter spacing to 0.12em and word spacing to 0.16em, which widens text by roughly 15–25%. Two things broke. A legend row drew its meta as a separate text at a fixed x, so a widened name ran into it. And text widened past its SVG's edge was lost: an inline SVG clips its overflow, and even with `overflow: visible`, Chromium treats SVG overflow as ink rather than scrollable overflow, so on a phone, where the narrow arrangement fills the viewport, it could not be scrolled to. **Layout now reserves the room:** every wrap point, block size, row target and document extent uses the *layout width*, the shaper's advance (FR-84) plus `0.12 × size` per character and `0.16 × size` per word separator. **The legend's inline meta continues the name's text** as a tspan after a no-break space (U+00A0, because `resvg` drops a leading U+0020 in a tspan where a browser keeps it), so it follows the name however it is spaced. Line height and paragraph spacing have no effect on SVG text at absolute baselines. **The accepted cost:** names wrap sooner, panels are a little wider, radar labels reserve more room, and more narrow documents widen past 480 (FR-95 warns), measured on the PRD example as a wide document of 1025 units against 977. The key's two longest labels were shortened to fit the narrow column. Rejected, with the maintainer: `overflow: visible` alone, which still loses text on a phone; scoping NFR-1's claim with an exception; drawing the legend as HTML, which breaks the shared `Document`; and deferring past the first release. |
| **D-79** | **Line breaks in cells: a space in labels, a newline in prose** (FR-97, NFR-6). | *Added at the maintainer's request, from the PR 14b QA review.* A CR survived FR-90, which keeps displayable characters, and reached the HTML as `&#13;` in a control's `aria-label`, which an HTML5 parser reports as a parse error. A label is one line by nature, so its break is a space, as a spreadsheet would show it squeezed. Prose keeps its breaks as plain newlines for the panel. Rejected: a space everywhere, which loses paragraph breaks the panel could show; and normalising to LF everywhere, which would carry a newline into a name, its marker label and its tooltip. |
| **D-80** | **The table carries every column, and the detail panel reads it** (FR-78, FR-38, NFR-6). | *Added at the maintainer's request, from the PR 15a plan review (RG-015A).* FR-78 listed only the optional columns, leaving out `description`, the one required free-text column. Without script the table is the only place an item's prose can be read, so it carries every named column. D-38's objection, that inline prose duplicates the panel, is met by building the panel from the item's table row rather than from a second copy. The page then holds each item's prose once, escaped once for HTML (NFR-6), and the panel cannot show a link differently from the table (FR-96). Rejected: a JSON island of every item (a second renderer, and NFR-6's script context); per-item templates (a second copy of every value); `description` left out (prose unreadable without script). *Revisited in v0.91 (D-95):* "every column" is now `--table all`'s; the default keeps the columns radargen reads, `description` among them, so prose is still readable without script. The panel still reads the row, so the table decides what the panel shows. |
| **D-81** | **A marker meets WCAG 2.5.8 through the Equivalent exception** (FR-94, D-66, NFR-1, SC-17). | *Added by the maintainer's ruling on crumb `rg-frk`, raised by the PR 15c audit.* At 320 pixels on the interaction fixture, two pairs of markers sit 24.14 pixels apart, and their 27.43-pixel hit areas overlap. 2.5.8 does not count overlapped area, and a circular hit area that size holds no 24-pixel square, so a marker alone passes neither the size test nor the spacing exception.
