| **SC-18** | A render that fails part-way — an unwritable path, a rasterisation failure, a `--palette` that will not parse — exits non-zero and leaves any file already at the `-o` path **byte-unchanged**, verified by hashing it before and after. A run with several row-level errors reports **all of them** in one invocation, and one with several warnings likewise. | NFR-8, NFR-14, NFR-15, NFR-16 |
| **SC-19** | Under `--table all`, every data row in the CSV appears in the table view, unplotted rows included and visibly flagged, carrying every named column the header declares; and an unplotted row's detail panel is reachable — the route FR-18 denies it through the legend and the graphic. Under the default `--table radar`, the table holds exactly the plotted rows and the columns radargen reads, and no unplotted row or unrecognised column reaches the page, panels included. | FR-41, FR-78, FR-17, FR-18, FR-27, FR-106 |
| **SC-20** | Every colour in FR-81 and FR-82 meets its contrast floor against both surfaces of its theme — 3:1 for series entries, ring strokes, dividers and the focus indicator — the indicator against Surface1, since FR-48 draws it above every marker on its own Surface1 backing, and against Surface2 on a legend row, which has no backing; 4.5:1 for text — and each series entry's marker- and badge-number ink (FR-82) clears 4.5:1 against that entry, all checked mechanically rather than by eye, in both built-in themes. A sector count above eight renders with cycled colours and no warning, and every sector remains identifiable by position and sector label alone (FR-39). | FR-67, FR-81–FR-83, FR-39, NFR-1 |
| **SC-21** | A `--palette` file missing any FR-82 role is a hard error naming it; one whose series entries have a gap is a hard error naming the missing index; one defining **five** series entries renders a five-colour radar that cycles at the sixth sector, and one defining **twelve** entries is accepted and honoured at its declared length, where the **built-in** eight would have repeated four of those twelve. The twelve case tests that the count is read from the file rather than assumed; it is not an endorsement of twelve sectors (A-5). A value that is not `#RRGGBB`, such as `rgb(0 114 178)`, is a hard error naming its role (FR-86). Supplying `--palette` with `--theme` is a usage error (FR-59). A file with a second block — a copy of the two-block `palette.sample.css` that preceded 1.3.0, kept under `tests/fixtures/` with its `:root[data-theme="dark"]` rule, is the fixture — an unknown property such as `--surface1`, or a duplicate declaration is a hard error naming it and its line (both lines for the duplicate), and a file with several such problems reports all of them in one run (FR-102). | FR-59, FR-67, FR-86, FR-87, FR-102 |
| **SC-22** | A CSV with seven distinct sectors renders successfully, exits 0, and emits exactly **one** warning naming the count; one with six emits none; one with twelve emits one, not seven. The warning appears before any row-level warning (NFR-11) and names no CSV line. The README and `--help` both state the range (NFR-19). | FR-88, NFR-19, NFR-9, NFR-11, A-5 |
| **SC-23** | A sector+ring band holding more items than fit at the growth the radar is drawn with (D-86) renders **every** item — none dropped, none resized — emits exactly one warning naming the sector, ring, count and comfortable capacity, and exits 0. Each of those items still has a legend row, a table row, and a tooltip, so it is identifiable with the markers overlapping. A band at or under capacity at that growth emits nothing, including one that overflows at natural size and fits once the radar grows. | FR-33, FR-89, FR-35, FR-36, FR-41, D-86 |
| **SC-24** | A CSV with forty rows carrying an undeclared ring produces **one** warning naming the count, the value and the first line — not forty — while a duplicate `name` elsewhere in the same file still produces its own warning rather than being buried. Forty `In Use` rows **and** one typo'd `Adpot` still produce **one** unrecognised-ring warning, and that line names both values with their counts, so the typo is visible without being given a line of its own. Groups arrive ordered by first occurrence (NFR-11), and every one of those forty rows appears in the table view under `--table all` (FR-78, FR-106). | NFR-20, NFR-11, FR-17, FR-78 |
| **SC-25** | The HTML output's `<head>` carries `<meta name="generator" content="radargen X.Y">`, the SVG output carries `<!-- radargen X.Y -->` before its root element, and the PDF carries `/Producer (radargen X.Y)` in its Info dictionary, where `X.Y` is the binary's major and minor version and no patch number appears; the PNG carries none of these. A patch-version bump alone leaves every HTML, SVG and PDF golden file byte-identical. | NFR-12, NFR-4 |
| **SC-26** | A config file setting `title`, `rings` and `output` renders the same document as passing those three flags. A flag passed alongside overrides its key. An unknown key, a wrong-typed value and an unparsable file are each hard errors naming the key or line, with no output written. `theme` in the file with `--palette` on the command line is FR-59's usage error. A relative `palette` path resolves next to the config file when the command runs from another directory. With neither `-o` nor `output`, the run is a usage error. `--title ""` over a config `title` renders no title. | FR-91, FR-59, FR-65, NFR-9, NFR-15 |
| **SC-27** | With `output = "{edition}.html"` and `subtitle = "Edition {edition}"`, `-e 2026-09-01` writes `2026-09-01.html` whose subtitle reads `Edition 2026-09-01`, and rerunning the command on any later day produces byte-identical output. Omitting `-e` is a hard error naming the value that needs it; `{editon}` is a hard error naming the placeholder; `-e 2026-02-30` is a hard error. `-e` with no value using it warns and renders; `--title "Radar {beta}"` without `-e` renders the braces literally. `-e today`, or `edition = "today"` in the config file with no `-e`, renders as the local date typed would, and prints `warning: edition <date> (today, <zone>); pass -e <date> to reproduce this render`; with `TZ` naming no zone it uses UTC and warns, naming the `TZ`; no other run reads the clock. | FR-92, NFR-4, D-24, D-58 |
| **SC-28** | `--create-palette` writes a file that `--palette` accepts unchanged with no warning, and a PNG and an SVG rendered with it are byte-identical to the same renders with the built-in light theme; the file is UTF-8 with LF line endings and no byte-order mark, and identical on two runs; every neutral's dark value appears in its line's comment; `docs/product/palette.sample.css` equals the written bytes; an existing path is refused and left unchanged; and the flag given with any other is a usage error. | FR-101, FR-81, FR-102 |
| **SC-29** | `--create-config` writes a file that, given to `-c` unchanged with a CSV and `-o`, renders byte-identical output to the same command without `-c`, with the same stderr; it names every FR-91 key once, in FR-91's order, each commented out; with every key line uncommented but `palette` it has no key problem, shows each key's default, and with a CSV beside it renders today's edition from `-c` alone; the file is UTF-8 with LF line endings and no byte-order mark, and identical on two runs; `docs/product/config.sample.toml` equals the written bytes; an existing path is refused and left unchanged; and the flag given with any other is a usage error. | FR-109, FR-91, FR-92 |
| **SC-30** | `-o radar.pdf` writes one page whose page box is FR-57's size at 0.75 pt per px, rounded up to whole page units; text extracted from it holds every sector, ring and item name the bundled font can draw, each intact and once, in reading order (title, key, ring labels, legend); the viewer's window title is FR-65's title or *Technology Radar* (D-75), judged by hand in manual-checks step 19; rendered at the PNG's pixel size by an independent PDF rasteriser (hayro) the largest difference in any one channel stays within a ceiling measured from faithful cases, with damage tests that exceed it; and two separate runs write identical bytes, with no creation date and no file identifier. |
| **SC-31** | `--paper letter` and `--paper a4` write landscape pages of exactly the paper's size; the first page holds the title, radar, key and the legend's first part only; text extracted from all pages holds every legend name once, in legend order, and every page's *Page n of m*; each page drawn by an independent rasteriser matches that page's PNG as SC-30's oracle measures; and two runs write identical bytes. |
| **SC-32** | *(Planned, PR 21c.)* Every PDF the test suite writes passes a PDF/UA-1 validator. |
| **SC-33** | `--pdf full` on Letter and A4 writes the leading pages, then the table pages; text extracted from the table pages holds every published item's name, and every word of its cells, once, in FR-34's order, matching the HTML table's cells; every table page begins with the heading and the header row; no row's cells span two pages unless the row is taller than a page; each page drawn by hayro matches its PNG as SC-31's oracle measures, under the legend pages' budget; two runs write identical bytes. |

## 13) Out of scope

Restating §4 for emphasis: no decision-link graph, no policy enforcement, no live reading of a standards catalogue, no server or dashboard mode. Additionally excluded by decision: JPEG output (D-17), batch rendering (D-18), auto-computed `status` (D-9), and a print stylesheet for the HTML output beyond FR-103's one rule (FR-58).

## 14) Release plan and implementation estimate

### 14.0 Specification readiness

No section is implemented while the specification debt it rests on is still open (§11.2). **v0.15 and v0.16 cleared it**, bar one product decision and one bounded question that A-4 keeps off the current path.

| Must close before | Items | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The text-metrics spike | *(none)* | **Clear** |
| Layout-engine work begins | OQ-8, OQ-12 | **Clear** — OQ-8 closed (FR-33, FR-77), OQ-12 closed (FR-81–FR-83) |
| Shared-SVG generation begins | OQ-14, OQ-15 | **Clear** (FR-79; NFR-6) |
| HTML-output work begins | OQ-11, OQ-13 | **Clear** (FR-78; NFR-1, FR-80, SC-17) |
| The first golden file is written | OQ-8, OQ-9, OQ-10 | **Clear** — NFR-17 fixes the rounding and line endings a golden file needs, and D-40 settles what PNG comparison means |
| v1.1 packaging | OQ-6, OQ-7 | **Clear** — OQ-6 closed (D-44), OQ-7 closed (D-45), the font named (FR-85) |
| The comparator is ported to Rust | OQ-9's residue | **Clear** — closed in v0.37 (D-53) |

**What this leaves: nothing. Both slices are unblocked.** Every area either slice builds has a written, testable specification, and every question that gated construction is closed. What remains in §11 is one item that gates *adoption* rather than the build (OQ-4), one that shapes v1.1's `--width` (OQ-16), and four that are genuinely optional (OQ-2, OQ-3, OQ-5, OQ-17). OQ-9's residue closed in v0.37 (D-53). Nothing on this list is now an unwritten rule that would have to be invented under pressure, which is the state §14.0 was added to reach.

**OQ-4 remains the deliberate exception:** it gates *adoption* — the date BYOR can be switched off — not the build. R-5 routes it to a data audit that runs alongside implementation.

**Closing an item means writing it down.** Each item closed in v0.15 and v0.16 became `FR-`/`NFR-` text in §§6–7 and, where it changed observable behaviour, a criterion in §12 — not a note in §11. That is what the gate asks for, and the difference between a decision that survives to implementation and one that has to be taken again later.

### 14.1 Release slices

Two slices, so BYOR can be retired without waiting for the whole scope (D-30). The boundary is drawn where the *justification* is: SC-1 and SC-2 — replacing BYOR, rendering three sectors without workarounds — are the criteria that make this project worth doing at all, and HTML alone satisfies both.

| Slice | Contains | Met when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **v1.0 — replaces BYOR** | The whole CSV contract (§6.1), the layout engine (§6.2), the shared SVG document including title block and key (§6.3), **both arrangements and the switch between them** (FR-68–FR-70), accessibility and responsiveness (§6.4), HTML output, `--rings`, `--title`, `--subtitle`, the HTML output's dual-palette theming (both palettes inlined, `prefers-color-scheme` honoured, in-page toggle) — which is the thing `--theme` is *ignored in favour of* on HTML rather than a behaviour of the flag (FR-56), with `--width` likewise accepted and ignored (FR-57) — the pinned font loaded for text measurement (NFR-7's first half), and the error/warning taxonomy of §7 less `--palette`'s conflict rule (FR-59), which arrives with the flag. | SC-1, SC-2, SC-3, SC-4, SC-5, SC-6, SC-12, SC-13, SC-14, SC-16 (from 1.0.x, D-84), and — for the one format it ships, with SC-10 less its FR-59 case — SC-8 (its same-machine half; the cross-platform half from 1.0.x, D-82), SC-9, SC-10, SC-15, SC-25 |
| **v1.1 — the static formats** | PNG and SVG export of the wide arrangement (FR-71), `--width` and `--theme` **taking effect** (FR-56, FR-57), rasterising through that same font (NFR-7's second half), `--palette` with its conflict rule (FR-59) and file validation (FR-86, FR-87, FR-102), `--create-palette` (FR-101), and `--config` and `--edition` (FR-91, FR-92). | SC-7, SC-11, SC-21, SC-26, SC-27, SC-28, SC-8/SC-9/SC-10/SC-15/SC-25 extended to PNG and SVG, and SC-10's FR-59 case |

**Release numbers.** Every PR ships as its own release (the maintainer's rule, 2026-10-16), so "v1.1" names this slice's release line, not one version: PNG export ships as **1.1.0**, SVG export as **1.2.0**, `--palette` as **1.3.0**, and `--create-palette` as **1.4.0**. `palette.sample.css` takes FR-101's single-`:root` form in 1.3.0, with `--palette`, together with the palette guide its header names (`docs/palette.md`), so no release ships a sample its own parser rejects or a link that leads nowhere; the header describes the file without naming the flag, so it is true in both releases. 1.4.0 adds the flag that writes the same bytes and the test that keeps them equal.

`--palette` sits in v1.1 even though it applies to every format, because the argument for it is a PNG argument: HTML colours can be hand-edited in the `<style>` block and an SVG can be recoloured in a design tool, but a rasterised PNG cannot be re-themed at all. Without PNG in the slice, the flag has no case that a text editor doesn't already answer. `--theme` and `--width` are accepted in v1.0 and ignored there, exactly as FR-56 and FR-57 already require them to be on HTML output — so carrying them in a v1.0 invocation is harmless rather than a usage error. A `-o` naming a `.png` or `.svg`, by contrast, fails on a v1.0 binary (FR-52). Its message must say the format is **not supported in this version**, naming the version that adds it — not "unrecognised extension", which sends the caller hunting for a typo in a path that is correct. This is a live UC-1 path, not a hypothetical: the maintainer's documented workflow is one HTML run and one PNG run, so on v1.0 the second half of it fails by design and the message is all that explains why.

From 1.2 every format FR-52 named then shipped, so the "not supported in this version" message has no trigger and was removed (D-88). PDF export (FR-110) ships as **1.19.0**, a v1.2 slice of its own with the full PDF (crumb `rg-4b8`) and tagging (crumb `rg-ffj`) to follow. Paper (FR-111) shipped in 1.20.0; the full PDF (FR-112) ships next, then tagging and PDF/UA-1 (FR-113, FR-114), each a minor release (design RG-104).

**The shared SVG document is built in v1.0, not deferred with the formats it enables.** That looks like carrying cost early — the whole point of FR-44 is cheap PNG export, which v1.0 does not ship. It is deliberate: retrofitting the legend from HTML into SVG afterwards would mean building the legend twice, and D-14 and D-15 are load-bearing enough that discovering a problem with them in v1.1 is far worse than discovering it in v1.0. The text-metrics spike (R-1) therefore stays the first task of the project, not the first task of v1.1.

**What this ordering costs:** UC-3 — the slide deck and the vector touch-up — is unavailable until v1.1, UC-1's "once for HTML and once for PNG" is halved to the HTML step, and G-7 is not met by v1.0. Anyone needing an image in the interim screenshots the HTML, as they do today. That is a real gap, accepted for a bounded period, not a claim that the static formats are optional.

### 14.2 Rough estimate

*Rough, not committed — there is no historical velocity for this shape of tool. Covers both slices: all three output formats, the accessibility and responsiveness requirements, and configurable rings.*

| Area | Slice | Estimate | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Text-metrics spike** | before both | **Done** (was 0.5 day) | **Complete — see `spikes/text-metrics/FINDINGS.md`.** Came in under estimate and produced FR-84. Retires R-1 before it can distort everything downstream: measure a string through `resvg`'s font stack, confirm wrap points match what the HTML output will produce. If this spike overruns, the two estimates below move with it. |
| Project scaffold (cargo, CLI args, error types) | v1.0 | 0.5 day | Positional input (FR-60), `--rings`, `--title`, `--subtitle`, `--theme`, `--width` with ranged parsing (FR-64), extension-based format inference, output-path checks (FR-62), and the warning/error taxonomy (NFR-8–NFR-11). `--palette` and its `conflicts_with` group (FR-59) land with v1.1 |
| CSV parsing and validation | v1.0 | 1.5–2 days | Required/optional columns, BOM and encoding handling (FR-2, FR-61), header normalisation, ring validation against a configurable list with warn-not-error handling, `--rings` list validation (FR-63), the `qualifier` asymmetric fallback, `status`, date tolerance, duplicate-name warning, per-row error reporting with line numbers in stable order |
| Layout engine (quadrant angles, ring bands, placement, numbering) | v1.0 | 1–1.5 days | Geometry already validated in `mockup.html`; this ports it. Ring bands generalise from a fixed 4 to N the same way sectors already do — a small generalisation of solved geometry. Includes the N=0/1/2 cases (FR-42, FR-43), the full numbering tiebreak chain (FR-34), and palette cycling (FR-67) |
| Shared SVG generation (radar + legend + title + key, both arrangements) | v1.0 | 3.5–4.5 days | The real new scope: legend built in SVG rather than ported from the prototype's flexbox version — manual text layout and wrapping (depends on the spike above). Includes the title block (FR-65), the key (FR-66), and both arrangements (FR-68–FR-70). The second arrangement is cheap *if* the blocks are built as independently-placeable units returning their own dimensions, and expensive if position is baked into them — the prototype demonstrates the former. Includes resolving the theme to concrete colours at generation time, since a static document has no media query to lean on, and context-correct escaping throughout (NFR-6) |
| HTML output wrapper | v1.0 | 1.5–2.5 days | Embeds both arrangements with the media-query switch (FR-69); adds the interactive layer as inline JS/CSS — hover tooltip, marker↔legend cross-highlight, click-through detail panel with a real close affordance, table-view toggle, and keyboard operability. Larger than a naive estimate because accessibility and responsiveness are requirements here, not afterthoughts |
| PNG + SVG export | v1.1 | 1–1.5 days | `resvg` integration for PNG with pinned font loading (NFR-7); SVG export writes the already-generated document directly, effectively free once rasterisation works. `--theme`/`--width` plumbing |
| Config file and `--edition` | v1.1 | 0.5 day | TOML via `serde` merged under the command-line flags before validation (FR-91), path resolution against the file, `{edition}` expansion and its errors (FR-92) |
| Tests | folded into each area (TDD) | — | Unit tests for CSV edge cases and layout maths; golden-file snapshot tests per format and per ring set (SC-8, SC-13); an escaping test with hostile input (SC-9); a keyboard-navigation test (SC-5); an exit-code and diagnostics-ordering suite (SC-10); an offline-load check and an external-reference grep over each output (SC-15); a clean-machine smoke test of the released binary (SC-16) |
| Docs, CLI help, packaging | split across both | 0.5–1 day | Three output modes and the flag set, `--palette`, `--create-palette` and the palette guide (`docs/palette.md`), the OFL notice shipping beside the vendored font (FR-85), the GPL-3.0 licence text (NFR-18), and the rendered-range note in both README and `--help` (NFR-19) |
| Review cycle (code-review → Copilot → simplify → code-review) | per slice | 0.5–1 day | Per the standing workflow, not optional |

**Total: roughly 11.5–16.5 focused working days** — of which **v1.0 is roughly 9.5–13** — counting the review cycle once per slice and splitting the docs line between them. The text-metrics spike (R-1) remains the dominant source of variance, and it is charged to v1.0 despite being what makes v1.1 cheap. Up from v0.3's 10–15 by the title block, the key, and the second arrangement; nothing was re-scoped downward. Auto-computed `status` (D-9) is excluded.

**This estimate predates OQ-8–OQ-15 and does not cover them.** Every line above was costed against requirements that §11 now records as underspecified: marker geometry and orientation (OQ-8), the determinism rules NFR-4 needs (OQ-9), the failure and diagnostics taxonomy (OQ-10), the table view's contents (OQ-11), the palette (OQ-12), NFR-1's evidence path (OQ-13), the wrapping algorithm (OQ-14) and NFR-6's contexts (OQ-15). Closing those may **add** requirements rather than merely clarify existing ones. The Tests row also carries no day figure at all against eight named suites, which is an omission wearing a label rather than a costing. Read 11–16 as a floor for the scope as *understood*, not the scope as *specified*, and re-cost once OQ-8 and OQ-10 close — those two change how much code exists, not just how carefully it is written.

**v1.0 is not much cheaper than the whole thing, and that is the honest reading of this table.** Slicing buys a working replacement for BYOR roughly 1.5–3 days sooner and a review checkpoint at a natural boundary — not a materially smaller first delivery. The reason is FR-44: the expensive part is the shared SVG document, which v1.0 builds and v1.1 merely rasterises. If that trade doesn't look worth the release overhead once the spike lands, collapsing back to a single v1.0 costs nothing but this section.

| 0.16 | 2026-08-27 | **OQ-9's byte-identity question resolved, and the determinism mechanics NFR-4 always depended on written down.** NFR-4 promised byte-identical output for every format; for PNG that requires pinning the deflate implementation, compression level, filter strategy and chunk set, since identical pixels encode to different bytes under any of them. That collides with NFR-12 — a routine `cargo update` would change output bytes with no version bump to explain the diff — and holding it would mean vendoring an encoder and auditing every bump indefinitely. Weighed against the guarantee's own stated purpose, "so output is diffable in git across editions", which is textual: a binary diff of two PNGs is unreadable whether or not the bytes match, so byte-identity there bought a constraint that served nothing. **NFR-4 now promises byte-identity for HTML and SVG and pixel-identity for PNG** (D-40), SC-8 compares the PNG by decoded pixel buffer rather than by hash, and the accepted cost is stated: a committed PNG can show a spurious git diff after an encoder bump. **NFR-17 added** for what the text formats do need and nothing stated — 3-decimal rounding half away from zero, `.` separator, no exponent or locale, LF line endings on every platform including Windows, UTF-8 with no BOM. Rounding is what makes NFR-4 survive `sin`/`cos` not being bit-identical across architectures: the discrepancy is around 1e-13 user units against a third decimal place of 1e-3. The exact-boundary case is acknowledged as possible, vanishingly unlikely, and accepted rather than engineered around. **OQ-9 reduced, not closed:** what remains is which case folding FR-10 and FR-34 mean and what "ordinally" means above the BMP, where the prototype's UTF-16 code-unit comparison and Rust's UTF-8 byte comparison invert. A-4 bounds entry text to Latin script so nothing current reaches either, and it gates the comparator's Rust port rather than the layout engine; crumb `rg-wld` holds the analysis. §14.0's gate is now clear for every v1.0 area except OQ-12's palette. No requirement was re-scoped downward and the §14.2 estimate does not move. |

FR-82 defines the eight neutral roles for both themes. Two alternatives were measured and rejected (D-41): per-theme series values reach ΔE 33.6 to normal vision but collapse the deficient-vision floor to 3.5, and optimising purely for that floor yields three browns and two magentas. SC-20 requires the contrast floors to be checked mechanically rather than by eye. `docs/product/palette.sample.css` added, carrying the values, the measured bounds, and the note that NFR-1's AA claim does not travel to a custom palette. §14.0's gate now reads clear for every v1.0 area; OQ-6 and OQ-7 remain, and both are v1.1 product decisions. No estimate change. |

| 0.19 | 2026-08-28 | **Text-metrics spike done; R-1 retired** (`spikes/text-metrics/`, with FINDINGS.md). Measuring text without a browser works and matches `resvg` to 5e-6 px — but **not** the way R-1 described it. R-1 said real font metrics via `fontdb`/`ttf-parser` were what was needed; those give per-glyph advances, and a string's width is not their sum. `resvg` shapes with `rustybuzz`, which applies GPOS kerning, and the naive sum drops it: on Libertinus Sans at 12.5px, `Terraform` measured 0.79px wide and `WWWWWWWW` 2.1px narrow — 2.2% of the string — while `Pulumi` and `Internationalisation` agreed exactly, having no kerning pairs. No constant factor corrects a discrepancy that is zero on some strings and signed differently on others, and a spot check on the wrong sample finds nothing; at 2.2% it is more than enough to move a wrap point, which is the silent HTML-versus-PNG divergence SC-7 exists to catch. **FR-84 added:** measurement goes through the same shaper that rasterises, so the agreement is structural rather than coincidental (D-42) — and letter-spacing, which the shaper does not apply, is added by the generator as `n-1` gaps. Also verified: FR-79's greedy wrap including the mid-token break, rendered ink falling inside the measured advance through `resvg` itself, and byte-identical output across processes (NFR-4, NFR-17). The prototype's 1.2 fudge factor is unnecessary and is recorded as not-to-be-ported. The spike used Libertinus Sans (OFL) as a stand-in — **R-7 and OQ-7 still own the font choice**, and the mechanism is font-independent. §14.2's spike row closed; the two costly lines it gated keep their estimates, with less risk attached. |

Three qualifiers carry weight rather than taste (D-43): *static* because a variable font resolves metrics from axis coordinates and a browser disagreeing with `resvg` about an axis default would reopen the divergence FR-84 just closed; *two weights* because FR-35 separates the legend's levels by indentation and type style, and each further weight is bytes inlined into every published edition; *vendored subset* because a build-time subsetting step is one more thing that can differ per machine, which NFR-4 does not tolerate. R-7 drops from Medium to Low and keeps only its script-coverage half, bounded by A-4, with two packaging checks recorded — the measured subset size, and whether the font declares a **Reserved Font Name**, since OFL permits subsetting but subsetting *is* modification. **OQ-6 resolved to ship `--palette`** (D-44), on an argument that did not exist when the question was posed: FR-83 caps the built-in palette at eight because one colour set must clear 3:1 on *both* themes, and a custom palette serves one theme, so it has roughly twice the luminance band — making the flag the answer to a capability limit rather than only a branding affordance. **FR-87** therefore leaves the series count **free**: five entries give a five-colour radar that cycles at the sixth quadrant, twelve give twelve without repeating. A **gap** in the numbering is a hard error, since it cannot be told from a typo. **FR-86** goes the other way on the *roles*: all eight of FR-82 are required, because filling a missing one from the built-in palette would mix two palettes silently and produce exactly the contrast failure NFR-1 disclaims on custom palettes. R-6 closed. §14.0's gate now names **OQ-7 alone**, and only at packaging time — the dependency-licence scan recorded there found all 63 crates permissive, so nothing downstream constrains that answer either. No estimate change. |
| 0.21 | 2026-08-28 | **OQ-7 closed: published, GPL-3.0-or-later** (NFR-18, D-45) — the last question gating either slice. Three options were weighed against the stated intent, which was that anyone building on this opens their own work in turn. Permissive `MIT OR Apache-2.0`, the Rust ecosystem default, was rejected on exactly that ground: it permits a vendor wrapping the renderer and selling it closed. MPL-2.0 was rejected as weaker than intended, since file-level copyleft still allows linking into proprietary software. GPL-3.0's usual objection is largely inert for this shape of tool — **a CLI binary obliges nobody who merely runs it**, and a rendered radar is output rather than a derived work, so a client's CSV, HTML, PNG and SVG stay entirely theirs. The obligation lands only on redistributing a modified tool or linking the crate. **The accepted cost is stated rather than glossed:** blanket enterprise GPL bans exist, and a client whose procurement carries one may be unable to run the tool even though running it imposes nothing — mitigated by the output travelling unencumbered, which is how the radar actually reaches its readers (UC-2, UC-3). Verified before deciding: all 63 crates in the dependency tree are MIT, Apache-2.0, BSD or Zlib, and GPL-3.0 specifically because Apache-2.0's patent clause is compatible with GPLv3 and not GPLv2. The vendored font keeps its own OFL-1.1 terms, being aggregated with the binary rather than derived from it. §8 now records that the BYOR clone is **archived rather than deleted** on retirement. **§14.0's gate is clear for both slices** — every question that gated construction is closed, and what remains in §11 either changes no code yet (OQ-9's residue), gates adoption rather than the build (OQ-4), or is optional (OQ-2, OQ-3, OQ-5). No estimate change. |
| 0.22 | 2026-08-28 | **FR-83 corrected — an overclaim introduced in v0.17.** It stated that past eight quadrants FR-67's cycling "puts one colour on two **adjacent** sectors", which is true only when the quadrant count is one more than a multiple of eight. Because the last sector and the first are neighbours around the circle, an adjacent repeat needs `(N-1) mod 8 == 0` — so N=9 and N=17 clash, while 10 through 16 place their repeats at least one sector apart. Verified by enumeration. **N=9 is therefore the worst case, not the general case**, which leaves D-41's reasoning intact — eight entries were chosen precisely to push the clash out to nine quadrants — while removing a claim the document could not support. SC-21 reworded alongside: its twelve-entry case now says explicitly that the **built-in** palette would have repeated four of those twelve, so the sentence cannot be read as a claim about twelve quadrants in general. Found by a reader asking how twelve quadrants could render distinctly from eight defined colours — they cannot, and only the custom-palette path reaches that. No requirement changed in substance. |

D-44 now rests on the argument OQ-6 itself identified, which was always the strong one: a rasterised PNG cannot be re-themed at all. **The behaviour does not change** — FR-87 still accepts any number of series entries, because capping the list would hard-error on a legitimate file for no benefit — but the free count is now stated as a consequence of the file being a list rather than a capability to reach for. **A-5 added:** quadrant counts are expected to stay at or below eight, with the taxonomy rather than the renderer being the thing to revisit beyond it; the tool still renders any N ≥ 1 and warns about none of it, per §4. Eight is where colours run out; the geometry is uncomfortable well before it, a sector narrowing from 120° at three quadrants to 45° at eight while labels crowd one circle under no collision rule (OQ-14). **A-5 now says three to six in practice**, seven or eight correct but busy, beyond eight a taxonomy question — and re-casts the palette's eight entries as **headroom**, so even the busy end gets distinct colours rather than eight being an endorsement. **FR-88 added:** a quadrant count above six emits one warning naming the count and renders anyway. Whether to warn at all was the harder call, since §4 rules out governance; resolved on what the warning is *about* — FR-88 reports that this tool will not render this well, which describes the **output**, where §4's prohibition is on judging the **input**. That distinction is now written into §4 so it is not re-argued. Two things keep it honest: it fires **once per render, not per row**, so it does not feed the diagnostic volume crumb `rg-m59` already flags; and its threshold is defensible, which is precisely why FR-33 still declines to warn about items-per-band — that limit moves with band radius and name length, so no honest number exists, and a warning nobody can justify is one everybody learns to ignore. **NFR-19 added:** README and `--help` both state the range, since documentation reaches the author deciding what to put in the CSV and FR-88 only reaches one who has already run it. SC-22 covers both. No requirement was re-scoped and no estimate figure moved. |
| 0.25 | 2026-08-29 | **D-47 records why ring bands are never sized by their content**, an idea raised in review and rejected on measurement. It is intuitive enough to resurface, so it is written down rather than left to be re-argued. **It does not work**, which is the reason worth keeping: marker capacity in a band comes from the arc length at FR-33's two radial tracks — from their *radii* — and a band can only widen by pushing its inner edge inward, where arc is scarcer. The effects nearly cancel, since capacity tracks `2·inner + 1.06w`, which for a ring widened from 65px to 131px is **198.9 either way**. Measured at eight quadrants, an adopt-heavy quadrant given a 131px Adopt band held four markers — the same four the 65px band held, against the twelve it needed. Capacity is **angular, not radial**: the same band holds about seventeen at three quadrants and seven at eight, so the remedy for an overfull ring is fewer quadrants or fewer items. Equal-area bands were measured as a data-independent alternative and are worse overall, `[2,7,7,8]` against `[2,4,9,13]`, trading the outer rings for the inner one. The second objection stands alone anyway: content-sized bands are data-dependent, so boundaries move between editions and a stationary item renders at a different radius — what D-7 refused for quadrant ordering on a dated, side-by-side-comparable artefact. FR-32 now states the rule and points at the decision. **An earlier pass at these numbers was wrong** — it gave every marker FR-40's status ring and added an invented gap, inflating the required separation from 20px to 28px — and was corrected against `mockup.html`, which also retired a phantom FR-33 defect the same analysis had produced (crumb `rg-cbx`). No requirement changed in substance. |
| 0.26 | 2026-08-29 | **What happens when a band overflows, answered.** FR-33 said only that legibility degrades, with no cap and no error, which left the behaviour to an implementer. **FR-89 warns**, naming the quadrant, ring, count and how many fit — and the threshold is *computed*, being the largest `n` for which FR-33's placement leaves no two markers closer than a marker diameter. That corrects D-46, which had asserted items-per-band could not be warned about because its limit "moves with band radius and name length": it varies with radius and sector angle, both known at layout time, and not with name length at all, since a marker carries a number rather than a name. **FR-33 now states the behaviour after the warning — markers overlap.** D-48 records why, against three alternatives. Dropping or capping items would let the graphic's comfort dictate the record, when FR-18 already reserves "not drawn" for rows that genuinely cannot be placed. Shrinking markers costs the number legibility FR-36 depends on. **Growing the canvas** was the serious candidate, and unlike D-47's ring resizing it does add angular room — capacity is linear in radius, 28 markers to 58 when the radar doubles — but at a fixed output size it is conservation rather than gain: the same doubling shrinks the marker in a 1920px PNG from 29px to 14px. It pays only when output pixels grow too, which `--width` already controls, and automatic growth would make the document's geometry data-dependent, redrawing every marker because one item was added — the defect D-47 and D-7 both reject. So the graphic overlaps and the existing fallbacks carry it: legend row, table row, tooltip, cross-highlight. NFR-19 documents `--width` as the lever. SC-23 covers the behaviour. **Adding radial tracks was raised and deliberately left unmeasured** — a first attempt spaced them below a marker diameter and made things worse, proving only that the attempt was wrong; `rg-cbx` requires geometry claims to be validated against `mockup.html` first, and this one has not been. |
| 0.27 | 2026-08-29 | **OQ-16 opened: should the marker be sized in output pixels rather than user units?** Raised in review against D-48, and it survives the argument that killed auto-growth. Today the marker is 10 user units in a 260-unit radar, so `--width` magnifies uniformly and a band is equally crowded at any output size — 28 items per quadrant at eight quadrants whether the PNG is 660px or 3840px. Holding the marker at a fixed output size makes capacity **linear in `--width`**: measured at 10 output px, 57 per quadrant at 1280, 88 at 1920, **182 at 3840**, with the dot the same physical size throughout. D-48's conservation objection does not reach it — that argument assumes the marker is fixed in *user* units, where size and count trade against each other, and fixing it in output units breaks the trade. D-48 now says so, since its wording could otherwise be read as closing a question it never addressed. **Recorded as an open question rather than specified**, because four things need closing first: SC-7 requires the wide arrangement identical across formats and a 3840px PNG would differ from the HTML, needing a carve-out scoped to equal output width; HTML has no `--width` and no generation-time output size, so it would keep today's ratio and widen that same gap; FR-89's overflow threshold would become width-dependent, so a band warns at 660 and not at 3840; and the marker must still hold its number, which at four digits forces a floor that grows with digit count. Recommended **yes** once those are settled. No requirement changed. |

At eight quadrants and 3840px the outer band holds 83 of that 182 while the **innermost holds 8**, a ratio near 1:10 that `--width` does not move because it is set by the radius of FR-33's tracks (about 25 and 44 units against 220 and 239). The innermost ring is the figure worth quoting, being both where the constraint binds and, under the default ring set, `Retire`: 2 items to 8 at four quadrants and 1920px, 2 to 3 at eight quadrants. That is a fourfold gain where it is needed, and it also bounds the idea — dropping from eight quadrants to four buys more innermost capacity at a fixed 1920px than doubling the width to 3840px does at eight, which is A-5's argument arriving from the other direction. | 0.29 | 2026-08-29 | **The innermost band's scarcity interacts with `--rings`, which nothing said.** Capacity follows the radius of FR-33's tracks, so the innermost band holds roughly a tenth of the outermost — and *which lifecycle stage lands there is the author's choice*, made by the centre-to-edge order of `--rings` (FR-12). The in-house default puts `Retire` at the centre, a transient state, leaving `Adopt` in the roomier band 2: about 10 items per quadrant at four quadrants. **The ThoughtWorks convention that FR-14 advertises inverts exactly that**, putting `Adopt` — the stage that accumulates on any mature radar — in the tightest band, where the same geometry holds about 2. Same tool, same data, a fivefold difference from the ring set alone, and a ThoughtWorks-set radar therefore meets FR-89's overflow warning far sooner. FR-14 now states the consequence where the option is offered, and NFR-19 puts the general form in the documentation: the sparsest stage belongs at the centre where the lifecycle allows a choice. D-4 notes that the default's advantage here is **luck, not design** — it was chosen on the semantics of a terminal state with an owner and an exit date, and nothing geometric entered the argument. **OQ-16 strengthens accordingly**: for the ThoughtWorks convention, raising the innermost band from 2 to 8 is closer to a prerequisite than an improvement. No behaviour changed — §4 keeps the tool from objecting to either order. |
| 0.30 | 2026-08-29 | **v0.29's ring-set claim narrowed — it was true of one meaning of `Adopt` and asserted of both.** v0.29 said the ThoughtWorks order puts "the stage that accumulates" in the tightest band, which assumes `Adopt` means *we have adopted this*. On ThoughtWorks' own radar it means *we think the industry should take this up* — a curated recommendation, deliberately short and re-chosen each edition, which the innermost band holds perfectly well. That is the **advice-versus-estate distinction §1 already draws**, and its geometric consequence had not been connected to it: an advice radar's innermost ring is a shortlist, an estate radar's is an inventory, and only the second outgrows the space. **So both conventions are sound for their native use.** The failure case is the **cross** — estate semantics carried into the ThoughtWorks ring order, an easy mistake because the ring names are the familiar ones — where `Adopt` grows monotonically at about 2 items per quadrant against band 2's 10. FR-14, D-4 and OQ-16 all corrected to say this rather than the blanket claim. D-4 also now notes the pleasing direction of the original argument: the default was chosen on semantics, and the geometry turned out to follow. No behaviour changed. |
| 0.31 | 2026-08-29 | **D-49 recommends a curated `Adopt`** — *what we recommend*, not *everything we run* — as documentation guidance rather than a rule, since §4 forbids the tool policing content. Three parts of the document already assume it: **A-2** puts item counts in the tens, which an exhaustive estate would exceed on its own; **§4** puts the authoritative catalogue outside this tool, making the radar a view that may legitimately be a subset; and **FR-14's geometry** rewards it, an inventory overflowing the innermost band at about 2 items per quadrant where a shortlist does not. It also makes the two ring orders behave alike, which was the motivation — v0.30 had left the ThoughtWorks order sound only for advice radars. **The cost is stated rather than hidden:** §5's engineer asks whether a technology is Adopt or Trial, and under curated semantics an absent item means "not featured this edition" rather than "not in use", so the radar answers *should I use this?* while the catalogue answers *do we run this?*. Consistent with §4, but a real narrowing of what absence can be read to mean — the persona row and NFR-19 both now say so. Nothing is validated or enforced; an author wanting an exhaustive radar gets one, with FR-89 warning as bands fill. |

| 0.34 | 2026-08-29 | **NFR-20: repeated warnings are summarised, one line per group.** D-50's recommended pattern — an undeclared `In Use` ring holding the estate — printed one FR-17 warning per row, so a forty-item inventory printed forty warnings on every render for a configuration the author chose deliberately. A group is a warning **class** plus the **value** that triggered it, so two different undeclared rings are named separately; a group of one prints the ordinary message, a group of several prints the count, the value and the first line. **A `--quiet` flag was considered and rejected** (D-51), twice over: it is not a new capability, since NFR-8 already routes warnings to stderr and `2>/dev/null` does the same; and it is the wrong shape, discarding the duplicate-name and bad-date warnings — accidents worth hearing — to silence a ring warning that is not an accident. Volume was the problem, not the warnings, and grouping fixes it for every class crumb `rg-m59` complains about rather than only this one. NFR-11 extended so a group sorts by its first occurrence, keeping the order total; SC-24 added; SC-10's golden files will assert the grouped form, a one-time change made before any exist. `rg-m59`'s remaining question — whether a blank `qualifier` should warn at all — stays open. |
| 0.35 | 2026-08-29 | **NFR-20 regrouped: one line per warning *class*, not per value.** v0.34 gave each distinct value its own line, so two undeclared rings meant two warnings. One line per class, enumerating the values it saw with their counts and first lines, is both more compact and more uniform — the same shape now serves blank qualifiers, unrecognised statuses, unparsable dates and duplicate names. **Naming the values is retained as the load-bearing part:** it is what separates a deliberate `In Use` from a typo'd `Adpot`, and a bare count would report that something went unplotted without revealing that one of them was a mistake, which is the only actionable half. Values are listed in order of first occurrence with **no cap**, since truncating would discard exactly the rare value most likely to be the error. NFR-11 extended to order the enumerated values as well as the classes; SC-24 now asserts that forty `In Use` rows and one `Adpot` produce a single line naming both. **D-51 also records the rejection of a targeted `--ignore`**, which aims better than `--quiet` but lost on three counts once grouping existed: it removes one line rather than forty; that line documents a lossy operation (FR-17 drops the rows and nothing else says so, which a build script would then hide from whoever inherits it); and it splits ring vocabulary across two flags. |

Any other undeclared ring still warns, and FR-17's warning names the flag only in a run not given it. **FR-17's wordings are unified for every run**, with or without the flag, so its output changes for unchanged input: `not in --rings` and `rows use a ring not in --rings` for the flag set, `pass --rings with every ring to draw (it replaces the defaults)` for the default set, and `--table all lists it` without `unplotted`. FR-91 gains an `unplotted_rings` key, with the overlap checked across sources. FR-90 covers the flag. D-5 and D-95 note that a named ring's rows leave no trace by default. A targeted ignore, rejected in D-51, is accepted in this form, on habituation. |

On a window between the two breakpoints a press switches the arrangement once, the button keeping focus, the page keeping its scrollbar's gutter and the button its place on screen. FR-105 still reads the legend-shown breakpoint. **Separately, D-95 is reworded** for the maintainer's ruling of 2026-10-18 (rg-fgf): a quadrant used only by off-radar rows keeps its empty sector on purpose, to show that the quadrant was considered and nothing in it is on the radar. |

| 0.102 | 2026-10-22 | **`-e/--edition` and `{edition}`, as built** (crumb `rg-u6u`, PR 19b; FR-60, FR-65, FR-91, FR-92, D-57, NFR-8, D-24, D-58, SC-27, NFR-9 and the §6.6 table and examples amended). **`-e today`**, at the maintainer's decision: the local date, in `TZ`'s time zone or the system's, read once and echoed with its zone as a warning so the render can be repeated with an explicit `-e`, UTC with a warning when no zone can be found, for scheduled jobs that should not need a date script; no other run reads the clock. **`input` becomes a key too**, at the maintainer's decision: the CSV path, resolved against the file and held to a regular file, overridden by a positional CSV, with neither a usage error, so `radargen -c radar.toml` alone renders (FR-60, FR-91, D-57). **`edition` becomes a key**, a string checked as `-e` is and overridden by it, at the maintainer's decision, so a scheduled job's file can say `edition = "today"`; the echo, the unused warning and the missing-edition error read the effective edition and name its source. **Rulings on gaps:** an invalid date is clap's usage error and the placeholder errors exit `1`; the placeholder errors are NFR-16 tier-one checks in FR-91's key order, a file value's recorded and dropped while the later checks run, a flag's stopping the run, every problem in one value reported; values are named with their text, a title or subtitle from the file cut at 40 characters and an output path as written; a non-UTF-8 `-o` is used as given unless `{edition}` would have to change it, which is an error. |
